* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
* Accredited Entities are required to submit a revised funding proposal if there is any change in the data provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected accordingly.
* The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. The prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
* The report should be typed in Arial, size 11.

Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements. ### A.20 Executing Entity information

Regional Executive Entity (EE) – Indian Ocean Commission, intergovernmental organization created in 1984 by the Victoria Agreement. Headquarters agreement signed on 30 June 2015.

### A.21 Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The Global Risk Report (2019) indicates that extreme weather events are amongst the risks with highest impact of the global climate risk landscape. In the SWIO region, these include tropical cyclones and other hazards jointly causing severe economic damages and loss of life than others disasters*. Within this region, floods induced by rainfalls from tropical cyclones and non-tropical cyclones are the most frequent catastrophic hazard, followed by droughts. From 1950 to June 2020, there were a total of 348 disasters in the SWIO region (Table 1). These include both natural hazards and hydrometeorological hazards, of which 68 were due to storms (including tropical and non-tropical cyclones), 14 floods are due to heavy rainfall, and 270 are droughts. The period from 1950-2020 is the most recent period for the population of the four countries for the 1950-June 2020 period – and the physical damages associated to climate-related disasters have increased over time (Figure 1). The number of disasters per country is presented in Table 1 below. The table also provides the number of hazardous events and related impacts per country*. These are expected to increase due to climate change, as well as population growth. In addition to these natural hazards, we have climate-related drivers of risks for small islands, including Comoros, Mauritius and Seychelles – during the 21st century include sea level rise, tropical and extratropical cyclones, increasing air and sea surface temperatures, and changes in precipitation patterns. These are expected to increase the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events and increasing temperature, which also have increasing impacts on livelihood, food security, economic development and infrastructure.

Table 1: Number of disasters and related impacts in the SWIO region per country, from 1950 to June 2020*

| Country | Number of Disasters | Affected Population (including people) | Damages (million USD) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Comoros | 12 | 538,304 (94%) | 48 |
| Mauritius | 7 | 6,000 (100%) | 10 |
| Mauritius | 21 | 1,050,439 (98%) | 803 |
| Seychelles | 1 | 5,447 (100%) | 11 |
| Total (4 countries) | 113 | 18,263,982 (97%) | 3,206 |

Despite a long tradition of coping with weather, climate, and hydrological variability, and extreme events, countries in the SWIO region continue to face serious challenges in managing the risk of disasters. The baseline status of the NMDRs is presented in Table 1 below, according to the GFCS categorization*. Noting that, in general, Comoros and Madagascar have low capacity not even being at the “Basic” level, and Mauritius and Seychelles being at the “Basic” level*, there is a need to shift from disaster risk management (DRM) towards climate change adaptation (CCA). The proposed project will support this paradigm shift in the target region where the four countries are facing serious challenges in responding to increasing social and economic demands, in particular as climate-change risks increase.

In particular, the proposed Hydromet project will raise the capacity of the NMRIs of Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles to a higher category (see Table below) in all GCFs components, with significant investments in three major components, namely "observations and monitoring", "climate services", and "user engagement".

Specifically, the project will support the production of high-quality (i.e. more accurate, increased lead time, location-specific) climate services and predictions for the region, including through the development of early warning systems (EWS), at the regional and national levels. This will be possible through both (1) the use of recent scientific and technological advances in climate science and modeling, as well as the application of new methodologies that represent products from advanced centers at very high resolution (e.g., as those generated within the framework of the Climate Prediction Center (CPC) Global Ensemble Forecast System (GEFS)), which are currently not fully represented such as artificial intelligence (in combination with the expanded surface observation network); and (2) jointly developing target CPCs for use with specific user groups.

Climate services will be developed in response to climate-induced risks and vulnerability by engaging decision-makers, climate-sensitive sectors and communities with essential information to prevent, mitigate and adapt to the impacts of extreme climates, extreme weather and climate change. For example, the latest IPCC report (SROCC 2019) indicates that the region is highly vulnerable to climate change, particularly in terms of increased frequency and intensity of extreme events such as droughts, heatwaves, floods and storms, and sea level rise. However, CP-CPS Africa are not well developed and the wider region "has the least developed weather, climate and hydrological services in the world". The Hydromet project will support the development of climate services that meet the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) observation standard(s). In particular, the Hydromet project will seek to develop a regional early warning system (EWS) for floods, droughts, heatwaves and storms. The SWIO region aligned with the Global Basic Observing Network (GBOON) requirements, which defines a minimal set of surface observations that are required in order to provide accurate climate services.

The Hydromet project will also have a strong component related to service delivery and engagement with users/stakeholders and end-users, including last mile communities, to ensure that users (including the DRM institutions)

B

work closely with the MfDiFs in the development, testing and validation of the multi-hazard impact based forecasts and early warning services (MH-IBF-EWS), so that the MH-IBF-EWS are available and accessible in a more meaningful and actionable manner. This would include the establishment of a web-based knowledge and decision support system where various color-coded hazard maps, vulnerability assessments, and other relevant information on how different hazards contribute to the WMO Global Multi-Hazard Alert System (GMAS) can undergo early actions at the regional, national and local levels.

The proposed Hydromet project is primarily focused on preparedness; hence designed to bolster climate resilience among communities by reinforcing NHRSs, through strengthening national and regional institutional frameworks, focusing on capacity building of key stakeholders, and supporting the development of a knowledge management system. The project will also support improving national and local capacities in implementing a people-centered MH-IBF-EWS through coordination and collaboration with relevant actors at all levels. This will be achieved by: (i) strengthening the institutional framework for implementation of the project, backed by IIRC(s) and its national counterparts (i.e. National Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies); (ii) building capacity in NHRSs to implement the MH-IBF-EWS; (iii) supporting the development of a knowledge management system at each country, which will then be expanded and rolled out nationally using scenarios, and through the dissemination of knowledge products and outreach materials with the assistance of NGOs, in order to reach the leaders of the communities.

The regional approach is key to underpin improvements in the production and dissemination of CP-CS, as the four target countries are vulnerable to similar climate hazards that face common problems, challenges and lessons learned. As shown in Table 2, seven proposed project components will address these gaps and build on the existing NHRSs of Comoros and Madagascar, and the NHRSs of Mauritius and Seychelles, by sharing expertise and tools to reach a sustainable solution for the region. The regional approach will also help reduce duplication of efforts, minimize implementation costs, together with a higher level of harmonization, integration, and complementarity within the region. Table 3 (see section 4) provides an overview of the seven proposed project components, along with some additional options or few examples that would provide increased benefits from taking a regional approach. The proposed interventions/technologies follow the WMO Guidelines on Emerging Data Issues (WMO-No. 1259).

Given the critical role played by the Indian Ocean Commission (IOC) in the region, and its unique position as the only intergovernmental organization with a mandate to promote sustainable development of the oceanic environment and knowledge sharing among these countries, with a view to reduce climate-related vulnerability. The IOC launched the design process of the proposed Hydromet project through various consultations with the beneficiary countries to define the priorities for the project. The Hydromet project will be implemented in close collaboration with the IOC, which will also provide technical and financial support.

Enhanced institutional capacities and regional cooperation to produce high-quality CP-CS, and will explore the public-private engagement: both national and regional improvements will be achieved through enhanced institutional capacity building of NHRSs, as well as through the establishment of Regional Climate Networks (RCNs) will be developed. A Regional Climate Centre Network (RCC-Network) strategy, alongside with an operating plan for the RCCs in the region, will be established based on the guidelines and principles defined by the IOC and its member states; whose operationalization will be done through building the capacity of the existing RCCs in Mauritius and Seychelles. The proposed Hydromet project will support the development and demonstration and tested throughout a period prior to the submission to WMO for official designation as part of the WMO Global Data-processing and Forecasting System (GDPS). At the same time, the RCC-Network strategy will also support the identification of potential climate change gains in the production of CP-CS in the SWIO region. At the same time, the RCC-Network strategy will also include business plans for the engagement of the relevant private sectors in the SWIO region (e.g., shipping, large-scale fisheries, insurance).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 6 OF 119

## High-quality climate-related data, improved multi-hazard impact-based forecasts and EWS, and climate risk assessments: the technical expertise of NIMHST staff on observations and monitoring, data management, ICT technology, and hydro-meteorological services will be used to support the development of a **hydro-meteorological service** process, including training, experience and exposure. Moreover, hydro-meteorological data collection and analysis will be strengthened in order to improve climate-related hazard prediction and risk assessment among the four target countries. This will be done through data integration and establishing a quality management system (QMS) for the hydro-meteorological services (hydro-meteorological service solutions). Quality Management Principles will be introduced following the WMO Guidelines on Quality Management in Climate Services, so that well-established standardized processes are in place. Training and capacity development activities will be conducted to ensure the sustainability of the project’s outputs.

The understanding of climate change impacts on development processes and key economic sectors for the four target countries is still limited. The project will support the development of a hydro-meteorological service system, given the significance of agriculture, fisheries and tourism sectors for the four target countries, training to provide specific expertise in hydro-meteorological services, and the development of a climate risk assessment framework. This will be done through (1) developing a hazard matrix that can be used as an input into impact-based forecasting and early warning systems/services capability236 will be built through shifting from deterministic to probabilistic techniques that highlight not only the direct impacts but also indirect impacts, such as disease transmission or crop failure. Extreme weather analysis will be done for establishing threshold values that will cause flooding and other climate-related hazards. By working with the DRRM authorities and the relevant sectors, the project will support the development of a hazard matrix that can be used to assess potential impacts from extreme weather- and climate-related events by making use of the risk matrix, and (2) developing a hydro-meteorological service system for the four target countries. This will be done through the production of hazard and vulnerability maps thus supporting climate change adaptation planning.

## Quality Management Systems (QMS & User Feedback)

### Capacity Building, Institutional Arrangements

- Enhanced accessibility and use of climate services for climate change adaptation, and improved capabilities implementing a people-centered MfH-EWS for disaster risk reduction.
- CP-ICS – including data visualization tools, decision support systems, and user feedback mechanisms – will be co-developed with the four target countries to ensure that climate change adaptation plans will be co-developed based on the enhanced expertise and high-quality data supplied by the GCF. The MfH-EWS for disaster risk reduction, which is a key component of these plans, will especially target key sectors underpinning the economy of the four target countries, namely agriculture, fisheries and tourism.
- These sectors are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts, but key to support livelihoods of the vast majority of people in these countries. The MfH-EWS for disaster risk reduction will be co-developed together to develop high-quality products to ensure they are packaged according to end-user needs and use the most efficient communication channels.
- A web-based knowledge and decision support system will be developed, which will include a “meteogram”/“vignette” type platform that would also contribute to the WMO Global Multi-hazard Alert System. Regional partners jointly developing and supporting early warning by the national meteorological and local DRM authorities. Regional and local capacities for implementing a proposed MfH-EWS will be improved through coordination and collaboration with PIROG (the Indian Ocean Regional Intervention Platform). PIROG is an intergovernmental organization that supports the development, testing, and validation of the MfH-EWS in pilot sites in each country, which will then be expanded to other countries. PIROG will also provide technical assistance for capacity building and outreach materials with the assistance of NGOs.
- In order to reach the leaders of the communities in the four countries, PIROG will also be able to leverage its relationship with the German Red Cross and the IFRC, for the implementation of the Forecast-based Action: Financing (FBAfAF) models in the SWIO region. In order to align the science-based developments supported by the proposed Hydromet project.

Noting that DRM institutions in the SWIO region have been benefiting from the support of the Indian Ocean Regional Intervention Platform (PIROG), the weakest link in EWS in the SWIO region lies in the ability to select, monitor, forecast.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2/2 | PAGE OF 119

and generate and disseminate meaningful and actionable warnings (see Annex 2 – FS, for further details on the SWOT analysis and case studies). Hence, the proposed Hybrid intervention designs have been designed to support cost-effective and locally-appropriate GCF projects and services in climate services and tools, as well as the uptake of relevant socioeconomic sectors) and end-users, including local communities. Through coordination and collaboration with key stakeholders, a set of sector-specific hybrid interventions will be developed and revised annually for each hazard at certain impact levels. Key sectors have been selected based on their vulnerability to climate change impacts and their potential to contribute to adaptation and mitigation in climate change. Engagement with the private sector (e.g., shipping, large scale fisheries, insurance, tourism, etc.) will be essential to ensure that the investments made under the project are sustainable over time and that they support sustainability of the investments beyond the lifetime of the project. Climate change impacts will mainstreamed into CP-CS not only to reduce impacts of climate-related risks but also to ensure enhanced climate change resilience of the sector and its users. The project will contribute to achieving the GCF’s Strategic Objective 1: “Promote sustained economic growth in key sectors.” Through sector target interventions, 67% of the population in the four countries will be directly or indirectly supported.

The executing entity (EE) will be the Indian Ocean Commission (IOC), which will manage the project in close collaboration with GCF, the accredited entity (AE). The IOC will support four National Project Coordinators (NPCs) based in the four countries. The NPCs will be responsible for implementing and monitoring the project activities. The role of NGOs will serve as partners in the development, testing, and validation of the MiH-BF-EWS, as well as in the dissemination of information to end-users. The NPC will also work with other stakeholders such as the private sector in four countries. The government of these four countries, as well as users and end-users of CP-CS such as sectoral ministries, private sector and local communities have been consulted during the process of preparing this proposal.

The project will be aligned with the GCF’s Strategic Objective 1: “Promote sustained economic growth in key sectors” and the World Hydrological Cycle Observing System (WHDYCOSS). Finally, the project is fully compliant with the Global Alliance for Hydromet (GAH) endorsed by the GCF during COP25, and thus aim to be one of the pioneer projects to be implemented under the GAH. The project will also contribute towards achieving the Sustainable Development Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (SF-DRR) and to support risk informed sustainable development.

The project will scale up current initiatives and national ongoing projects financed by climate facilities. Development partners, including GCF, are committed to supporting the implementation of this project. The project will ensure necessary steps to ensure coordination and leadership with development partners to integrate and build synergies with current and future initiatives and projects on hydromet services and disaster risk reduction at national and regional levels.

The cost of GCF Hydromet Project will amount to USD17,386,006 million comprised of USD 52,747,966 million applied from the GCF Trust Fund, USD 11,638,040 million from the World Bank’s Global Environment Facility (GEF) trust fund and USD 6,941 million from beneficiary countries contributions.

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Project title (max. 100 words, excluding pages) (2 pages)

### 1- Regional climate overview

The regional climate of the South-west Indian Ocean (SWIO) is influenced by the following atmospheric features: (a) the Intertropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ), responsible for the convergence of the Monsoon and trade winds (privileged zone for cyclone genesis, and convective precipitations), (b) sub-tropical Highs, responsible for the trade wind regime, and (c) the South-West Indian Ocean Anticyclone (SWIOA). The SWIO regional climate in terms of season is mainly driven by the Monsoon annual cycle. These features represent expansive bands of large-scale wind convergence and high rainfall, which strongly influence intra-annual and inter-annual variability of precipitation over the SWIO countries.

The SWIO region has experienced dramatic changes in weather in the SWIO countries, most obviously during El Niño and La Niña events, and their connection with the Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD).

### Figure 2 – SWIO atmospheric features

State of the climate

Vincent et al. (2011)⁹ produced an analysis of climate trends for the countries of the SWIO (i.e., the four target countries—Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles—plus La Réunion), including those targeted in this proposal. The results show that there are some consistent changes in means and extremes during these 48 years in the countries of the western Indian Ocean. These consist mainly of a significant increase in temperature (Figure 3) and a decrease in precipitation, and this is due to higher spatial and temporal variability in precipitation as compared to temperature. Table 3 presents regional trends for periods of 1961–2004 and 1975–2004 for each of the chosen indices.

#### Temperature

It is observed in the SWIO countries. Regional average series indicate significant trends of 0.19 and 0.21°C per decade (Table 3) over these two periods, with a gradual increase for both temperature indices during this period (Figure 4). At the majority of the stations, an increase of 0.15 to 0.25°C per decade is observed in the diurnal temperature range (Figure 4). The regional average series indicate a significant decrease of 0.037°C per decade for these 48 years (Table 3 and Figure 4).

No spatial coherent change is obtained in the diurnal temperature range (Figure 4); the regional average series indicates a significant decrease of 0.037°C per decade for these 48 years (Table 3 and Figure 4). The results for 1975–2006 are very similar for these three temperature indices.

The regional average series indicates that the warm days (TX10P) and warm nights (TN10N) have significantly increased at a rate of 3.53% per decade, respectively (Table 3 and Figure 6). Significant increasing trends ranging from 2 to 8% were also found at most stations (Figure 6). Observed changes in cold extremes are smaller than in warm extremes. The number of cold days has decreased by about 0.74% per decade while the number of cold nights has increased by 1.35% per decade, respectively. A significant decrease of 1.03% per decade is found in the percentage of cold nights (TN10P) (Table 3 and Figure 5). Maximum temperatures are increasing at a rate of about 0.24°C per decade while minimum temperatures are decreasing by 0.09°C per decade.

The analysis of the annual highest and lowest daily maximum and minimum temperatures confirm the previous results. The trends in TX10P, TN10N, TN10P, and TN5NP are significant at a rate of about 3% per decade for both maximum (TX) and minimum (TN) temperatures, respectively, while significant increases of 0.09 and 0.25°C per decade are observed in the diurnal temperature range (TTR).

The results for the precipitation indices show less evidence of changes and spatial agreement compared to the temperature series. The regional average series indicates a significant decrease in the annual total precipitation amount (PRCPTOT) of 2.65% per decade (Table 3 and Figure 7). Most stations show decreasing trends ranging from -10% to -40%, with some exceptions showing increasing trends, particularly in the western Indian Ocean and Seychelles. The annual precipitation intensity (SDI) shows very little change with an insignificant regional trend of +0.2% per decade. The number of days with rain greater than 5 mm (R5mm) has decreased by about -13% over the period, while the number of days with rain greater than or equal to 10 mm (R10mm), and R20mm have increased by +194 days per decade but the trend is not significant. The number of consecutive dry days (CDD) has increased by +1.94 days per decade but the trend is not significant either.

Projected changes in Tropical Cyclones over the SWIO

On average, the SWIO experiences nine tropical cyclone annually. Mutthup et al (2018) studied the potential changes in tropical cyclone tracks over the SWIO under different extents of global warming (1.5°C, 2°C and 3°C of warming). The results show that there is a significant increase in the frequency of landfalling tropical cyclones simulated on six model simulations (CORDEX4) under the RCP8.5 scenario (a low mitigation future), it is projected that the number of landfalling tropical cyclones will increase by 20% to 30%. The frequency of landfalling tropical cyclones is being a critical threshold, after which the rate of cyclical frequency with further temperature increases no longer has a distinct trend. In addition, the intensity of simulated tropical cyclones also shows an increasing trend in the SWIO was demonstrated (Figure 12) by larger and more intense negative anomalies associated with stronger global warming. However, there is some spread among their models in their projections for the frequency of landfalling tropical cyclone trend in simulated tropical cyclone-like vortices is consistent; there is some spread among their models in their projected changes in intensity. In addition to the increase in the frequency of tropical cyclones simulated, there is also a decrease ranging between 25% and 50% in landfalls simulated.

Figure 12 – Simulated change in the frequency of tropical cyclone-like vortices per 2° grid point across six downscaling’s under RCP8.5 global warming. The area where the changes are significant are indicated by crosses.

Climate variability in the Indian Ocean

As shown above, there is a correlation between the SST and temperature and precipitation. Predicting changes in the patterns of sea surface temperature (SST) fluctuations over the tropical oceans are critical for attributing changing climate variability and extreme weather. While considered a minor driver of climate variability as compared with greenhouse gas emissions, increasing concentrations of carbon dioxide have been linked to stronger SST variations. These long-term changes appear to be forced by increasing greenhouse gas (GHG) concentrations in the atmosphere.

DiNezio et al. (2020)** analyzed an ensemble of simulations of 21st-century climate performed by 36 models participating in the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project 5 (CMIP5). The simulations indicate that under greenhouse-gas-forced warming, the Indian Ocean will experience a pronounced departure from current variability in the eastern Equatorial Indian Ocean (Figure 13, A and B). This pattern of intensification resembles recent observations. In contrast, there is no significant change projected for the western basin or an equatorial region.

| --- |
| *CMIP5* - Coupled Model Intercomparison Project 5. The models present within CMIP exhibit a similarly altered mean state featuring stronger upwelling and an eastward shifting Hadley circulation.

DNeico et al (2020)**(i)** concludes that the emergence of the equatorial mode could drive rainfall variability with stronger amplitude and altered patterns over the Indian Ocean and surrounding land masses relative to currently existing climate models. The authors suggest that this mode, which is associated with a weakening of the Walker circulation, drives rainfall deficits over the western Indian Ocean, in addition to increased rainfall over the eastern Indian Ocean (EIO). This pattern is consistent with the observed EIO rainfall variability during the last glacial period when warm Indian Ocean Decadal (IOD) events are extremely weak and their rainfall impacts are restricted to the EIO. The authors suggest that this mode could be a key driver of the recent increase in EIO rainfall, which could drive rainfall anomalies with a similar spatial pattern and magnitude as the warm events, but with opposite precipitation signatures.

The emergence of the equatorial mode could make these high-amplitude SST anomalies a common occurrence by the second half of the 21st century when the modeled El Niño–Southern Oscillation (ENSO) events occur at cost per decade (range estimate from the subset of models with ENSO activation). Over the next few decades, this could predict and/or influence changing distributions of future rainfall in a warming climate must consider these changes in SST variability alongside thermodynamic effects.

2- Regional climate vulnerabilities and impacts

The countries of Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles face up collectively to USD 231-45 million losses due to natural hazards. A 100-year return period cyclone in Comoros could produce direct losses of USD 83 million, equivalent to 7% of the 2014 GDP, and require approximately USD 9.9 million in emergency costs. In Madagascar, a 100-year return period tropical cyclone could produce direct losses of USD 83 million, equivalent to 8% of the 2015 GDP, and require approximately USD 167 million in emergency costs. A 100-year return period tropical cyclone in Mauritius could produce direct losses of USD 43 million, equivalent to 75% of the 2014 GDP, and require approximately USD 9.9 million in emergency costs. Finally, in Seychelles, a 100-year return period tropical cyclone could produce direct losses of USD 83 million, equivalent to 1% of the 2015 GDP, and require approximately USD 4.3 million in emergency costs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (V2) PAGE 18 OF 119

## Comoros

Comoros is a 2,235 km² group of islands, including Anjouan, Grande Comore and Mohéli, with a total population of 872,000 in 2019*. Comoros is classified as a LDC by the World Bank** and a SIDS; approximately 40% of its GDP comes from tourism.

Comoros is very vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, due to an economy largely based on subsistence agriculture and fishing. The country has experienced significant droughts in recent years, which have severely impacted by climate change and related hazards. Agriculture—which engages about 55% of the population—is particularly vulnerable to climate change as it is highly dependent on rainfall. Sea-level rise will increase more frequent extreme events and sea level rise. The impacts of climate-related hazards, including water stress, soil salinization, and increased frequency of cyclones, are expected to be significant in Comoros.

Comoros has a high female literacy rate (65%), but women in Comoros are particularly vulnerable to climate change as 87% of them are employed in the agricultural sector. Women also play an important role in fishing activities and are responsible for collecting firewood, which is essential for cooking and heating. The impacts of climate-related hazards on women include loss of access to water resources, increased vulnerability to marine habitats, as well as the proliferation of toxic algae and the disappearance of nursery areas for marine wildlife.

The impacts of climate change will also be felt in other sectors including (including tropical cyclones), which threaten fishing trips and safety at sea, as well as boats and equipment.


## Madagascar

Madagascar is a 587,041 km² island with an estimated population of 27 million (as of 2019*). Madagascar is classified as being among the world's most vulnerable countries to climate change and one of the world’s poorest in terms of GDP per capita. The population is predominantly rural, and agriculture is the main economic activity in Madagascar.

Climate change is particularly harmful to two key sectors for the country: agriculture and water management; in fact, climate-related hazards have already caused significant damage to these sectors. For example, cyclones Idai and Fani in 2019 and 2015 respectively devastated more than 40,000 hectares of rice fields, destroyed two water reservoirs and carried away tonnes of sediment into the fields.


## Mauritius

The Republic of Mauritius is an island nation with an estimated population of 1.3 million in 2019*. Mauritius is considered an upper-middle-income country**. The country has developed a mixed economy based on manufactured exports, tourism, and services.

Climate change impacts on coastal development are a significant threat for the tourism industry, which accounts for 8% of GDP and employs about 5% of the population. Climate change will also have negative effects on agriculture, as this sector is seen as a driver for future economic development by the government. Most hotels and tourist infrastructure are located in low-lying coastal areas that are vulnerable to sea-level rise and more frequent extreme events such as cyclones. Sea-level rise and more frequent extreme events and heavy rainfall will accelerate coastal erosion and cause flooding of low-lying coastal areas, which could lead to loss of life and property damage. Climate change also impacts agriculture, which is the main economic activity in Mauritius. The impacts include changes in crop growing patterns and extreme events, like tropical cyclones. The resulting decrease in food-crop production could have serious impacts on nutrition and health in the country.

## B

### Seychelles

Seychelles is a 455 km², 115-island archipelago, with an estimated population of 96,000 as of 2019. In 2018, its per capita GDP was USD 16,730.

### Climate Change Adaptation Strategies

Seychelles' high-income status is particularly threatened by climate change, due to its small island status and economic reliance on tourism (which accounts for approximately 45% of the country's GDP) and fishing (which provides employment for over half of the population).

Over half of the climate hazards-related damages are caused to the residential sector, followed by the transport infrastructure. The majority of these damages are due to direct impacts such as flooding and damage to buildings and infrastructure in the residential and tourism sectors.

The most vulnerable areas are those located on low-lying land (50% of the country's total area) or close to coastal infrastructure, but also by indirect impacts, such as the deterioration of the natural environment - biodiversity and fisheries. The fishing sector is particularly affected due to changes in fish migration patterns and the decline of artisanal fisheries as the degradation of coral reef and mangrove habitats generates a reduction in fish stock.

The tourism sector is also vulnerable because it depends on the quality of its natural resources, which are severely impacted by extreme rainfall events in the periods 1997-1998 and 1999-2000, accounting for 45% of the total estimated losses.

### Regional and National Climate Change Adaptation Strategies

Governments across Africa have recognized that climate change is a reality and are committed to initiate and support climate-resilient interventions to reduce the vulnerability of their countries and populations to climate change impacts. This includes developing several policies, strategies, programmes and projects pertaining to climate change adaptation and climate services.

All four countries have adopted the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs); and Comoros and Madagascar have also developed National Adaptation Plans (NAPs). The NAP process is a key component of the national process of developing their National Adaptation Plan (NAPP). Other relevant domestic policies include Comoros' National Strategic Plan 2017-2025, Madagascar's National Climate Change Management Policy (NCMCP), 2012; Mozambique's National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) and National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP); and Malawi's National Climate Change Strategy (2009) and the Climate Change Policy (currently under development). See Annex 2 for details on existing policies.

The proposed Hydrometeorological Project will contribute to achieve the objectives of the SAMOA pathfinder, in particular goals (a) gaining access to technical assistance and financing for early warning systems; disaster risk reduction and post-disaster recovery; (b) strengthening national capacities for climate change adaptation through the development of preparedness and recovery education programmes and disaster risk management; (ii) promoting cooperation and information sharing among countries on climate change adaptation, including by developing a regional contingency planning and provisions for disaster preparedness and response; (iv) harmonizing national and regional reporting systems; and (vi) increasing participation in international climate change processes.

The project will contribute to the following objectives of the SAMOA pathfinder: (i) improving the technical capacity of NMHSs, as well as the production, dissemination and uptake of CP-CIs, including early warnings by users/stakeholders and end-users, including last-mile delivery; (ii) strengthening national capacities for climate change adaptation through the development of a risk management by establishing a WMO-designed RCC-Network and related protocols. Disaster preparedness and response will be strengthened at the national level in collaboration with the NMHSs and other relevant national counterparts, and directly involving the local organizations and communities (last mile partners) as the focal points for disaster risk reduction; the project will also contribute to the objectives of the largest countries in international and regional risk management initiatives, including inter alia: (i) the RCC-Network established by the WMO's Global Data-processing and Forecasting System (GDPS); and (ii) the web-based knowledge and decision support system with multi-hazard impact-based forecasts and color-coded risk-based warnings; regional

## Implementation of a Regional “One-Stop” Platform Contribution to the WMO Global Multi-hazard Alert System – GAB

### and (2) GBON compliance with the mandatory requirements.

The proposed Hydromet project will contribute to sustainable economic growth and development in the target region by improving climate-related hazard monitoring, forecasting, and harmonization of early warning systems. Based on the Mission Briefing Agenda 2035, and the expected impacts of climate-related hazards on economic sectors, especially agriculture, fisheries, and tourism, as well as human health, in the countries of the SWIO region (see Annexes A, B, C), it is expected that interventions will contribute to improving food security, e.g., through developing seasonal forecasts and agricultural advisories for farmers; generating hazard and risk alerts for fishermen; and ensuring an appropriate dissemination of climate-related information. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the GBON in each target country, and will also contribute to implement sectoral frameworks of the Agenda 2035, in particular the Climate Change Adaptation Framework.

Because of the regional nature of climate extremes (such as tropical cyclones), of their shared vulnerability to climate change and climate-related hazards (such as floods and landslides), and of their recognition for the need to strengthen early warning systems, the countries of the SWIO region, including Mauritius, Seychelles, Comoros, and the Maldives, have adopted the IOC strategy: « Document cadre pour la stratégie d'adaptation au changement climatique dans les régions vulnérables » (2013). The implementation of this strategy, coordinated by IOC, provides the guidance to foster climate change adaptation in the SWIO region. In particular, it recommends enhancing regional coordination, hydrometeorological forecasting and risk assessment, and knowledge exchange among countries within the region. This is achieved through the establishment of a Regional Warning Centre (SWOWCFC), and in disseminating seasonal outlooks through a Regional Climate Portal. Hence, the IOC is the ultimate responsible for the implementation of this strategy.

The Hydromet project will contribute to the following objectives: (1) strengthening climate change adaptation and harmonization of efforts for climate-related hazard monitoring, forecasting, and service delivery; and (2) in particular, by contributing to the development, testing, deployment, and evaluation of early warning systems and mitigation strategies in the SWIO region.

### 4. Scaling up on past and ongoing initiatives and projects

The regional Hydromet project will build on, scale up, and complement past and ongoing regional and national initiatives and projects. To ensure complementarity and identify synergies with existing and planned projects, several meetings were held between the GCF Secretariat, the WMO, and the countries of the region (including Mauritius and EU), which are strongly involved in the region, specifically in developing hydromet projects. The most relevant meeting was held on 20 June 2017 at the WMO headquarters in Geneva, Switzerland, during which the proposed Hydromet project item was discussed; however, the full text of the discussion is included in Annex 2 (section 2.2.3). In addition, the proposed Hydromet project will build on and complement existing initiatives and projects that are already being implemented by the small islands, including the inventory of operational solutions for the detailed design and technical specifications of the investments supported by the proposed project.

## CAMEROON
* **"Empowering climate resilient smallholder farmers in the Common Market of Central Africa (CMAC)"**
    * **Project Period:** 2018-2026
    * **Implementing Agency:** UNDP
    * **Budget:** USD 60.8m

Contributions to improving water resource management through installation of 10 hydrological (surface water) and meteorological (rainfall) monitoring stations on Grand Comore and across Anjouan and Mohéli islands, with the existing synoptic sites (i), automatic weather stations (ii), and rainfall gauges (iii). Data will be captured, processed, and transmitted to a central server. A flood alert early warning system using total rainfall and rainfall intensity thresholds will be developed to provide a flood risk warning system for each island. The project also aims to provide SMS flood warning messages.

## MADAGASCAR
* **"Adaptation des agriculteurs aux changements climatiques en agriculture de subsistance et en agriculture d'exportation"**
    * **Project Period:** 2017-2022
    * **Funding Entity:** GIZ

Using the climate information generated, farmers will benefit from better access to agrometeorological and agricultural advisory services. In addition to improving adaptation to climate change in agriculture, the project also aims to improve food security through the development of a quality agriculture policy, the organization of training sessions for extension officers, and ensuring that policies are adapted. Finally, the project supports the establishment of a national early warning system (NIEWS) under the PRACHI project which aims at transmitting climate information from the regional level to the local level using mobile phones.

## MAURITIUS
* **"Land Drainage Master Plan"**
    * **Planned to start late 2018**
    * **Funding Entity:** ADB
    * **Budget:** EUR 1.5m

The project will complement the existing drainage infrastructure and hydraulic designs on the island, focusing on areas of high risk for flooding. It includes technical and socio-economic studies, development scenarios and recommendations for optimal design changes to reduce flood risks.

## SEYCHELLES
* **"SeyCat" (Seychelles Climate Adaptation Trust Fund)**
    * **Project Period:** 2017-2023
    * **Funding Entity:** Global Environment Facility (GEF)
    * **Budget:** USD 5.4m

Invests in ocean stakeholders to generate new learning, bold action and innovative solutions for climate change adaptation. Going projects include Blue Grants Fund I, which empowers the private sector with robust science, evidence-based decision-making tools, and access to finance; and Blue Nature Conservancy II, which supports governance, sustainability, value and market options.

* **The accredited entity for the proposed project is also taking part in the Africa Hydromet program** and is in continuous liaison with World Bank team and other development partners. The equipment installed for water monitoring will be linked to hydrological stations through the existing network (2.1.1 training provided, and 2.1.3 data collection). The project will be complemented by the implementation of a flood risk management system (2.1.2) with impacts in terms of flood risk management and resilience

The agriculture-related data and climate services generated from this project will be complemented and disseminated to other sectors, for other crop varieties and livestock species. This will increase outreach through the existing network of the project. Note that agriculture is a key sector for Madagascar.

Flood hazard maps produced by the project will be complemented by the implementation of a flood risk management system which will build on data and information generated from this initiative.

Information and climate services generated by the project will be disseminated to support these efforts to strengthen the private sector with robust science, evidence-based decision-making tools, and access to finance. The private sector indeed acted as a key actor in the development of local CS.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 22 OF 119

## Observations and Monitoring

It is recognized that to improve delivery of national CP-5S, it is critical to pursue regional hydromet strategies (as the once adopted by IOC – see also Subsection B.1., item 2), as well as effective regional cooperation, collaboration with other organizations, and development of new technologies. To achieve this goal, adaptation and mitigation measures will be developed and implemented. Integration of the region’s hydromet systems provides opportunities for improved service delivery to the region’s users. The implementation of a regional hydrological monitoring networks needs to be shared with other NMHSs through regional data exchange in order to improve monitoring of hazardous hydrometeorological conditions, numerical modeling, and forecasting. The optimum regional cooperation would be achieved by developing a common strategy that ensures the provision of high-quality service delivery that meet the existing and future needs of societies due to climate change—would ensure robust implementation of the proposed project.

The livelihoods and economies of the SWIO region depend heavily on the ocean and climate-sensitive sectors such as agriculture, fisheries, and tourism. Changes in temperature and precipitation12 are already having devastating effects on the availability of fresh water and agricultural yields. Fisheries are under threat, as well as the food security of millions of people who rely on them for their livelihoods. The projected increase in sea level rise in the SWIO13, which coincides with a poleward shift in the position of the 7°C mean annual SST isotherm—the threshold temperature above which coral reefs are at risk—will have significant impacts on coastal communities and their livelihoods, assets and infrastructure are threatened by various climate-related hazards. Current forecasts issued by the National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS) for the SWIO region do not provide decision support information to help people adapt to these risks. The lack of a coordinated approach in disaster risk reduction (DRM) and socio-economic sectors (such as agriculture, fisheries and tourism). Essential services provided by NMHSs are often fragmented across the region and there is no single entity that coordinates DRM activities across the region. Their weather, climate, water and ocean information services are critical to the safety and livelihoods of SWIO people. However, these services are not yet fully integrated into disaster risk reduction (DRM) and socio-economic sectors. These services are crucial to enhancing resilience and reducing vulnerability of SWIO people to climate-related hazards and the impacts of climate change. The proposed Hydromet project will address constraints including gaps in geographical coverage (e.g., oceans, coastal areas), limited capacity for data collection and processing, lack of end-to-end climate and ocean information services, from data collection, monitoring, modelling, forecasting, to service delivery and response, and the need for a coordinated approach across DRM and socio-economic sectors. The case studies such as in Madagascar which is a large country, and despite small islands in the other three countries in the region, have shown that climate change adaptation strategies are more effective when they are integrated into existing services. These services are crucial to enhancing resilience and reducing vulnerability of SWIO people to climate-related hazards and the impacts of climate change. The proposed Hydromet project will address constraints including gaps in geographical coverage (e.g., oceans, coastal areas), limited capacity for data collection and processing, lack of end-to-end climate and ocean information services, from data collection, monitoring, modelling, forecasting, to service delivery and response, and the need for a coordinated approach across DRM and socio-economic sectors. The case studies such as in Madagascar which is a large country, and despite small islands in the other three countries in the region, have shown that climate change adaptation strategies are more effective when they are integrated into existing services.

The Hydromet project will address constraints including gaps in geographical coverage (e.g., oceans, coastal areas), limited capacity for data collection and processing, lack of end-to-end climate and ocean information services, from data collection, monitoring, modelling, forecasting, to service delivery and response, and the need for a coordinated approach across DRM and socio-economic sectors. The case studies such as in Madagascar which is a large country, and despite small islands in the other three countries in the region, have shown that climate change adaptation strategies are more effective when they are integrated into existing services.

A key challenge to improving weather, climate, water and ocean information services in the four target countries (outlined above) limits the ability of these countries to provide reliable, timely, relevant and early warning on their local weather, climate and ocean conditions. This is because many of them have not yet been adapted for longer lead times or for longer range change impacts. This restricts their capacity to safeguard vulnerable populations and assets against multiple and compounding climate-related hazards.

As a result, the NMHSs in the SWIO region are unable to provide reliable, timely, relevant and early warning on local weather, climate and ocean conditions. This is because many of them have not yet been adapted for longer lead times or for longer range change impacts. This restricts their capacity to safeguard vulnerable populations and assets against multiple and compounding climate-related hazards.

A key challenge to improving weather, climate, water and ocean information services in the four target countries (outlined above) limits the ability of these countries to provide reliable, timely, relevant and early warning on their local weather, climate and ocean conditions. This is because many of them have not yet been adapted for longer lead times or for longer range change impacts. This restricts their capacity to safeguard vulnerable populations and assets against multiple and compounding climate-related hazards.

The preferred solution is to implement integrated climate and ocean information services, multi-hazard impact-based forecasting systems (EWS), and ensure that the SWIO region will be able to address the major barriers in coherent systems. While ongoing projects have often focused on improving NMHSs’ capacity within a country or region, with limited area of intervention within a country, the Hydromet project proposes such a regional integrated approach, which is more effective.

This will address the common vulnerabilities of the target four countries: populations, their livelihoods and assets to climate-related hazards. The SWIO region has been identified as one of the most vulnerable regions in Africa; these four countries have indicated interest in adopting demand-driven solutions that establish sustainable climate-proof systems. This project will provide a coordinated approach for improving weather, climate, water and ocean information services across the four countries through a harmonized approach. If NMHSs in the SWIO region are equipped with improved hydrometeorological infrastructure and tools (see the examples in Table 1), they will work closely with socio-economic sectors, DRM and communities; these four target countries in the SWIO region will become more resilient to climate change, because they will be able

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND

To produce and make use of more accurate, timely and reliable CP-CS including Mf-IBF-EWS, target to the users (which are the socio-economic sectors and communities). This will be achieved through:

* **Strengthened institutional capacities and regional cooperation through improved governance mechanisms;**
* **Strengthened capacities and capabilities for detection and monitoring climate-related hazards;**
* **Improved quality of forecasts and warnings, which in turn will be integrated in the decision-making processes;**
* **Enhanced preparedness and response capability with improved gender and vulnerable people inclusion across the EWS value chain.**

The proposed Hydromet project will ensure that:

* **(a) governments in the five target countries implement policies, plans, and early actions based on more accurate, timely and reliable CP-CS and Mf-IBF-EWS;**
* **undergo investments in infrastructure and tools (as those described in table 1 above);**
* **(b) integrated CP-CS including Mf-IBF-, EWS are used to inform decision-making processes at all levels of government, from national to local, based on demand, in recognition of the accepted-and practical value; and**
* **(c) communities and socio-economic sectors adopt new practices by using improved CP-CS, risk knowledge and Mf-IBF-EWS, through co-production of CS-CP, and learning and adaptation as part of the process of Mf-IBF-EWS.**

The IOC, as the executing entity for the Hydromet project, is the unique institution in the SWIO region in position (i) to implement a comprehensive approach to climate change adaptation and risk mitigation strategies in the SWIO region through: (a) improved monitoring, modeling, forecasting, and service delivery; and (ii) to support the development and implementation of climate change adaptation and risk mitigation strategies in the SWIO region. This will result in cost savings and prevention of loss of life and property, as well as increased resilience of communities and socio-economic sectors against climate change impacts. The Hydromet project will also contribute towards improving the accuracy of forecasts, as well as underpin successful, cost-efficient climate risk mitigation and climate change adaptation strategies in the SWIO region by targeting various socio-economic sectors from agriculture to fisheries, tourism, and urban development.

By adopting a regional approach to risk reduction and adaptation, by targeting various sectors—from agriculture to fisheries, tourism, and urban development—the Hydromet project will provide an innovative regional approach to improve climate resilience in the SWIO region; the success of a regional approach to monitor and manage climate variability and change, thereby improving the region’s socio-economic development, which is critical for the long-term sustainability of communities and socio-economic sectors across the SWIO region.

In this context, the proposed Hydromet project investments will focus on harmonization of approaches and joint efforts for the generation of science-based multi-hazard weather and climate risk information by adopting multi-hazard impact-based forecasting and early warning services/MHs (Mf-IBF-EWS). This will include shifting from deterministic to probabilistic forecasts, which are more accurate than traditional deterministic forecasts and provide worst-case scenarios, which are often the cause of avoidable disasters. Extreme value analysis will be done for extreme weather events, such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, and tropical cyclones.

In terms of implementation, the NMHSs will work with the DRRM authorities and the various sectors; (i) to undertake risk analysis incorporating climate change impacts, and assess potential impacts on socio-economic sectors and related events by making use of the risk management tools developed in section 2.3; (ii) Training of NMHSs and stakeholders is critical for successful uptake, and will be an important component of the proposed Hydromet project. This will include training on climate change adaptation planning, including downscaling and calibration, thus more accurate hydro-meteorological forecasts and warnings, as well as climate change risk assessments and vulnerability studies through the production of hazard and vulnerability maps that also support climate change adaptation planning.

# Annex B - FSWI SWIO analysis and case studies demonstrate that DBM violations in the SWIO region have been supported by the Indian Ocean Regional Intervention Platform (IRIO) in coordination with IRIO, and therefore, identified priority investments in support climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction include activities that will be implemented through the proposed Hydromet project. These investments are expected to address gaps that require strong collaboration and partnership between NMHSs and DRRM institutions. Hence, in addition to the support for the development of a regional early warning system (see Annex 2C), the proposed Hydromet project will support the development and implementation of a web-based knowledge management platform for sharing information on climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction, including early warnings (regional “meteo-earlywarning”/“vigilance”-type platforms that would also contribute to the WMO Global Multi-Hazard Information System). The Hydromet project will support the development of a regional database of national and local levels. Regional, national and local capacities in implementing a people-centered MH-EWS will be improved through coordination and collaboration with IRIO (the Indian Ocean Regional Intervention Platform) on the development of a regional early warning system for climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction. The project will also support the testing, and validation of the MH-EWS in pilot sites in each country; which will then be expanded and rolled out nationally. This will be done through capacity building activities at national level, including training workshops, and assistance of NGOs, in order to reach community leaders in the four countries. Collaboration and collaboration with IRIO will also be strengthened by supporting the development of a regional database of national and local levels for the implementation of the Forecast-based Action / Financing (FSA/F) models in the SWIO region, in order to align the science-based developments proposed by the Hydromet project. In addition, the proposed Hydromet project will support the development of a regional early warning system for climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction, including early warnings (regional “meteo-earlywarning”/“vigilance”-type platforms that would also contribute to the WMO Global Multi-Hazard Information System). The Hydromet project will support the development of a regional database of national and local levels. Regional, national and local capacities in implementing a people-centered MH-EWS will be improved through coordination and collaboration with IRIO (the Indian Ocean Regional Intervention Platform) on the development of a regional early warning system for climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction. The project will also support the testing, and validation of the MH-EWS in pilot sites in each country; which will then be expanded and rolled out nationally. This will be done through capacity building activities at national level, including training workshops, and assistance of NGOs, in order to reach community leaders in the four countries. Collaboration and collaboration with IRIO will also be strengthened by supporting the development of a regional database of national and local levels for the implementation of the Forecast-based Action / Financing (FSA/F) models in the SWIO region, in order to align the science-based developments proposed by the Hydromet project.

Private sector in these sectors (e.g., shipping, large scale fisheries, aviation, tourism, etc.) will also be supported through capacity building and awareness raising activities. The Hydromet project will support the development of a regional database of national and local levels for the implementation of the Forecast-based Action / Financing (FSA/F) models in the SWIO region, in order to align the science-based developments proposed by the Hydromet project.

To overcome the barriers to progress towards climate resilient societies and climate friendly climate services, interventions with the most transformative impact on reducing disaster risk and will foster adaptation to climate change, have been identified as key areas of focus for the Hydromet project. These include: (i) strengthening NMHSs (see Section B.3) will support the strengthening of NMHSs to produce target-based forecasts/products (BFPs), and the timely dissemination of accurate and high-quality CPFs, including multi-hazard early warnings (MHEW). The activities will also contribute to the development of a regional database of national and local levels for the implementation of the Hydromet project. In addition, the Hydromet project will support the development of a regional database of national and local levels for the implementation of the Forecast-based Action / Financing (FSA/F) models in the SWIO region (supporting several projects such as BRIO and Indian Ocean Data Rescue Project(T)) makes the commission the lead agency to coordinate the SWIO countries within the proposed Hydromet project, and coordinate its activities to ensure harmonization of efforts in climate-related hazard monitoring, modeling, forecasting, and

service delivery as described in Section B.1., Item 4. Above for further details on the regional approach and IOC’s role. The Hydromet project is built around three components to be implemented at both regional and national levels.

Component 1: Capacity building, institutional development, regional cooperation and public-private engagement

Component 2: Improved high-quality climate-related data, improved multi-hazard impact-based forecasts and EWS (MR-IBF-EWS), and climate services

Component 3: Enhanced accessibility and use of climate services for climate change adaptation, and improved capabilities in implementing a people-centred approach for disaster risk reduction

DRM initiatives in the four countries and there is no regional hydrometeorological counterpart (except for the link with the Regional Specialized Meteorological Centre for Tropical Cyclone Forecasting in La Réunion to work with Météo-France on MR-IFS-EWS. As a result, there is limited capacity to provide a range of climate-related services including: improving the implementation of established MH-BF-EWS at national and regional levels; to reduce risk exposure and vulnerability among communities and economic sectors; this is further exacerbated by the lack of institutional capacity in the four countries to implement DRM initiatives, including adaptation and mitigation strategies at the national level. Throughout the implementation of the proposed project, it will be important to ensure that there are strong linkages between the activities supported under the RCC-Network (to be established under the project) and PRIDI. This is particularly important for establishing a regional early warning system (EWS) based on the Regional Hydrological EWS (RHEWS) in the four countries. While noting that the implementation of the Forecast-based Action / Financing (FPA/F) models in the SWIO region is not directly supported by the proposed Hydromet project, coordination and collaboration will be in place to ensure that the RHEWS and FPA/F models are complementary and mutually supportive with respect to these aspects, which are supported by the German Red Cross and the IFRC, under other initiatives and projects.

Finally, there are limited interactions between the producers and users of weather and climate services, and therefore limited capacity for the four countries to effectively use weather and climate information in decision-making on climate services. The financial resources to overcome these barriers are also not sufficient or not available within the scope of the proposed Hydromet project. In addition, there is a lack of institutional capacity at national level highly dependent on climate-sensitive sectors. In this context, the proposed Hydromet project will support market and policy reforms in the four countries by strengthening the institutional framework for establishing or strengthening existing national funds for climate information and early warning services (CIES).

### 3 - Project activities to overcome barriers and catalyse transformative change

The three components are designed to achieve the paradigm shift of improved monitoring, and management of climate-related socio-economic risks in the four countries. The project will also aim to facilitate growth in key climate sensitive target sectors (agriculture, fisheries and tourism), and enable resilience and adaptation to climate change impacts.

This project provides an opportunity for countries to leverage each other's strengths, deploy complementary fields of expertise and risk management capacity, and capitalize on successful regional and national initiatives. The regional approach will be based on the following three components:

#### Component 1: Strengthening institutional capacity

The objective is to strengthen institutional capacity in the four countries to manage climate-related socio-economic growth in the long-term. This is the only regional institution created and financed by the GCF, and it has a mandate to support the development of national meteorological services (NMS) for climate risk management (see Section 8.1). Through its regional status, ICA will champion increased collaboration among the target countries for climate-related hazard monitoring, modeling, forecasting and service delivery. Increased regional coordination will be achieved through the establishment of a RCC-Network, which will provide a platform to harmonize and coordinate activities across the four countries. The RCC-Network will also serve as a focal point for the development of national meteorological services (NMS) composites for each of the sub-systems—hydrological observational networks, modeling, forecasting, and service delivery platforms. This will be achieved through the establishment of an RHEWS in the four countries. The RCC-Network; strengthening regional training and calibration facilities, and use of a regional communication platform to facilitate information exchange (Component 1) and acquisition of specialised equipment and expertise (Component 2).

Through Component 1: regional cooperation will be strengthened through establishing an RCC-Network, which will provide a focal point for the development of national meteorological services (NMS) composites in the four countries. The RHEWS will be prepared to enable the delivery and development of climate services at the regional and national levels. Institutional strengthening, including the development of nationally-appropriate business models/plans for National Meteorological Services - will also be implemented in the four target countries to support robust NMS

## B

climate information for decision making: i) strengthen adaptive capacity and reduce exposure to climate-related risks; ii) and strengthen awareness of climate threats and risk reduction processes within the SWIO region.

As a result, these components will support the paradigm shift towards resilient institutions, communities, ecosystems and economies in order to enhance their ability to manage climate-related risks and promote sustainable development. Please refer to Annex 2 for details on the project components and their alignment with the GFCS led by WMO.

### B.1 Project/programme description (max. 2000 words; approximately 4 pages)

#### 1. Project development objective and contribution to climate change adaptation

The project objective is to strengthen adaptive capacity and climate resilience of the vulnerable communities and economies such as Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles. This will be achieved through (1) millimeter-scale and high-resolution weather forecasting by upgrading existing meteorological services; (2) improvement and modernization of hydro-meteorological systems and services, which will equip users with timely, reliable and accurate information on climate-related risks; (3) strengthening the capacity of communities to contribute to climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction®. Indeed, these services enable risk prevention and management by providing early warning for extreme weather events such as cyclones, floods, droughts, landslides, forest fires, hurricanes, and other natural disasters.

The project will support the implementation of NDCs, national adaptation and disaster risk reduction plans, and the achievement of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

#### 2. Project components

The proposed interventions will contribute to achieve the GFCS fund-level impact A10 Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions and A20 Increased resilience of health and social services, water resources, agriculture, fisheries, forestry, tourism, and other sectors.

The project components are as follows:

- Component 1: Strengthening climate information systems for decision making
- Component 2: Improving climate risk management capacity in the four countries
- Component 3: Enhancing climate resilience of communities and economies

#### 3. Project location

The four case studies and SWOT analysis of NMHS and DRM institutions, presented as part of the project Feasibility Study (FS), indicate that there are significant gaps between the current state of meteorological services in Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles.

- In Comoros and Madagascar, a key weakness is the limited number of functional weather stations for monitoring climate variables. This results in a lack of reliable data on climate variability and trends as well as technical capacities within the NMHS.
- In Mauritius, there are significant gaps between the current state of meteorological services and the required level of service delivery to meet the needs of users. The NMHS has limited capacity for providing adequate staffing capacity with relevant specializations to produce more diverse and/or localized CP-C3 that respond to user needs in a timely manner.
- In Seychelles, there are significant gaps between the current state of meteorological services and the required level of service delivery to meet the needs of users. The NMHS has limited capacity for providing adequate staffing capacity with relevant specializations to produce more diverse and/or localized CP-C3 that respond to user needs in a timely manner.

#### 4. Countries rely on deterministic numerical weather prediction (NWP) products, publicly available on the Internet or provided by regional centers. These products are based on initial conditions of the atmosphere at a given state or condition. Given the uncertainty in the initial state of the atmosphere, which cannot be perfectly observed and modeled, these NWP products can only provide reliable results for short-term forecasts (e.g., 2-3 days). Beyond this period, the forecast becomes increasingly imprecise—meaning that small changes in the initial state of the atmosphere and the processes in the model can lead to large differences in the final forecast. This is known as the "butterfly effect." As a result, these deterministic forecasts produce a single outcome which inevitably leads to significant mistakes when applied for decision making.

The countries have recognized this problem and are increasingly relying on ensemble prediction systems (EPS) and probabilistic forecasts.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WIND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 31 OF 119

The proposed project will support the access and use of high-resolution EPS and probabilistic forecasts with full data assimilation, which are generated from reliable sources, such as those working within the framework of the World Weather Information Service (WWIS) and the Global Agrometeorological Advisory System (GAMS). The model outputs will be shared to the SWIO region and countries, using innovative techniques such as Artificial Intelligence and by piloting a new approach for the production of high-resolution EPS. This will contribute significantly to improvements in global NWP/IFS through the GBCN commitment to share internationally these data through the World Weather Information Service (WWIS).

Dissemination and communication and engagement with the users of CP-5 CSs produce timely, high-quality services is also limited in the four target countries. Overall, there is a lack of procedures in place to co-produce climate information products with end-users.

* There is no operational service dedicated to agriculture which relates to precipitation forecasting, temperature forecasts, and other weather-related hazards such as floods, droughts, and heatwaves, which are affecting farming communities in the event of strong winds or swells.
* In Mauritius, there is no river hydrology monitoring system in place. While a flood is a predominant risk, though calendar for the rainy season for each type of culture and for each region are made available to farmers, their accuracy is not high enough to be used as a decision-making tool by farmers.
* In Mauritius, Emergency Operations Procedures (EOPs) are in place for all major hazards like tropical cyclones, but there is no specific EOP for floods. The same applies to the other target countries except Seychelles where they have one.
* According to reports to fisheries, Seychelles’ NMHS delivers marine forecasts for fishermen onshore; however, they do not have relevant equipment used to warn fishermen on sea. Finally, no river hydrology monitoring is available for settling rainfall and runoff data in the Seychelles.

None of the target countries have the capacity for HIF-BF-EDVS and therefore, the proposed project has a high potential to create new demand for CP-5 CSs.

The four countries are committed to address these shortcomings by improving the production and delivery of timely, accurate CP-5 CS; this can be achieved through their engagement within the Sendai Agreement as well as other strategies and plans like the NAP and NOC. Moreover, consultations conducted during the project development phase in Mauritius, Seychelles, Comoros, and Madagascar have shown that these countries are committed to the NAP (see Annex 1) and in Anne 07, are in need of better CP-5 CSs including timely alerts for cyclones, floods, and storms, accurate daily temperature forecasts, and other weather-related hazards. The project will also contribute to create new demand for CP-5 CSs based on further consultations and engagement with stakeholders and end-users.

Several GCF outcomes for adaptation will also be achieved through the proposed project; namely:

* A0 5 Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-response planning and development
* A0 3 Increased generation and use of climate information in decision-making
* A0 2 Improved access to climate services
* A0 0 Strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk reduction processes

## Scope of the project and description

The proposed project will seek to scale up current initiatives supporting climate services in Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles. This will be achieved by building on investments to:

1. Improve weather and climate monitoring technologies and downscale climate change projections, while continuously improving the quality of climate information products.
2. Enhance the production and dissemination of climate services by scaling-up existing initiatives in the production of agrometeorological advisories and early warnings for tropical cyclones, develop new services for the four countries, including but not limited to: timely, reliable and accurate HIF-BF-EDVS; and produce or update risk management and adaptation plans.
