B

## ii) Foster collaboration and knowledge exchanging for hazard monitoring and risk management through sustaining and strengthening of the SWOCCP and the UUP.

### iv) Improve capabilities in implementing a people-centered M-HIF- EWS through collaboration between NMHSs and other relevant stakeholders.

All supported by regional collaboration and national institutional development, including capacity building. The proposed interventions have been developed with the end-users of CPICs in mind to provide improved CPICs addressing the needs of the most vulnerable communities and ensuring that the information is relevant for the hazards associated with climate-related hazards, and to ensure that information is packaged to inform decision-making processes at the local level, using the best communication channels for such specific groups of users.

The proposed interventions will be implemented through a combination of public and private engagement. This Consortium will set up the basis for a regional approach and collaboration for weather and climate monitoring and prediction. It will support the adoption of a Regional Climate Services Framework (RCSF) that will contribute to developing a common risk awareness among IOC member states that reinforces weather data and information sharing in the region, including through the development of an operational framework for the early warning system for storms with strong winds and heavy rainfall. The establishment of an RCC Network under the aegis of IOC and as a key component of the RCSF will be based on four pillars: (a) the provision of climate services and related auxiliary functions; (b) operational activities for long-range forecasting (LRF); (c) operational activities for climate monitoring; (d) operational climate data services; and (e) training in the use of operational RCC products and services. Additional functions will be provided by the RCC Network, including: (a) climate prediction and climate projection (beyond 2 years' timeframe); (b) research and development; (c) regional climate change adaptation planning; and (d) capacity building for climate services. The Consortium has been working on these activities in the region over the last two decades. Attached to the RCC Network, there will be the establishment of a WMO Regional Information Centre, for the SWOCCP, which will provide support to the RCC Network in the development of its products and services, specialized training and capacity building, complementing the existing training in the region; and the development of a UUP for the dissemination of the RCC Network products and tools within SWOCCP region. This platform will also be used for the exchange of information on climate change adaptation planning and other related activities, including the need for dissemination of M-HIF-EWS and the SWOCCP climate outlooks.

The framework for the operation of this RCC Network will be hosted and run will be determined in the RCC-Network strategy and operating plan. Once the RCC Network is established, the roles, responsibilities and the rules and responsibilities of each RCC-Netwrok Team, is endorsed by the WMO Regional Association (ARICA) and approved by IOC Member States. The agreements between member countries and the IOC will be prepared following WMO recommendations.

The DRM institutions in the SWOCC region have been benefiting from the support of the Indian Ocean Regional Information Centre (IOTRC) for many years, and the experience gained through this collaboration with our countries has demonstrated that the weak link in EWS lies in the ability to generate, disseminate and update information on climate hazards. The RCC Network will provide a platform for the exchange of information between NMHSs and DRM institutions. This component will then strengthen supporting this partnership at national and regional levels, while fostering the development of stakeholder-based emergency response plans.

The SWOCCP will be funded by IOC, through its support to the RCC-Network and the NMHSs. To support the long-term production of regular services, the multi-lateral agreements will provide details of how each Member State will finance contributions to the running costs of the RCC Network and attached facilities beyond the project's lifetime – e.g. finance for the maintenance and calibration laboratory; the specialized training center; the UUP; and the SWOCCP.

A regional market completion will also be developed to identify additional sources of funding for the RCC Network from public and private sectors, e.g., through commercialization of regional CP-CCS (see Annexes 3a and 3t).

Capacity development plans will be developed to support organizational change required to support the digital transformation of the RCC Network. This includes developing a business plan for the network (Annex 20) and recruiting and talent staff (see Annex ZD).

The four target countries in the SWIO region are not Agencies, and therefore a mechanism needs to be sought to ensure that they are able to manage revenues and incomes from their commercial services and cost-recovery that would support GCM. Therefore, the legislative and regulatory material for each of the four target countries will need to be reviewed and adapted as necessary (Annex 20). This will include ensuring that there is a mechanism for revenue generation from commercial services and cost-recovery, or establishment of a CIEWS Fund to manage such funds; that is, in essence, a fund to support GCM. The legislative and regulatory material should be reviewed and adapted as necessary (Annex 20). Market surveys will then be carried out to explore opportunities to establish or strengthen existing national networks.

A baseline study on how CP-CCS and NHI-BF-EVS for hydrometeorological hazards are used in each beneficiary country, and on user requirements for improving such services will be carried out in order to reconcile the sector needs with the network capabilities. This will also provide a basis for developing a business plan for fine-tuning the design of the hydroclimatic observation, modeling, forecasting and service delivery systems. In general, users want more accurate, reliable, timely and location-specific forecasts, provided with a user-friendly terminology. However, there are many challenges to be overcome in order to meet these needs. For example, in the case of NHI-BF-EVS for instance, for the development of target products for the agriculture sector, detailed user requirements are needed, particularly regarding (a) the required content of the forecast advisories; (b) the format and structure of the forecast advisories that will be used to provide the information; (c) the required content of the agrimet advisories (i.e. variables, locations, observations, forecasts, etc.) that will be used to provide the information; (d) the required content of the advisory network to provide the data for the required locations and variables; (e) is the agrimet data to calculate forecast data from NWP?; (f) define the channels for dissemination of the agrimet advisories; (g) etc.

As a first step, the RCC Network will be connected with the Global Basic Observing System (GBOS) surface observation network and associated ICT systems for the SWIO region aligned with the Global Basic Observing Network (GBON) and the World Climate Research Programme (WCRP). The GBOS is being implemented by the Global Systematic Observations Financing Facility (SOFiF) to the completion of the implementation of the networks. Component 1 will also strengthen institutional and financial status of NHIWs with particular attention given to regional harmonization of climate-related products and services, and to ensure that the RCC Network provides core training to NMHS staff members and ensures the harmonization of climate-related products and services across countries, based on international good practices and WMO technical regulations that defined the service.

While the level of the NHIWs of the 4 countries is different (please see Table 2 in section A.21), they all face similar challenges with regard to institutional capacity, regulatory framework and financial sustainability. In this sense, the activities under Component 1 will be implemented equally across the four countries.

## B

Under each activity, each country will develop a National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS) to coordinate, facilitate and strengthen collaboration among national institutions for enhanced use of CP- CS and to facilitate a long-term sustainable operational model for national climate services.

As a result of this activity, the Regional Climate Services Framework (RCSF) will be developed to coordinate, facilitate and strengthen collaboration among regional institutions (e.g. NGOs) for enhanced use of CP-CS and as the first step in introducing an harmonized approach across the region and input to the NFCs.

* **Activity 1.1.2 Establish the legal, institutional, financial and organisational model for the establishment of the RCFCS:** This activity will develop and develop an operating plan for setting up and operationalization it; reach formal designation by WMO

This activity includes the development of a legal, institutional, financial and organizational model for the establishment of the RCFCS. This process will be done through regional meetings and workshops led by IOC, with the participation of the four countries.

As each country will have, a particular function(s) in the RCC Network, this need to be discussed and agreed at the national level. The WMO Regional Climate Services Centre (RCC) Secretariat will be responsible for coordinating the inputs and organize the relevant consultative meetings and workshops at the national level.

* **Activity 1.1.3 Define requirements and develop operating plans for the establishment of a regional laboratory for maintenance and annual calibration of equipment as a WMO Regional Instrument Centre, and its operational strategy:** At the regional level, led by IOC, there will be regional workshops with the participation of the four countries to refine the requirements and develop strategies for the establishment of a regional laboratory in Seychelles (led by the NIMHS of Mauritius).

At the national level, there will be initial meetings for development of the operating plans, and follow-up meetings/field visits to assess progress in the establishment of a regional laboratory in Seychelles (led by the NIMHS of Mauritius) and a regional training for specialized training in Mauritius (led by the NIMHS of Mauritius).

* **Activity 1.1.4 Prepare a strategy to improve Regional Numerical Weather and Climate Prediction (WNCP):** The development of the Regional Numerical Weather and Climate Prediction strategy will be done at the regional level. Led by IOC, there will be consultative workshops with the participation of the four countries.

## Output 1.2 Institutional arrangements for NMHS operation strengthened and hydromet laws prepared with potential CIEWS funds established

* **Activity 1.2.1 Strengthen the institutional, operating and financial strategy of NMHSs for each target country:** Under this activity, each country will review existing arrangements and will develop institutional, operating and financial strategies of their NMHSs.

## Component 2: High-quality climate-related data, improved multi-hazard impact-based forecasts and EWS (MH-IF-EWS), and climate risk assessments

Under Component 2, equipment for observing, monitoring networks, and information systems will be implemented in the selected countries following the WMO technical regulations standards. Moreover, the technical capacities and expertise of NMHC staff members will be improved through specialized and hands-on training on the use of the new equipment, as well as on the management of the networks, services, and their operationalization. Stakeholders and other users will also be trained under this component.

To facilitate hydro-meteorological equipment maintenance and organization of trainings, a regional specialized technical center (RSTC) will be established in one of the selected countries. The RSTC will be fully refurbished and run attached to the RCC-Networks. These activities will also enhance regional collaboration, enable sustainable financing for the long-term production of effective CF-CPs, which require high-quality, well-maintained equipment (e.g., sensors).

To further support CP-CS, understanding of climate-related risks on economic sectors and communities will be improved by developing a series of hazard maps and vulnerability assessments. These products will take into account climate change impacts on return periods/intensity of selected hazards; moreover, they will be produced using participatory approaches with NMHCs, DRM institutions, local authorities of hazard-prone areas, and public and private sector stakeholders to ensure that the information is relevant for decision-making and risk management.

The maps will be used as main assets at stake and must vulnerable people affected by the selected hazards from a local point of view. Based on how populations were affected during past events. By indicating the location, frequency, intensity, and impacts of hazards, these maps will be useful to develop risk prevention plans under Component 2.

Multi-hazard impact-based forecasting and early warning systems/services/capability will be built through shifting from deterministic to probabilistic forecast techniques. NMHCs will make use of recent scientific and technological developments in the field of climate change and extreme events. The new generation of hydro-meteorological system products from advanced centers at very high-resolution (e.g., as those generated within the framework of the Copernicus Climate Change Service) will be used to provide more accurate forecasts, including those exploring newly developing such as artificial intelligence. This will result in more accurate forecasts of the likelihood and severity of the events which will also increase confidence of end-users in using such forecasts and warnings in their decision-making processes. Threshold values for issuing warnings will be determined based

## Climate change projections, downscaled to specific sectors, and maps produced under this Component will be used by all countries in the region to strengthen their climate-sensitive sectors through the UfP, in association with the RCC-Net, and strengthened under Component 2.

### As none of the target countries have the capacity for end-to-end MiH-EWS and the Component 2 of the proposed project is focused on strengthening the national early warning systems (N-EWS) and climate monitoring capabilities, making use of advanced modeling techniques and downsampling to the country/sectoral levels for improved forecasting, production of target products to user sectors, and proper dissemination of forecasts and warnings. Aligned with the UfP, the proposed project will also support the implementation of the 4 components of the proposed MiH-EWS as per the proposed hybrid project interventions.

### In general, all activities under Component 2 are to be implemented by the 4 countries in the region through their national meteorological and hydrological services (NMHSs) and the “Observation and Monitoring” component of GFCS, where investments in Madagascar and Comoros are on the critical and attention will be given to ensure that these systems are fully operational and sustainable. The UfP will support the operation and maintain in order to be sustainable), while Mauritius and Seychelles will be able to operate and maintain an advanced observation and monitoring, in a sustainable way (Output 2.1). As the observational data generated by these countries will be shared with other countries through GFCS, it is expected that all countries will benefit, even if there are different levels of investment.

### Some of the activities under Component 2 include: implementation of the Climate Baseline Information System (CBIS), which the current baseline for the 4 countries is at the “Basic” level; no interventions will be done equally across all countries, to reach the “Regional” level. The CBIS will be implemented in a phased manner, with Mauritius and Seychelles being the first two countries to benefit from this intervention (Output 2.1). This difference relates to the fact that these countries will be hosting the “regional calibration lab” and the “regional training center” (Output 2.2). Activities under the Output 2.3 will be equally implemented in all four countries, none of them will be left behind. The CBIS will provide a framework for generating climate data and climate-specific techniques to generate science-based hydro-meteorological information.

### **Outcome 1: The Observations, Expected Results, Outputs, Results, and Activities (summary below) and detailed description provided in section E.5.3 are implemented under Component 2**

#### Outcome 2: Strengthened capacities and capabilities for detecting and monitoring climate-related hazards

##### Output 2.1 Enhanced hydro-meteorological observation and monitoring, by making use of innovative technologies and specific techniques
* **Activity 2.1.1 Medium/long-range climate-related observation and monitoring network**  
Under this activity, the networks will be modernized in each country based on the detailed plan and specifications provided.

##### In Comoros: Rehabilitation/vestment of the building where the National Meteorological Service is based; upgrade of the meteorological radar (Nexrad) to a Doppler radar; installation of 2 new weather stations (one hydrometeorological station – Annex 1, and two hydrologic stations following HYCOS recommendations); sub-synoptic meteorological network (30 stations); implementation of the national meteorological satellite system (ASOS, meteorological/agroclimatic buoys; Upper air radiosounding system; Doppler radar; Tower; Rader for radar).

##### In Madagascar: Upgrade national synoptic networks (meteorological stations following the Feasibility Study recommendations), sub-synoptic meteorological network (30 stations); implementation of the national meteorological satellite system (ASOS, meteorological/agroclimatic buoys; Upper air radiosounding network; Doppler radar; Tower for radar).

### Activities 2.1.2 Modernise/upgrade the information and communication technology systems

### Output 2.2 Regional facilities for research and training established

#### Activity 2.2.1 Establishes a maintenance and calibration laboratory (WMO Regional Instrument Centre)

A regional maintenance laboratory (WMO Regional Instrument Centre) will be built and equipped in Seychelles, Mauritius, Madagascar, and Comoros to provide maintenance and calibration services of equipment for the four countries in the region, following the strategy developed under Activity 1.1.3.

#### Activity 2.2.2 Expands the specialized training centre for the region, hosted by Mauritius (WMO will contribute to the WMO Global Campus® by making available training materials at the center to all WMO Members)

A specialized training center for the region will be equipped in Mauritius to support training in the region, for the four countries. The training center will be linked to the WMO Global Campus® and will use training materials developed by this training center, following the strategy developed under Activity 1.1.3.

Expected result 3: Generation of science-based multi-hazard weather, climate and water risk information improves the quality of forecasts and warning, and enhances their use by stakeholders.

Outcome 3.1: Science-based multi-hazard weather and climate risk information generated.

#### Activity 2.3.1 Downscale and calibrate meteorological forecasts by making use of numerical weather prediction models and other tools such as artificial intelligence – these developments will be done at a regional platform (in a cloud) with contribution of experts from the four NMHSs

Aligned to the WMO Regional Numerical Weather and Climate Prediction developed under Activity 1.4, staff from the four NMHSs will be on hands-on training at the regional center in Mauritius. This activity will be coordinated by IOC, and includes the participation of international experts as trainers. The training will cover a range of topics including downscaling techniques and other technical tasks such as forecast verification and downscaling to national level for each of the four countries.

## Activity 2.3.2 Establish threshold values for issuing warnings based on extreme value analysis review of historical hydrometeorological events

This is an activity carried out at the national and local levels in each country, with workshops with NMHDs and DRM institutions and other stakeholders (including NGOs) to agree on national threshold values for issuing warnings based on extreme value analysis and review of historical hydrometeorological events in each country.

There will also be regional workshops led by IOC, with the participation of the four NMHDs and of PIROD, for harmonization of threshold warnings across the region to facilitate integration of warning information into the WMO’s “weather” type dissemination.

## Activity 2.3.3 Develop hazard maps

This is an activity carried out at the national and local levels in each country. Based on the hazard maps produced under Activity 2.3.3, a mapping exercise for climate-risk vulnerability – of people and assets - will be conducted in a participatory way with stakeholders from hazardous-prone areas, public and private sector representatives and non-governmental organizations (NGOs), tourism, health, etc.

There will also be workshops at national and local levels to raise awareness of climate-related vulnerability and risks and to identify potential impacts from extreme events.

## Activity 2.3.5 Undertake risk analysis incorporating hazard, exposure and vulnerability to identify potential impacts from extreme events

This is an activity carried out at the national and local levels in each country. This activity will involve compilation of various datasets available for characterizing hazard, vulnerability and exposure to develop a tool in quantifying risks, i.e., potential impact. This tool will then be used to analyse exposures and determine the most vulnerable areas and populations in each country with respect to each year’s respective hazards. This risk analysis tool or methodology will be used in generating multi-hazard impact-based forecasts and decision support information through the knowledge and decision support system that will be developed in Output 3.2.

## Activity 2.3.6 Carry out downscaling of global and regional seasonal forecasts to national level, and use them for climate risk management

Aligned with the strategy to improve Regional Numerical Weather and Climate Prediction developed under Action 1.4, this activity involves training at the regional center in Mauritius. This activity will be coordinated by IOC, and includes the participation of international experts as lecturers. The training will cover a range of topics including downscaling techniques and other technical tasks such as downloading of global and regional seasonal forecasts to national level and use for production of agrometeorological products.

## Activity 2.3.7 Build the capacity to downscale climate models for national purposes

Aligned with the strategy to improve Regional Numerical Weather and Climate Prediction developed under Action 1.4, this activity involves training at the regional center in Mauritius. This activity will be coordinated by IOC, and includes the participation of international experts as lecturers. The training will cover a range of topics including downscaling techniques and other technical tasks such as downloading of climate models to national purposes in each of the four countries.

## Activity 2.3.8 Build the capacity for hydrological modeling for flood forecasting (i.e., flood propagation model)

Under this activity, staff from the four NMHDs will be on hands-on training at the regional center in Mauritius. This activity will be coordinated by IOC and includes the participation of international experts

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE 40 OF 119

## Component 3: Enhanced accessibility and use of climate services for climate change adaptation, and improved capabilities in implementing a people-centred MH-BF-EWS for disaster risk reduction

As regional cooperation for climate risk monitoring is strengthened (under Component 1) and equipment expertise and capacity are built up (under Component 2), the project will support countries to ensure that they are able to improve the range and quality of their climate-related information, products and services, based on user feedback. This will be achieved by strengthening the capacity of national meteorological and hydrological services to use this information in decision-making tools. Component 3 particularly aims at equipping decision-makers and communities with the necessary skills and knowledge for using climate services to better understand climate variability and change. The update of the long-term climate adaptation plans will be linked to sections that specifically address how climate services can be used to improve disaster risk reduction.

The project will also support countries in strengthening existing communication platforms for the producers and users of CFS (such as the UNFCCC) by discussion on the development of a common platform for sharing information, including through joint workshops and interactions among all stakeholders; collection of user feedback on CFS, and production of CFS-based user case studies. This will be done in close collaboration with the WMO, and in line with the work associated with the RCC-Network strategy and operating plan, under Activity 1.2.4 a national framework for MH-BF-EWS will be developed to guide the implementation of the project at country level. The framework will include the implementation of MH-BF-EWS at the local level. This involves collaboration among NMHSs, PRROs and the local government, as well as with other relevant stakeholders (e.g., UN agencies and NGOs). A list of risk colour-codes, and description of related impacts and responses (i.e., general impact table and general response table) will be developed for each region. The regions will be defined based on the climate-sensitive areas) to be selected to use such risk matrices and associated information for testing and validating MH-BF-EWS with the communities in these regions. This will be done through workshops, capacity building and training sessions followed by the development/update early warning products from hazard to impact-based for issuance, communication and dissemination of warnings. The project will also support countries in developing a collaborative approach among the NMHSs, the National DRM agencies, the local Government, and stakeholder in the selected regions.

Testing and validating the impact and response tables on early warning in project sites will be done in a collaborative and participatory manner. The above-mentioned impact and response tables will then be located for the project sites to ensure that they are relevant and useful for the communities involved (e.g., vulnerable communities, including indigenous), as well as the local decision-makers, including local government, UN agencies and NGOs. This will be done through workshops, capacity building and training sessions followed by the development of a collaborative approach among the NMHSs, the National DRM agencies, the local Government, and stakeholder in the selected regions to ensure coordination and collaboration throughout the testing and validation, as well as for the operationalization of the EWS.

The shift to impact-based forecasting and warning will imply a re-design of EWS – these would need to clearly incorporate both decision-making aspects and the perceptions in terms of risk vulnerable communities. The latter, as well as the former, are key elements that should be considered when designing an EWS. The re-design of the EWS will be optimal for use and relevance. Aspects related to the content of warning messages (which need to be clear and understandable), the frequency of issuance of warnings, the channels used for timely delivery and dissemination of these warnings, and the provisions of end-user feedback mechanisms will be part of this re-design process. The above-mentioned aspects are inter-related and therefore are further developed through done through meetings, workshops and capacity building/training activities.

These developments and testing at the local level will be done in the first three years of the proposed project. In parallel with these activities, a scenario analysis will be undertaken to test the impact of MH-BF-EWS in the whole country (in the 4 countries) will be undertaken using a scenario approach. Knowledge products and professional development workshops will also be developed for NMHSs, PRROs, government agencies, NGOs on the ground and community leaders, in order to reach all population in the 4 countries. Dissemination and outreach activities will be carried out through various channels (e.g., traditional media such as radio and television; other uses of traditional media (such as local radio programs); and social media (such as Twitter and Facebook) and other digital platforms). The project will also support the development of a national framework for MH-BF-EWS. This will be supported by the proposed project, and by working with other UN and other agencies on the ground (e.g., FAO, WFP, etc.). The framework will include the development of risk matrices and associated information, as well as the aggregation of information from the four target countries, with MH-BF and color-coded risk-based warning regions.

## Implementation of a “one-stop” type platform that would also contribute to the WMO Global Multi-hazard Alert System (GMAS) will be jointly developed to support early warnings/early actions at regional, national and local levels. While the GMAS is primarily focused on meteorological hazards, it will be extended to also address impacts to the relevant socioeconomic sectors in the country (i.e., agriculture, fisheries and tourism).

### Source: WMO Guidelines on impact-based forecast and warning services.

Under Component 3, significant investments are done in association with the GFCS Component “User Interface Platform” (UIP) to ensure that the GMAS is implemented in all countries by taking a regional and harmonized approach (please see Table 2 in section A.21). While noting that Mauritius and Seychelles are already an “Essential Node” of the GMAS, this project will be implemented as a Regional Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) project takes a regional approach to facilitate sharing of expertise and experiences among the countries, so that those countries with limited resources can benefit from the experience of others. The project will support the production of forecasts and risk-based warnings, including the last mile connectivity and engagement of vulnerable communities and relevant stakeholders in the delivery of weather and agrometeorological bulletins and early warning services to all 4 countries.

### Output 3.1: Outcomes, Expected Results, Outputs and Activities (summary below and detailed description provided in section E.6) are planned under Component 3:

Output 3.1.1: Develop and implement a response capability with improved gender and vulnerable people inclusiveness across the EWS value chain

Expected result 4: Improved community resilience through the implementation of person-centered MH-IBF-EWS at national level.

Output 3.1.2: Production, dissemination and uptake of CP-CS, including IBF-EWS, at regional and national level:

* **Activity 3.1.1.5:** Fulfill operational standards (protocol operating procedures) to produce and deliver improved daily weather bulletins, multi-hazard impact-based forecasts, seasonal forecasts and agrometeorological advisories at national level.

Under this activity, there will be a consultative process to improve the production and delivery of weather forecasts and agrometeorological advisories in each country. In each country, Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) will be developed for the production and dissemination of products between the NMHS and relevant stakeholders, and of delivery of forecasts, advisories and warnings. Specific agrometeorological bulletins will be developed in each countries.

## B

### Activity 3.2.1: Short-term risk information sharing and coordination with emergency services for on-the-ground interventions at national and local levels

In line with the disaster risk management cycle, this activity will first review existing emergency response plans and communication processes between relevant stakeholders with a view to improve them to guide quick-on-the-ground responses.

Because disaster risk happens at the local level, the national response plans will be downscaled into local-level response plans. These will be based on the same principles as those produced under Elements 2.3.1 and 2.3.4. At this level, it is not always possible for emergency services to reach the population in time to prevent or mitigate a disaster event. Therefore, at the local level (i.e., local volunteers or trusted local stakeholders/leaders who can facilitate emergency responses at the local level), there will be a need to develop and disseminate risk information and advice on risk prevention and to reduce disruption if a hazardous event will be strengthened through training, awareness campaigns, and other communication activities. The objective is also to know how to disseminate national emergency response plans at the local level to improve risk prevention, mitigation, and preparedness.

### Activity 3.2.2: Develop risk matrices for each hazard with agreed risk levels and colour-codes, and description of related impacts and responsibilities; test and validate them in selected sites in the four countries (Madagascar, Mauritius, Seychelles, and Comoros)

Aligned with the activity 3.2.1, a national framework for MfH-IBF-EWS in each country will be developed and harmonized with the existing disaster risk management frameworks at both the national and local levels. This involves collaboration among MNHI, PRCI and the national DRM institutions, and other relevant stakeholders, to develop a national framework for MfH-IBF-EWS that is aligned with the national DRM framework and includes the following elements: definition of the hazard(s), description of related impacts and responsibilities. Selected vulnerability sites in each country will be used as pilot sites to test and validate these risk matrices.

The development of the national framework for MfH-IBF-EWS, including the harmonization with the existing disaster risk management frameworks at both the national and local levels, is expanded and rolled out nationwide (in the last two years of the project) using scenarios, and through.

| AFD | GCF Accredited Entity (AE) | AFD will be the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Project and will be responsible for overseeing the implementation, financial management, evaluation, reporting and monitoring of the project. AFD will ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the approved project document and will be responsible for the execution of the project and ensure the proper management and application of funds. AFD will also be responsible for ensuring compliance with the GCF Agreement and the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA). |

The National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS) of each country will be responsible for implementing the project at the national level. NMHSs, as the NIP and others NSPs, will not be mandated for the use of funds.

The NMHS, as national lead implementing entity, will host the national project coordinator. The NMHS with the support of the EE through the PMU team and other partners will ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the national level and will actively participate to procurement process co-design and implementation, capacity building programs and training sessions, equipment's installation and maintenance, as well as monitoring and evaluation activities.

The National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS) of each country will be responsible for implementing the project at the national level. NMHSs, as the NIP and others NSPs, will not be mandated for the use of funds.

#### Figure 2: Project Organizational structure

Project delivery stakeholder roles, mandates, and governance structures are described and illustrated below.

AFD as the AE for the proposed project has a strong and established working experience in IUC countries. Its regional headquarters is based in La Réunion island, while three AFD offices are present in Mauritius (covering Madagascar), Comoros, and Seychelles. The AFD will manage the implementation of four proposals that have been approved by GCF Board (P2014, 42, 95 and 116). As the AE, AFD will manage the financial and implementation of the project. AFD will have a quality assurance role involving AFD staff in Paris headquarters and at the regional level, which will be supported by the Project Management Unit (PMU) based in Paris. The PMU will also support its Madagascar and Comoros offices. This quality assurance role will also be ensured through the Regional Project Steering Committee (RPSC) (see below for details) which will contribute to AFD objectives and provide independent

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (V2.0) PAGE OF 119

project preparation and monitoring functions together. AFG and the RPSC will ensure that appropriate project milestones are managed and completed, for example by reviewing progress against set up targets and providing recommendations to the project back on track if needed. Project quality assurance must be independent of the Project Management Unit (PMU) and the Regional Project Coordinator (RPC). The PMU is responsible for reporting to PMU and to the Regional Project Coordinator (RPC). As an Accredited Entity to the GCF, AFG is required to deliver GCF-accredited project oversight and quality assurance services including oversight of progess completion and oversight on project reporting.

IOG will be the EE as the regional organisation under which Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles are members. The IOG Secretariat (Secretariat) will be hosted by the International Organization for Migration (IOM), the PMU and the national counterparts, amongst them NMHSs as NIP and NSPS. The PMU will be hosted by IOM headquarters in Geneva. The Secretariat will be led by a Secretary General who is responsible to the Board of Directors. The Secretariat will be supported by a Project Officer (PO) who will be assigned by the Secretary General of the IOC to the project site. The IOC already manages several projects for GCF and has a strong track record in implementing projects on behalf of the GCF. The IOC has proven experience in project management with different development partners (including the regional EU-funded ISLANDS and AFD-funded ACCLIMATE multi-year projects), and is coordinating project implementation in its member countries. As part of the current project, the Secretariat will be responsible for the overall coordination of the project. As part of this initiative, IOC has received financial support from the GCF, as well as technical support funding from AFG to provide capacity building on climate services and related activities such as data management, quality assurance and auditing. Additionally, IOC is receiving EU support under the INCA (institutional capacity building) programme for the implementation of the project. The Secretariat will be responsible for the overall development and evaluate projects and programmes to align with the EU 9 pillars. AFG will support IOC in the implementation of the organisational actions already identified within the INCA programme.

**NIMBY**: National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHSs) in beneficiary countries will be the co-executing entities and will be involved in all the project activities co - design and co-implement at both national and regional level. NMHSs will be responsible for the implementation of the project, including the development and results achievements. NMS conducted by NMHSs such as DRM institutions will be strongly involved to co-design and co-implement the related EWS and last mile connectivity.

**Core services**: The core services are the following: (1) Climate Services; (2) Hydroclimatic Services; (3) Meteorological Services; (4) Agrometeorological Services. These services are Intera CACTA Climate Services Project: a large project supported by the EU and the S-ACP involving regional cooperation for the improvement of climate services and related applications. The IOC component of this initiative will be directly integrated into the AFG-funded ACCLIMATE project, which is currently being implemented in 10 countries. Funds mobilized through the intra-ACP CS Project will be key in the implementation and monitoring of the Hydroclimatic project to ensure its integration into regional and national policies and programmes, as well as ensure ownership at the national and regional level.

In addition other implementers partners VMDO, GFDRR and UNDRRR will facilitate coordination and synergies with other climate services initiatives. The project will be structured around seven major output objectives: (1) Institutional and human capacities in regional and intergovernmental organisations; (2) Improved climate information services for decision-making at the national level; (3) Improved service delivery improved, including the development of long-term service delivery strategies and development plans (National); (4) Information systems to guide early warning systems, hydroclimatic and weather service developed and accessible (National); (4) Information systems to guide early warning systems, hydroclimatic and weather service developed and accessible (National); (5) Preparedness and response plans with operational procedures that outline early warning decision-making processes and the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy; (6) Improved capacity on early warnings developed(National); and (7) Gender-sensitive training, capacity building programs provided (National). The project will be implemented in a stand-alone document as part of this joint proposal.

More information about the CREWS initiative is provided in a stand-alone document, as part of this joint proposal.

## Project Management Unit (PMU): a regional PMU will be established by IOC in its headquarters and will work under the responsibility of the IOC Project Manager (PM). The PMU, which will be constituted of (i) IOC permanent member staff which will be dedicated to the Hydrometric Project and (ii) National Focal Points (NFPs) and Technical Liaison Officers (TLOs) contracted by a selected international company, will be responsible for the overall project implementation and management. The PMU will be led by a Regional Project Coordinator (RPC). The PMU will work in close collaboration with NFPs, TLOs, and other relevant stakeholders including government partners, as well as in overseeing project activity implementation. The following staff members will form the PMU:

- **Regional Project Coordinator (RPC):** will be a full-time employee of international company hired as the Technical Advisor to the PMU. The RPC will be responsible for the overall project implementation and management, and will work under direct supervision of the PM.

- **National Project Coordinators (NPCs):** will be directly hired by the EAC and NMHS with the support of the supervising international company. NPCs are based in each respective country and are mandated to support NMHS Focal Points in achieving project goals and objectives. The NPCs will sit under the overall guidance and supervision of the RPC, and will work closely with the NMHS Focal Points (FPs) and other relevant stakeholders such as Meteorological Services, General Directorate of Meteorology in Madagascar, Mauritian Meteorological Services and Environmental Protection Agency in Mauritius, to ensure that project activities are implemented effectively. The NPCs will be responsible for facilitating daily project execution and follow-up, in close collaboration with NMHS team through a designated Focal Point (FP) or Project Officer (PO). At the start of the project, NPC responsibilities may include inter alia: i) to lead on relevant national implementation partnerships; ii) to develop policies and disaster risk management strategies; iii) to organize workshops or meetings with regards to project activity, as relevant to the country context; iv) to ensure that all project deliverables are met by the NMHS.

Additional PMU staff members based at the IOC office will include:

- **Hydro-meteorology Expert:** A part-time staff member with technical expertise in hydro-meteorology will be hired on a contract basis. The Hydro-meteorology Expert will provide technical guidance, review applications, and to supervise and advise the RPC on the implementation of technical project activities.

- **Environmental Officer:** to ensure appropriate application of environmental and social safeguards during the project.

- **Financial Officer:** to ensure proper accountability and transparency of project expenditures

- **Procurement Officer:** to ensure procurement processes are followed; manage the contract terms and equipment acquisitions contracts.

- **Monitoring Officer:** to report on technical and financial project implementation, prepare evaluation and monitoring reports.

- **Communication Officer:** to develop, disseminate and archive communication material related to the project.

- **Administrative Officer:** to support project management and conduct daily administrative tasks.

In addition, a number of experts will be hired on an ad-hoc basis by the IOC to review and assess applications and candidate profiles proposed in the offers submitted for vacant positions. The PMU will ensure that the selection of these experts will be to advise IOC on best candidate/service provider to hire for the project execution.

**Provisional PMU staff member profiles are presented in Annexes 4, F5.**

B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

The Hypothetical project as proposed with its components and overall budget of USD 71,364,986 is the most appropriate investment to achieve the desired pathway (see Shift D). A EUR 52,767,866 grant from GCF is sought by ICC and the Governments of Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles to invest in climate change adaptation projects. These countries are among the most vulnerable to climate change impacts on their services that respond to user needs among the most vulnerable communities and sectors. These countries are experiencing a range of climate-related challenges including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events leading to increases in intensity and frequency. Neither the governments nor the vulnerable communities and sectors have the required resources to address these challenges. The proposed project will support the four countries to strengthen risk prevention and mitigation at scale. Comoros and Madagascar, both LDCs, have a large portion of the population living below the poverty line. Their national budgets is in significant deficit and, given the socio-economic conditions, they are unable to finance their adaptation needs. Mauritius and Seychelles are both upper-middle-income countries with high debt levels and wealth inequality; neither country has the resources to address climate change impacts on their services.

Yet, the benefits of the proposed GCF project will essentially be public goods and the financial returns e.g. for climate-sensitive services – will not be sufficient to service debt payments. New business models will be explored and technical assistance provided by the GCF in order to ensure the sustainability of investments made through the project are sustained.

Traditionally, most of the revenues of the National Meteorological Services (NMSs) come from the public sector. The NMSs have been established as government agencies (e.g., Mauritius Meteorology Service (MMS)) or operate as public entities and received most of their funding (about 80%) to the form of direct government grants. However, the current financial situation in all four countries is such that the allocation of significant public funding towards this Project from the four GCF Member States is not sufficient to cover the operation and maintenance costs associated with the required equipment (see Annex 13 for details). The NMSs will not be able to cover all the operation and maintenance costs associated with the required equipment.

Nonetheless, the governments of Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles are committed to support the project through co-financing. In Comoros, the government will provide USD 1,578,000 in-kind, which corresponds to the portion of funds these services receive from their respective governments to support climate QM costs. In Mauritius, the government will provide USD 1,923,640 in-kind and USD 1,000,000 as cash contribution. In Seychelles, the government will provide USD 587,000 in-kind (see Annex 13 for details). These co-financing commitments will serve to finance the construction of new facilities to run and maintain the equipment.

Moreover, the AFU and EU have committed to support the proposed project through contributions in grants of respectively USD 5,946,183 and USD 12,710,916. The EU is supporting most of the regional activities which are already being implemented under the DEVCO-ACIP (Development Cooperation Instrument – Adaptation Component) and Pacific Countries (DEVCO-ACP). These include most of the Component I, except the capacity building. It includes all the activities under Component 2, except for the regional cooperation. Under component 2, EU will support the development of hazard and vulnerability maps.

Under Component 1, GCF will complement the support from the EU in the regional cooperation; it also supports the regional workshops and training sessions under Component 2. The EU is supporting most of the activities in Comoros, and co-finance partly the technical assistance for science-based weather and climate product generation, production and dissemination. Under Component 2, GCF will provide very limited funds to support the participation of staff in regional workshops.

GCF financing will primarily support capacity-building activities. Under component I, GCF will support just one activity under sub-component 1.1.1.1. Under Component 2, GCF will support: (i) Activities 2.1.1.2, 2.2.1 and 2.2.2 on modernization of the observation and monitoring, forecasting and ICT systems (networks and software), and (ii) partly Activities under output 2.3 on

technical assistance and science-based weather and climate product generation. Component 3 is mostly supported by GCF and relates to the implementation of the MNH-BW-EMS and last mile connectivity at national level.

Yet, based on these second contributions, only a partial improvement of existing hydrometeorological services and capacity building will be achieved. The full potential for increasing the value of weather and climate information and products, as well as the development of climate services cannot be realised in the absence of GCF investments (see section D.6 and Annex 3a).

The project will also contribute to the commercialization of new CP-CS, leveraging funds from the new GBON/SOFF initiative, coordination with UN and other agencies, and the implementation of a national HydroMet Law.

From funding donors has been other identified for climate-related projects (see section B.1), however these only cover part of the project costs and are not sufficient to ensure that the Hydromet Fund request does not duplicate work supported by other donors and that opportunities for complementarity are also identified (see Annex 2 F.5).

Funding from other donors has been other identified for climate-related projects. Indeed, climate data, information, forecasts, warnings and other products, supported under the proposed project are considered to be “public goods” because of their non-excludable nature and non-rivalrous consumption benefits; hence, it is unlikely that there will be short-term significant financial returns on investment to repay loans.

The regional CP-CS developed through the Project will not be profitable in the medium-term, so that private sector funding for these services would be required. However, this would require a large amount of additional private sector revenues, but it will only partially cover total costs (15%–20% of the NMBS’ budget) in four countries.

The project will also contribute to the commercialization of new CP-CS, leveraging funds from the new GBON/SOFF initiative and coordination with UN and other agencies. The project will also contribute to the implementation of a national HydroMet Law.

In addition to key national and sub-national stakeholders, additional demand will be created thus facilitating increased budget support in the long-term. Through the project, business plans will be developed for the transition from current business models (e.g., cost recovery) to new business models that are more sustainable and can be scaled up nationally. Meteorology Services Cost Recovery (WMO-No. 904)138 The new business models will be based on cost-recovery for the services provided, with a focus on the private sector. In addition, other revenue streams from weather and climate services in Comoros and Madagascar are provided by ASECNA139, e.g., billing, insurance, tourism, energy, among others.

The project will also contribute to the commercialization of new CP-CS, leveraging funds from the new GBON/SOFF initiative, coordination with UN and other agencies, and as a part of the strategy for ensuring long-term sustainability.

Finally, the IOC, representing the beneficiary countries, will be in charge of project implementation and will be the recipient of the funds through APOC financial transfers. By the nature of its status and mandate given by its Member States, the IOC is best placed to ensure that the project goes into effect.

### B.6 Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words; maximum 1 page)

The hydrometeorological equipment established through the project will be managed, maintained, upgraded and replaced as needed by the beneficiary governments during and after the project, or at the regional level by IOC/CRCC-NET. The project will also contribute to the commercialization of new CP-CS, leveraging funds from the new GBON/SOFF initiative, coordination with UN and other agencies, and as a part of the strategy for ensuring long-term sustainability.

The project will also contribute to the commercialization of new CP-CS, leveraging funds from the new GBON/SOFF initiative, coordination with UN and other agencies, and as a part of the strategy for ensuring that these stakeholders are able to continue co-producing and using climate services (CP-CS) once the project ends. The project will also contribute to the implementation of a national HydroMet Law.

1. **Strengthening of NMHS (including financial strategy)**

Under Output 1.3, institutional capacity of the NMHS in each country will be strengthened in order to streamline roles and responsibilities, improve their working environment (to retain skilled staff) and, in particular, improve their financial sustainability. This will be achieved through a number of measures:

- **National Hydro-meteorological Services (NMHSs)**: The NMHS is the national legal instrument establishing a National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS), an important element for the implementation of the WMO Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS). It defines roles, responsibilities; and legal authority for certain responsibilities. Giving recognition of its contribution to society, to the economy and to the environment will be essential in order to strengthen institutional capacity.

- **Hydro-meteorological Services Law**: The NMHSs will also be strengthened through a Hydro-meteorological Services Law (HMSL). This is a national legal instrument establishing a National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS), an important element for the implementation of the WMO Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS). It defines roles, responsibilities; and legal authority for certain responsibilities. Giving recognition of its contribution to society, to the economy and to the environment will be essential in order to strengthen institutional capacity.

- **Cost-recovery and business plan**: A cost-recovery and business plan that enables the production and delivery of improved CP-C5. Hence, the NMHSs will also be strengthened through a cost-recovery and business plan that enables the production and delivery of improved CP-C5. This will be achieved by:

  - **Cost recovery**: The NMHSs will be able to recover their costs from users for services provided.
  - **Business plan**: A business plan will be prepared, considering opportunities to commercialize CP-C5 in each target country to generate new income that can be reinvested towards maintenance and update of hydro-meteorological services.

- **National Hydro-meteorological Services Law (HMSL)**: The NMHSs will also be strengthened through a National Hydro-meteorological Services Law (HMSL).   - **Cost recovery**: The NMHSs will be able to recover their costs from users for services provided.
  - **Business plan**: A business plan will be prepared, considering opportunities to commercialize CP-C5 in each target country to generate new income that can be reinvested towards maintenance and update of hydro-meteorological services.

This is a national legal instrument establishing a National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS), an important element for the implementation of the WMO Global Framework for Climate Services (GFCS). It defines roles, responsibilities; and legal authority for certain responsibilities. Giving recognition of its contribution to society, to the economy and to the environment will be essential in order to strengthen institutional capacity.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WIND PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE OF 119

## Building and Enhancing Understanding of CP-C3 that are currently in place on user feedback

The CP-C3 developed with end-users will be significantly improved on addressing users' needs. The CP-C3 produced under this project will be co-developed with users during workshops, consultations, and joint training sessions. This will ensure a better understanding of the CP-C3 by end-users. In addition to understanding of CP-C3, additional user feedback on CP-C3 will be systematically collected via the UPM (the online platform). The UPM will include a page for users to assess the quality of CP-C3 and provide feedback regarding the selection of services, their relevance, and use. SWIOCCFOC, as users of CP-C3 is expected to share its experience and recommendations for CP-C improvements. This will ensure the hydro-meteorological services continue to meet user needs in a timely manner.

The CP-C3 generated by the proposed project will be based on open-source tools and formats endorsed by WMO, and will be compatible with the existing CP-C3. This will create a continuity of practice at the regional level, for using and improving CP-C3s, coordinated through the RCC-Netwrok and by IOC through the organisation of the SWIOCCFOC.

### Benefits for CP-C3 communication

As CP improves, demand from the sectorial private sector for such services is expected to increase. Primary commercial beneficiaries / sectors are agriculture, aviation, maritime, and tourism. Preliminary discussions in the 4 countries indicate that the current CP-C3s are not meeting user needs as they do not provide sufficiently accurate forecasts. Shipping sector will also be approached, as well as the aviation sector in Mauritius and Seychelles where aeronautical meteorological services are provided by NMMS (in Comoros and Madagascar; these services are currently being upgraded by ASEAN). Annual revenue from the private sector is estimated at USD 10 million. The commercialization of CP-C3 this interest will be further investigated under the proposed project. A detailed business plan will be developed, including a market analysis, identification of potential customers, development of products, designed to respond to the specific demands of users in the private sector (including insurance sector). The project will also support the development of a national policy on the commercialization of meteorological services ranging from 10% (Comoros and Seychelles) to 30% (Mauritius); this income will be redirected towards equipment investment, upscaling or repositioning of existing CP-C3s, training of users in private sector (including a specialized business unit), and the testing and the continuous production of high-quality CP-C3.

Finally, it should be noted that an estimate operation and maintenance budget has been proposed for the proposed project. The total estimated cost of the equipment is USD 10 million. The total estimated cost of installing the equipment installation is about USD 5,941,000 distributed to four countries. This will partly be covered by the governments of Comoros and Madagascar (in-kind) while the governments of Mauritius and Seychelles will take full care of their respective O&M budgets. The O&M budget will be updated during project implementation, based on

## B

### a more detailed estimation of current human and financial resources, and fine-tuned estimates of operation and maintenance needs for each country.

As for Mauritius and Seychelles, in order to complement government funding, the strengthening and expansion of the institutional capacity of the two countries will be achieved to achieve the availability and sustainability of the DCM budget for mainstreaming Climate services commercialization and establishing cost recovery of meteorological services, as described in detail in Annex 21. The same applies to Madagascar, where a similar objective can also be achieved by the project for Comoros and Madagascar (see in particular page 6 of the OPM plan, Annex 21). A Commitment Letter from IOC confirming country committed post-project implementation period to operate and sustain the DCM is expected to be received by the countries and IOC will be attached to the project package.

For Comoros and Madagascar, additional agreements with ASEANA (see OPM Annex, Annex 21 page 4) are being developed in order to ensure that the costs of the project are covered. The cost recovery mechanism for the DCM is also being discussed under the ASEANA umbrella. It is recalled that AFD is a long-standing partner of ASEANA and has recently supported ASEANA with a non-sovereign loan of 60 MD.

As for Mauritius, Seychelles and Madagascar, the project will be listed in the NMRFDS and DRM institutions to be strong enough to allow the Mauritius and Seychelles to leverage sufficient OSM costs and to allow Madagascar and Comoros to take over GCM commitments on DCM to be disbursed during the project onset. Description of all activities that are expected to be carried out by the countries in order to create a sustainable system for recovery of investments is described in the OPM Plan provided as part of the project proposal package (Annex 21).

## 5.- Leverage from funding sources

The World Bank, through its IDA project, is supporting the Global Alliance for Hydromet Development (GAHD), which is development the GBON concept and related Systematic Observation Financing Facility (SOFF). This facility will be used to finance the costs of the DCM. The objective of this financing mechanism is to ensure the sustainability of the system; the project is considering a more secure alternative based on the mobilization of funds from the SOFF/GBON in order to support the GCM costs (see OPM Annex, page 21, note 9). Nothing that has been discussed with the World Bank and it is expected that this will be confirmed by the WMO. Thus, a request has already been submitted to the WMO as a project technical partner, to consider these countries for the SOFF/GBON facility. A response is expected to be received by the countries and IOC from WMO to be attached to the project package.

## 6.- Support from PRIOD

The PRIOD Cross Network (see IO region), which is already involved in the IOC project generally and EWS actions during extreme events, will play a crucial role as it is implementation of the MiH-EF/EWS and last mile connectivity by ensuring that critical early warning information is delivered in a timely and efficient manner. PRIOD will also be responsible for the development of a pilot project to implement the concept of Forecast-based Financing (FiBF), which is being currently piloted in the region in Mozambique, but expanded to other countries in the region (see OPM Annex 21). The FiBF concept is based on the use of early warning systems and decision support tools (e.g. mobile phones, SMS, radio, etc.) as well as other mechanisms). PRIOD has already expressed its support to the project, and an official letter from IFRC is expected to be also received and attached to the project package to confirm PRIOD commitment.

### C

- **core training on climate services, climate related risks and impacts on communities and sectors for 15 staff members of NMHSs, DRM institutions and in climate-sensitive sectors**, as well as training of trainers (USD 810,000)
- **training on science-based product generation (USD 844,000).**
- **training will be provided as part of the development of hazard, vulnerability and risk maps (USD 1,400,000).**
- **In addition, people-centered multi-hazard impact-based forecasts and early warning systems will be established in beneficiary countries through the support of regional facilities and national focal points for insurance brokers (USD 5,428,000).**

These capacity building interventions implemented under Components 1 and 2 of the project, will be critical to ensure the production of timely, reliable, accurate and targeted to the needs of users in climate-sensitive sectors. The core trainings under Component 1 (Activity 1.3.2) will enhance understanding of how climate-related hazards can impact on key economic activities and livelihoods, as well as how they can exacerbate negative impacts, while the technical trainings to be implemented under Component 2 (Activities 2.3-1.2-3.8) will improve the capacity of NMHSs and other relevant institutions in beneficiary countries to produce climate-related information as decision-making tools to reduce climate-related vulnerabilities and risks. Finally, Activities 2.3.3 and 2.3.4 will serve to build understanding in the beneficiary countries of areas most impacted by climate-sensitive hazards, and the vulnerability of populations living there, with a view to using this knowledge to better use NMHSs in conjunction with weather forecasts in order to produce multi-hazard impact-based forecasts, which introduces a paradigm shift from the current practice of issuing warnings based on what is likely to happen (e.g. "the temperature will be high") or the weather will do", in accordance with the guidelines provided by WMO**17** (Activities 2.3-1.2-3.5; and 3.2-1.3-2.3).

It should also be noted that the regional aspects of the project that underpin collaboration, harmonization of approaches, sharing of best practices, and pooling of resources will be supported by technical assistance and capacities on successful national initiatives: the regional facilities to be set up attached to the RCC-Network, and short-term training for NMHS staff in climate services and early warning. The Focal Points Unit component will increase the capacity and expertise of each country in specific fields (depending on the regional components they will host).

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section outlines the performance of the project against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Total Investment Framework.

D.1 PROJECT OUTCOMES (approximately 1 page)

The project envisions increasing climate change resilience and reducing loss of life, property, and livelihoods due to climate-induced threats in Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles. The potential of the project to contribute to the GCF's Strategic Objective 2 is outlined below.

### 1. Strengthened institutional and regulatory frameworks

Institutional capacity for disaster risk management within the NMRIs will be strengthened through Component 1; moreover, regional cooperation for climate-related hazard monitoring and climate risk management will be institutionalized in the long term by establishing a Regional Climate Risk Management Centre (RCMCC). Regulatory framework to produce C-PS will be developed with Activity 1.1.1 focusing on the end-user requirements. Capacity building for governmental stakeholders in sectoral ministries and in the NMRIs will be provided to ensure that they are able to use the C-PS effectively, thereby reducing climate-related risks and exposure.

### 2. Increase generation and use of CP-CS in decision-making

The project will focus on increasing the reliability of three sectors, namely agriculture, fisheries and tourism. Additional sectors will also be considered, such as shipping, and the aviation sector in Mauritius and Seychelles. The project will ensure that C-PS is disseminated through mobile phone voice and internet. CP-CS services are provided by ASCENDA. Tailored CP-CS will be developed in partnership with users in these sectors responding to user needs, whilst ensuring use of best communication channels to maximize user outreach. The number of users of the C-PS will increase from 100,000 (current) to 250,000 by the end of the project.

In terms of avoided costs it was estimated to be about $4.1 million for the SWIO region as a whole (see Annex A3). Other benefits include: reduction in climate-related disasters and vulnerabilities; weather bulletin, impact-based forecasts and EWS; as well as the knowledge and capacity building gained from the project.

### 3. Strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced risk exposure

The project will focus on the end-use of the CP-CS to ensure production and dissemination of services is relevant and effective in each sector. The C-PS will be disseminated through mobile phone voice and internet, but will be identified for each target users and CP-CS will be “packaged” based upon their requirements. For example, agrometeorological advisories will be disseminated by mobile phone and radio in Comoros, Madagascar, and Mauritius (Activity 3.1.2). The project will also ensure that the C-PS is used to improve agricultural productivity through an up to 10% increase in agriculture productively. Likewise, with access to reliable climate information, fishermen will be better prepared for extreme weather events such as cyclones, and thus reduce their exposure to these extremes (Activity 3.1.2). Climate-related conditions also impact their ability for fish capture. At the same time, the establishment of a regional climate risk management centre in Mauritius (Activity 3.1.4) will ensure that associated NCI will undergo early warning / early action, and therefore will provide significant socioeconomic and environmental benefits for the main coastal communities (Activities 3.2.2 and 3.2.3). The expected result is a 50% reduction in risk exposure at the end of the project (see Annex A3).


---
**Page 62**
---

# 4. Strengthening preparedness of climate threats and risk-reduction measures

This will be ensured as CP-CS, including MNP-IBF-EWS, are designed with the stakeholders (e.g., PIRDI, national DRM institutions and NGOs) and last mile end-users (local communities), who will be involved in the development, testing, and validation of the tools and products. The project will also ensure that the tools and products are fit for use by all stakeholders (including local communities). This will be done through the involvement of related impacts and responses (Activities 3.2.2 and 3.2.3). These developments and testing will be done in the context of the four pilot areas, which have been selected based on their vulnerability to climate-related hazards (project) using scenarios, and through the dissemination of knowledge products and outreach materials with the assistance of PIRDI and other national agencies. The project will also ensure that the tools and products are fit for use by all stakeholders (including local communities), who will be involved in the development, testing, and validation of the tools and products.

The benefits of the proposed project will span across the following areas: i) receiving direct climate-related early warnings; ii) receiving early alerts and best practices; iii) reducing heavy rainfalls and other climate-related hazards; iv) receiving safety alert and warnings; v) developing and implementing MNP-IBF-EWS; vi) accessing more timely, accurate, and reliable information on climate-related risks and impacts. These benefits will be achieved through the following: i) providing early warning support before, during and after climate-related hazardous events. All together, these benefits will enable climate change adaptation by reducing the impact of climate-related hazards (e.g., heavy rainfalls). The total number of direct beneficiaries is expected to reach 194,532 people in Comoros, 780,600 people in Madagascar, 270,000 people in Mauritius and 96,000 people in Seychelles. The total expected number of combined direct (and indirect) beneficiaries benefiting from reduced vulnerability to climate-related hazards is estimated at 1,453,838 people in Comoros, 2,780,500 people in Madagascar, 1,300,000 people in Mauritius and 96,000 people in Seychelles. Direct beneficiaries are women beneficiaries. Direct project beneficiaries were estimated at 47% of the total population (194,238) and indirect beneficiaries at 33% (n=5,599).

The proportion of direct beneficiaries is based on the delivery of CP-CS to targeted sectors; and based on access to improved forecasts and more timely early warnings via mobile phone, radio and Internet. See Annex A for a detailed explanation on beneficiary calculations.

Total project beneficiaries: 845,671 in Comoros, 270,000 in Madagascar, 1,300,000 in Mauritius, and 96,000 in Seychelles.


* **Direct beneficiaries:** Direct beneficiaries are a proportion of the population with access to communication channels, i.e. radio, mobile phone and/or Internet access (whichever is the highest)11). Comoros: 60% in Comoros (mobile phone subscription) or 587,286 people Madagascar: 65% in Madagascar (radio) or 173,950,000 Mauritius: 40% in Mauritius (mobile phone) or 1,300,000 people Seychelles: in Seychelles (mobile phone)

Total expected direct beneficiaries = 194,532 which is 67% of the total population over the 4 islands; of which at least 50% are women.

* **Indirect beneficiaries:** Indirect beneficiaries are a proportion of the population that does not have access to radio and/or Internet access: 40% in Comoros or 381,198 people; 30% in Madagascar or 9,450,020; 0% in Mauritius and Seychelles. In summary, the proposed investments will contribute to forecast-based early action and therefore, working with partners that are supported by other initiatives and projects, 100% of the population will benefit from the project.
