# D3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project fully supports the implementation of SDGs in the four countries. In particular SDG 2: End hunger; achieve food security and improved nutrition and promote sustainable agriculture; SDG 8: Promote sustained, inclusive and sustainable economic growth, full and productive employment and decent work for all; and SDG 13: Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts. In addition, the project supports the operationalisation of the Sendai Framework Target on Disaster Risk Reduction (SF-DRR) as well as risk-informed sustainable development.

## 1. Environmental co-benefits

The project will reduce the vulnerability of exposed populations and assets to climate change. As people are less affected by climate-related hazards, and their economic activities become more resilient, pressure on the natural environment is reduced. The project will also contribute to the reduction in greenhouse gas emissions through better decision making among farmers and fishermen. Farmers will be able to use adequate crop varieties based on the information provided by the proposed weather station network. This will reduce the need for repeated warnings of hazardous meteoroeorological events. As a result, they will suffer less losses and damages from climate variability and extreme events. In addition, the project will contribute to the reduction in greenhouse gas emissions such as Madagascar and Comoros, unsustainable extraction of wood for charcoal production is a practice that compensates for climate-related agricultural losses. In addition, the design and management of small dams for water harvesting will be improved through the use of appropriate materials and technologies. Dams designed to capture water most efficiently, pressure on the ecosystems for water extraction will be reduced.

## 2. Social co-benefits including health impacts

The project will contribute to improving people’s health and save lives. This will be achieved through strengthening EWS and other climate services such as the production and dissemination of agrometeorological information to improve agricultural productivity supporting food security. The proposed hydromet project will provide information which will help farmers in their decision making, thus reducing the risk of crop failure. In addition, the project will contribute towards vulnerable communities and raise awareness of risk prevention and mitigation measures. With better information on climate variability and extreme events, people can take appropriate actions to protect themselves and assets against potential climate-related disasters. Likewise, fishermen and farmers will be able to move their assets away from areas that are prone to climate-related hazards such as tropical cyclones and associated storm surges. Finally, the project will update climate change adaptation plans, which will in turn improve the quality of life in the four target countries in the long-term.

## 3. Economic co-benefits

Significant economic gains can be expected from the project, both in terms of reduced losses linked to climate-related hazards and increased financial returns/productivity in key economic sectors like agriculture and tourism. The Annual Avoided Losses (AAL) for the four countries are estimated at USD 105 million per year or approximately one-third of the entire SWIO region. The Annual Average Benefit from agro-climatic services have been estimated at USD 29.91 million per year, which is equivalent to a return on investment of about 38% for the four countries and 47% for the two countries. Finally, the Net Present Value (NPV) of the project is estimated at USD 230 million at 5% discount rate. Some of these benefits will be realized in the short-term while others are expected to materialize over a longer period as expected. Moreover, in the long term, revenues from the NIMR’s commercial services can be expected through the marketing of CP-CS, and will progressively complement and partly replace a portion of the public funding of NIMR-commissioned services. The project will also contribute towards the development of the climate change sector in Mauritius along with the collaboration with PROI and its national counterparts will contribute to the implementation of knowledge-based economy. In addition, the project will contribute to the capacity building of the staff at NIMR, PROI, and supported through other initiatives, based on the lessons learned from the first experiment in the region. More information on additional details on the economic and financial benefits of the project, please read Annex 3: Economic Analysis.

## 4. Gender-sensitive development impacts


---
**Page 66**
---

The project will implement a Gender Assessment and Action Plan (see Annex B), which will operationalize gender-responsive adaptation actions through the project results framework. Hence, the gender mainstreaming approach of the Hydromet project will include: mainstreaming gender-responsive approaches for the design of CGS, hydro-meteorological services; building capacities in gender-sensitive data collection and analysis; supporting gender-balanced training and capacity-building activities, as well as institutional development targeted for hydrometeorological networks, tools and systems; ensuring that gender equality is a priority in all project components. The project will also support the design of CGS, hydro-meteorological services; and pioneering gender mainstreaming analyses and praxis regarding hydrometeorology and climate change adaptation in the four countries supported by the project.

### D.A.4. Number of recipients (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The SWIO region has been recognized as a “Climate hotspot” (see Annex C, and section B1). The affected population and physical damages resulting from climate-related hazards are likely to rise in the coming years, in the context of climate change.

Despite different socio-economic contexts – with Comoros and Madagascar classified as LDCs; Mauritius as upper-middle income; and Seychelles as high-income – all four countries share similar vulnerabilities. The islands are extremely vulnerable to climate-related hazards. In Comoros and Madagascar, the population is highly reliant on agriculture and fisheries, two key economic sectors which are severely affected by increased air and sea temperatures, more frequent extreme weather events (e.g., cyclones), and rising sea levels. In Mauritius and Seychelles, the economy is highly depending on tourism, which is also threatened by tropical cyclones, increased occurrence of floods and droughts, and rising sea levels.

These four islands are also characterized by microclimates, which are strongly influenced by the ocean currents and sea surface temperature patterns, associated with the Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD). This makes it all the more difficult to predict climate-related hazards in these countries. The lack of reliable climate information is a major barrier for decision-making processes at national level, as well as at local community levels.

More precisely, there is a lack of location-specific climate and weather information to inform decision-making processes on agriculture, fisheries, tourism, water resources management, and disaster risk reduction measures. This results in vulnerability among communities and sectors, and limited cooperation at the regional level to monitor hazardous hydrometeorological events and share risk-related information in the SWIO countries. This is because of the limited technical capacity for climate monitoring and forecasting, as well as the lack of institutional arrangements that translate users’ needs in terms of CPs (see Annex 2). Improving CPs is crucial to respond to user needs, reduce vulnerability to climate change, and increase resilience at national level. However, this requires substantial investments, which cannot be met by the MRIs of the four target countries: while Comoros and Madagascar are classified as LDCs, Mauritius and Seychelles have limited resources for climate-related investments. The islands are threatened by climate change, with significant public spending already needed to recover from the physical damages resulting from extreme weather events (e.g., cyclones). In addition, there is a lack of institutional arrangements in the four target countries supporting the improvement of CPs as described in section B1 – hence, these interventions are limited to specific areas of a country and not at national level.

Timely, reliable weather, climate and hydrological monitoring, forecasting and service delivery, including EWS, are necessary to equip decision-makers with the adequate information for disaster risk reduction and long-term adaptation. The proposed project will support the development of improved CPs in the four target countries. Recipients will be required not only to equip or upgrade observation networks and foster the production of climate services that meet national needs (e.g., weather, climate, hydrological monitoring and forecasting), but also to improve service processes, and enhanced communication and knowledge sharing at the regional level, through inter ala, the establishment of a Regional Climate Centre (RCC) in Mauritius (as described in section B1.2 – see also the regional scope) which underpins improvements at the national level; hence, the outcomes at the national level. The outcomes of the proposed project will enable decision-making that will enhance livelihoods of the most vulnerable communities and support economic growth and development, increased economic growth rates. In turn, support the production of climate services beyond the project’s lifetime.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 64 OF 119

It should also be noted that the project responds to a call to provide support at scale to NHRIs in the Indian Ocean region launched in 2015 by the African Union (AU) and the WMO. The project concept was developed by the IOC, which received repeated requests and approval from its ministerial council to develop the Hydromet Project as a full-scale demonstration of how to support NHRIs at scale. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with staff from the DRM institutions at national level in the four countries and an regional level with PIRRO, while also involving other relevant stakeholders (e.g., meteorological services, disaster risk management and legal experts [one in each area]) who will be hired to support the implementation of the project in the four countries.

Section B.1 above discusses how the proposed project contributes to the SAMOA path and the AU Agenda 2063. The Hydromet Project is a key component of the SAMOA path, particularly in relation to SDG 7: Ensure access to affordable, reliable, sustainable and modern energy for all. In particular, the project will strengthen risk governance and management in the four target countries, by establishing a climate-resilient hydrometeorological information system (CRIHS) at national level in each country, implement multi-hazard impact-based forecasts and early warning systems (Global Target G1) in support of prevention and preparedness for disasters, including extreme weather events, droughts, floods, landslides, and on-the-ground organisations and communities to better understand and use CP-CCS and contribute to the development of climate-resilient hydrometeorological services. The Hydromet Project will also strengthen DRM in countries, through more accurate and timely detection of climate-related risks, the development or strengthening of early warning systems, the implementation of risk management plans, the development of climate information services, the development of hazard maps and risk management plans, and capacity building interventions targeting the priority groups.

The project will support the implementation of SDG 3 in the four countries, as hydrometeorological and impact-based forecast and early warning services, as they be strengthened under the proposed project, are key enablers for a balanced approach to climate change adaptation and mitigation. The Hydromet Project will also contribute fully supporting the achievement of SDG 2: End hunger; achieve food security and improved nutrition; SDG 5: Achieve gender equality in life and work; and SDG 10: Reduce inequality within and among countries.

The project will support the IOC Member States’ ability to combat climate change and its impacts. As an example, the Hydromet Project will contribute to the implementation of the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy Document cadre pour la stratégie réponse adaptative au changement climatique des pays membres de la Commission de l'océan Indien (COCI) by supporting the development of climate information services and tools for addressing climate-related issues, and improving the coordination of actions. To reach these aims, the strategy advocates strengthening forecasting systems to provide early warning; developing climate risk management plans; and disseminating climate-related information. The proposed Hydromet Project will contribute to achieving these goals as it will develop a CRIHS at national level in each country, implement multi-hazard impact-based forecasts and establish a RCC-Network and a similar risk management culture. Dissemination of CP-CCS will also be improved through the development of climate information services.

With regards to the NDCs, the project will support their achievement in each target country:

* **Comoros:** The priority actions of the NDC target the water and agriculture sectors, aim to integrate climate change considerations into sectoral policies and plans. The Hydromet Project will contribute by better capturing the vulnerabilities of rural communities and farmers to climate change. Capacity and resilience of the agricultural sector in Comoros will be strengthened through the development of climate information services and tools for developing seasonal forecasts and agricultural advisories for Comoros and improving the dissemination of information on climate-related risks, including droughts, floods, landslides and extreme weather events. The priority action of the NDC is the integration of climate change into relevant strategies, which will be supported under activity 3.2 as part of the Hydromet Project.

* **Madagascar:** The priority actions of the NDC target adaptation and mitigation. Several priority measures are identified to support adaptation and reduce GHG emissions in the country, including a Large-scale wind energy project. The Hydromet Project will contribute by developing and disseminating seasonal forecasts and agricultural advisories for Comoros.

### Mauritius

NDC underlines several priority actions to which the Hydromet project will contribute, for example: "Develop and implement climate change adaptation strategies and plans at all levels of government, including management of related governance, and invest in resilience. The Hydromet project will also partly contribute to the NDC objective on water resources by supporting the development of a national water strategy, improving quality of water resources, including upgrading and building of new treatment plants and reservoirs and reducing pollution from urban runoff. Furthermore, the project will support climate change adaptation through enhancement and training of hydrological services i.e. flood mitigation programs under activity 2.3.8 and risk management strategies for coastal areas under activity 2.3.10."

### Seychelles

NDC recommends further research to understand the impacts of a changing climate on "coral reefs, oceans and air currents, and other climate phenomena such as El Niño". The NDC also notes the necessity of developing an early warning system for cyclones and other extreme weather events.

Hydromet project will contribute to this under activity 2.3.8 by developing climate change projections for Seychelles. Furthermore, the Hydromet project will support the development of a national water strategy which will develop hazard maps for tourism revenue under activity 2.3.1. Improved, more accurate forecasts including rainfall and temperature data will be used to improve planning and decision-making in agriculture, fisheries channels for tourism and fisheries under activity 3.1.2, along with early warnings. Finally, under activity 3.2.4, the project will support climate change adaptation strategies and plans at all levels of government, including management of related governance, and invest in resilience.

The Funding Proposal was prepared based on extensive in-country consultations and field visits which took place between April and June 2016. The project team met with representatives from a wide range of stakeholders across sectors, last mile partners (as organizations involved on-the-ground disaster risk management, focused group interventions, community-based adaptation, etc.), civil society organizations, and of women's groups, local governments and sectoral representatives to gather insights into critical interventions (see Annex 7). The four country NDIAs have also been consulted during the project development phase. Moreover, the NDIAs will follow the project implementation through a series of quarterly meetings with the Project Steering Committee and Project Steering Committees and Regional Project Steering Committee (see section 8.4), in particular to ensure that the project is aligned with national priorities for climate change adaptation objectives, and its complementarity and synergies with other existing initiatives in this field.

The project is aligned with several international and national policies and strategies pertaining to climate change adaptation and disaster risk management (the list of which can be found in Annex 2). In a nutshell, these are:

* **Comoros:** The country has submitted its NAPA in 2008, which emphasised the need for "installing and reinforcing effective sectoral early warning systems". It is currently developing its NAP and has submitted its NDC in 2016, which underlines the importance to develop an effective cross-sectoral early warning system. Comoros also faces challenges related to lack of institutional capacity, limited human resources, difficulties delivering services on time, and lack of capacity in the MNRs. Key partners that were consulted are: Department of Fisheries, Agriculture, Environment (acting as sector users), CERVO and COOP-PROD (civil society for disaster risk management and climate change adaptation intervention); and Ulanga NGO and Red Cross/Crescent (civil society for preparedness/emergency relief).

Based on in-country consultations and the case study analysis provided in the FS - an assessment of needs to improve climate services and EWS was undertaken. For example:

* In particular, there is a need for improved coordination of disaster risk management activities at the local level, including through strengthening community capacity and engagement with local communities at the center. For example:

## The Hydromet Project

### Objectives

- **Objective 1:** To build capacity for climate change adaptation in the water sector at national level.
- **Objective 2:** To support the implementation of the National Hydrological Risk Management Strategy (NHRMS) and to ensure that the NHRMS is fully operationalized by the end of the project period.

### Expected Outcomes

1. The Hydromet project will be implemented in a manner that ensures full ownership, sustainability, and replicability.
2. The Hydromet project will contribute significantly to climate change adaptation at national level through the implementation of the NHRMS.
3. The Hydromet project will provide technical support for the development and implementation of the NHRMS.

### Implementation

The Hydromet project, with its inter-linked components, is the most appropriate investment to achieve climate change adaptation objectives. In the absence of GCF funding - based on financial support from AFD and the EU only, respectively - the Hydromet project would not be able to address the enabling environment and frameworks, neither fostering a regional approach, as set out in the NHRMS, nor providing technical assistance for the development and implementation of climate services, for and by the private sector customers which are detailed in Annex A: Economic Analysis and summarised below - would not be realized in the absence of GCF investment.

When considering the Hydromet project as a stand-alone project, a major part of its activities, namely all the infrastructure allowing strengthened climate services delivery and multi-hazard impact-based forecast and early warning systems at national and regional levels. In this scenario, the major benefits from the original Hydromet project would be limited to the following:

- **Improved decision-making:** The Hydromet project will provide a range of climate services based on model-based forecasts of hazard evolution, expected impacts or consequences and precursors for particular vulnerable sectors. These services will be provided in a timely manner through an integrated system of early warning systems (Feasibility Study). It is noted the requirement for strengthening the ability to detect, monitor, forecast, and generate and disseminate climate information and services at national level. The Hydromet project will also benefit from the support of the Indian Ocean Regional Intervention Platform (IPRiO) and its related initiatives and projects. However, in order to ensure a people-centered end-to-end MH-IBF-EWS coordination and collaboration with other stakeholders, it is recommended that the Hydromet project should be implemented as part of the overall implementation of MH-IBF-EWS in pilot areas and rolling out nationwide. Therefore, all benefits of the annual benefit analysis will be realized only if the Hydromet project is implemented as a stand-alone project.

The benefits from the potential private sector will also be affected. The process of developing user-tailored climate services for the private sector will be limited to the extent that there are no funds available about the climate into decision-support tools to suit the needs of specific sector applications, will no longer be possible.

With the proposed Hydromet project, the economic rate of return is estimated to be around 15%. This implies that the project will generate an additional revenue of USD 230 million over the period 2015-2035.

---
**Page 72**
---

The financing structure of the project is essentially based on investment grant for the implementation of the project and co-financing (from Mauritius and Seychelles) for construction work and the operating and maintenance costs. As a result, the financial model will not weigh on the public debt of the targeted countries. Country ownership of operational and maintenance costs is ensured by the fact that these costs are covered in the medium term by strengthening the capacity of the personnel already assigned to operate the various systems at the National Meteorological Services.

Moreover, the private sector of the four countries is not in a position to support the financing of the Hydromet project since most of the project objectives fall under open access public services. In these four countries, the Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) approach is still in its infancy and there are few examples of companies that have some companies are more conscious of the impact they are having on aspects of society, including economic, social and environmental issues. The private sector has no interest to invest in a project that would compete with the production of public services. There is therefore no risk of crowding out the private sector.

The financial viability of the project is based on grant financing of investments. The absence of loan and therefore of obligation to repay the debt, as well as the fact that the project does not increase the public debts of the States are therefore not affected by the project funding.

Finally – importantly - the four countries benefiting from the Hydromet project will be able to build sustainable, long-term capacity in their National Meteorological Services (NMS) and improve their meteorological systems and recover the costs of operation and maintenance (O&M) through revenue-generation / cost-recovery from commercial services, and through the development of new products and services.

The budget funding analysis (see Annex 3a) shows that the impact of the Hydromet project will be easily handled by the four countries together, as the O&M costs of the new Hydromet equipment overall, on the budgets of the four beneficiary countries. The share of public funding for the Hydromet project is expected to vary from 10% in Comoros and Seychelles, as per the ICC-Network multi-lateral agreement signed between these countries under Activity 1.1.2 of the previous phase of the project, to almost 30% (Mauritius) or more than 50% (Comoros), which will replace part of the current public funding of NMMS, from 10% (Comoros and Seychelles) to almost 30% (Mauritius) of their budget. This indicates that the Hydromet project-related interventions may no more rely on international funding in the long term.

# E.5. Progress monitoring and performance indicators

The performance indicators for progress reporting and implementation should seek to measure pre-existing conditions, progress and results at the most relevant level of scale (GCF monitoring and AE reporting).

## Expected Results

| Indicator | Baseline | Target |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1.1 Established National Hydrometric Network with supporting O&M of NRMSs | No NRCC or NRMS in the SMMO region | 1 RCC-Network (designated by WMO) |
| 1.2 Upgraded and improved hydrometric network to monitor climate-related water resources, including flood monitoring capabilities | No National Hydrometric Network prepared with mechanisms for supporting O&M of NRMSs | Adapted National Hydrometric Network with mechanisms for supporting O&M of NRMSs |

## Assumptions

* Partner countries agree on a strategy and implementation plan to support the functions of the RCC-Network, and to ensure that the network is aligned with their priority plans.
* The upgraded and expansion of the existing hydrometric network will provide adequate infrastructure to generate profit-and-revenue generating forecasts.
* Government committed to secure adequate O&M for the data management system on a continuous basis during project implementation and afterwards.

* Full Systems integration through a centralized platform will secure related capacity to monitor climate-related water resources.

This document is a proposal for funding from the Green Climate Fund (GCF). It outlines how the GCF will identify and support the establishment of the Regional Climate Change Network (RCC-Network) in the region. The RCC-Network strategy will define its purpose, goals, scope, and the operational activities to be carried out by the network.

The proposal includes:

- **Administrative and logistical responsibilities**: These will lie with the GCF, under the auspices of the Secretariat.
- **IOC's role**: This will include setting up a regional expert team composed of selected hydro-meteorological experts from member states.
- **Operational activities**: These are divided into three categories:
  - Long-term strategic planning (LSP) for operational activities to promote the use of operational RCC products and services in the region.
  - Additional use of RCC products and services beyond the scope of the LSP, including climate adaptation (beyond two years' timeframe), research and development, and capacity building.
- **Supporting entities**: The proposal mentions the involvement of WMO, IIOC, and other stakeholders.

The document also discusses how the network will be funded through national contributions and sub-activity funding. It outlines a business model to ensure financial sustainability for the RCC-Network during its participation in GCF activities.

1.2.3 Review and/or update existing legal regulatory materials, including potential establishment of a CIEWS fund

The national legal instrument establishing a National Meteorological and Hydrological Service (NMHS) should be reviewed and updated to ensure its operation. It helps to define its mission and mandate, ensures clarity in the roles and responsibilities of the NMHS and other organizations that have responsibilities, gain recognition of its contribution to society, and facilitate harmonization with international agreements.

The legal framework for CIEWS funds is also important as it defines the obligations under various international agreements, including the WMO. Rogers et al (2018)39 emphasize that:

(1) National institutional frameworks should be established to clearly define the roles and responsibilities of the NMHS and other organizations which are part of the national and local governments, and to enhance collaboration with stakeholders.

Under this activity, each country will review its legal and regulatory framework for meteorological services. This will include mechanisms to establish a CIEWS fund. Based on this analysis, each country will develop or revise their respective legal and regulatory materials.


---
**Page 90**
---

(2) To facilitate effective early warning services, it is important to establish a national framework that includes a legal and institutional framework for such services, along with efficient communication and dissemination mechanisms for timely delivery of information.

### Policy, legal, and institutional frameworks help build government commitment and accountability

- **Policy**: Defines the overall direction and objectives.
- **Legal**: Establishes the rules and regulations.
- **Institutional**: Sets up the structures and processes to implement policies and laws.

### Responsibilities of NMHDs
- **Leader**: IOC with the Support of PMU expertise to lead the development of the CERSW.
- **Stakeholders involved in the process**:
  - Beneficiary supervision and validation: NMHD in each country.
  - Government representatives from relevant ministries, public sector entities, and private sectors, NGOs, UN Agencies and development partners involved in each country.

### Steps to be taken by the NMHDs
1. Review and revise the Hydromet Law of each target country to allow consistent implementation of the CERSW.
2. Define the legal framework for the Trust Fund (the CERSW) with clear definition of its grants, and:
3. Develop a detailed work plan that defines the components and structure, including: (a) defining the programmatic and financial management arrangements; (b) identifying the specific sources of funds linking with the objectives – this would require discussions with stakeholders to identify potential funding sources; (c) establishing an effective governance – typically a Steering Committee is established with clear Terms of Reference, which includes the Chairperson, usually the Director-General of the NMHDs (who usually acts as the Secretary), and with the role of providing overall direction for the CERSW, including industry and implementing arrangements; and (d) establishing monitoring, reporting and evaluation processes.

## As indicated above, the system integration will ensure functional compatibility of the modernized meteorological and hydrological systems with the global and regional climate models and other relevant systems that are to be installed at each NMRH.

This activity includes the design of an optimum composite surface observation network and associated ICT systems for the SWO region which will be aligned with the GCMs, as well as the development of step-by-step practices and procedures for the installation and coordination of project activities in all countries. The stations in target countries, in terms of type, number and location of the station, will be selected based on the requirements of the climate models and climate analysis.

This activity includes the technical assistance required to fine-tune the specifications of the networks and technology systems, oversee their installation and commissioning, as well as provide training for NMRH staff. 

### 1.4.3 Ensures full implementation of the system integration from observation network design of CP/CS to end-user

This activity includes the development of step-by-step practices and procedures for the installation and coordination of project activities in all countries.

The observation and monitoring network and remote sensing equipment will be modernized completely with harmonized equipment for all NMRHs to facilitate their use by the GCMs.

In addition, ICT systems will be upgraded following the SWO VMS technical regulation (see Annex 2.1). The ICT systems of different manufacturers use different dialogue software and protocols which makes it difficult to interconnect them in a single management system. This activity includes the development of the network and ICT systems for all NMRHs, taking into account innovative techniques and technologies.

### 1.4.3.1 Conduct capacity gap analysis of ICT system in each country

1.4.3.2 Design the proper technical integration of the different existing networks and ICT systems (including those provided by previous activity to make the overall system as efficient as possible, taking into account the various types of equipment, their service life and exchange, and protocols and standardization issues) through the development of a harmonized network and ICT system for all NMRHs.

1.4.3.3 Implement the data integration "architecture" through the development of a harmonized network and ICT system for all NMRHs, taking into innovative techniques and technologies.

Leader: IOC with the Support of PMU expertise to:

## Stakeholders to be involved and consulted: NMHS in each country

In each country, modernize/upgrade the networks based on the detail plan and specifications produced under output 1.4.

### 2.1.1 In Comoros:

- Modernization of the building where the National Meteorological Service is located.
- Upgrade the national synoptic network (NWS) in food-prone areas, including sub-synoptic networks in flood-prone areas and lightning detection systems.
- Doppler radar for Moroni.
- Weather balloon system.
- ASOS, meteorologico-buoyant towers. Upper air radiosounding.

### 2.1.2 In Madagascar:

- Modernize national meteorological stations following the Feasibility Study recommendations – Annex A and B.
- Upgrade the national synoptic network (NWS) in food-prone areas, including sub-synoptic networks in flood-prone areas and lightning detection systems.
- Doppler radar for Toamasina.
- Weather balloon system. Upper air radiosounding.

### 2.1.3 In Mauritius:

- Modernize national meteorological stations following the Feasibility Study recommendations – Annex A and B.
- Upgrade the national synoptic network (NWS) in food-prone areas (Port-Louis). Weather radar for North Maurice, South Maurice, Rodrigues, Agalega and Brandon.
- Lightning detection system. Upper air radiosounding system for Agalega; Saint Brandon and Rodrigues Doppler radar under output 4.2.

### 2.1.4 In Seychelles:

- Modernize national meteorological stations following HYCOS recommendations, sub-synoptic networks in food-prone areas (Praslin and La Digue). Weather radar for Mahe, Praslin and La Digue. Tode gauge for the island of La Digue.
- Lightning detection system. Upper air radiosounding systems for Mahe, Praslin and La Digue. Doppler radar for Mahe. Tower for radar.

## Stakeholders to be involved and consulted: NRHS in each country

### Trained staff of NRHS remunerated and authorized for NWP-IFS

#### 2.3.1 Downscale and calibrate numerical weather forecasts by making use of the high-resolution numerical weather prediction (NWP) models and applying advanced techniques such as artificial intelligence
This activity will serve to improve the production of short-range, day-to-day forecasts and warnings in each target country (see Activity 3.1.1). Moreover, it will contribute to improving the accuracy of probabilistic forecasts and on its use to produce multi-hazard impact-based early warning systems (MHIWS) for NRHS in each country with support from the government. Both daily forecasts and MHI-SF will benefit from high-quality data assimilation and numerical weather prediction models developed under Activity 2.1.1 and 2.1.2. This activity is aligned with the strategy to improve National Weather and Climate Prediction developed under Activity 1.1.4.
- Conduct objective forecast verification and develop a methodology for verifying probabilistic forecasts based on observed data in the SWIO region, using modern techniques
- Conduct NWP-IFS aligned with the strategy to improve Regional Numerical Weather and Climate Prediction developed under Activity 1.1.4
- Conduct training using e.g., Artificial Intelligence techniques (deep learning) to adapt the numerical weather prediction models to the local conditions by observing them with observing data and in situ data analysis capability
- Conduct a training in probabilistic forecasts and ensemble prediction systems, so that NRHS can predict their weather will do rather than only what the weather will be

### Responsible parties:
Local IOC with the Support of PMI expertise to work with relevant experts (e.g., existing staff of the broadcast media, meteorologists, etc.) and other training facilities in the region
- Training provided by: National Meteorological Services in each country
- Training provided by: Regional Meteorological Services and NRHS institutions (or BIF) in each country

#### 2.3.2 Establish threshold values for extreme weather events based on extreme value analysis of historical data
Technical analysis using Extreme analysis tools and climate data and review of the historical hydro-meteorological events that caused flooding and other extreme weather events, to establish threshold values for each hazard for this country. This would have to be harmonized at the national level for effective MHI-SF EWS at regional, national and local levels
- This is an activity carried out in collaboration with NRHSs and NWP-IFS institutions and other stakeholders (including NGOs) to agree on national threshold values for issuing warnings based on extreme value analysis

## 2.3.4 Develop vulnerability maps
### 2.3.4.1 Develop vulnerability assessments of climate-related vulnerability and shifts in the context of climate change for areas most affected by selected hazards in each country.
Based on the hazard maps produced under activity 2.3.3, a mapping exercise will be conducted in a participatory way with relevant stakeholders (scientific experts, public and private sectors representing agriculture/fisheries, tourism, health, water resources, etc.) to identify vulnerable assets and most vulnerable people affected by selected hazards (selected under activity 2.3.1) in each country. The mapping process will draw on local experience and knowledge of the communities concerned.
The exercise will also contribute to awareness-raising among diverse stakeholders and provide a basis for developing adaptation strategies and policies.

## 2.3 Build the regional downscale climate models for national purposes

Regional downscaling and national calibration of the different climate models used by the four countries will be done in close collaboration with the National Meteorological Services. The production of downscaled climate models is based on the use of high-resolution regional climate models, methodologies. Based on the downscaled climate change projections, impact assessments will be carried out at the sectoral level using the existing climate change models and projections as well as the sectoral models and impact assessment tools developed under the BRIQ.

The implementation of these downscaled and sectoral models will be done at national level under component 3.

### 2.3.2 Share downscaled forecasts and CC projections on the online platform

1. Align with the strategy to improve National Numerical Weather Prediction (NNWP) systems under component 1.4.

3. Be involved in the production of downscaled climate models by IOC, and includes the participation of international experts as well as the involvement of the National Meteorological Services in countries. This activity also includes individual tasks such as capacity building for the four countries.

The implementation of these downscaled and sectoral models will be done at national level under component 3.

# I.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements will comply with the relevant GCF policies.

## Reporting to the PMU

The EE, through its PMU, will ensure the day-to-day monitoring and overseeing project implementation, ensuring compliance with partners including NPCs, MFPs, NMRFs and MOAs, exchanging with local government etc. Every semester, the PMU will review project performance and assess any potential risks. This task will be performed by the Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Officer member of the PMU, with the assistance of the four national Project Fiscal Provinces (PFPs). The M&E Officer will prepare a semi-annual report on progress towards achieving the project objectives. The PMU will liaise continuously with the RPC. At the national level project implementation will be prepared by the NPCs as an annual report to the AE, and at the provincial level by the PFPs. This report will provide information on progress against the project objectives. At the end of the disbursement period, the EE will prepare a project completion report summarizing the main events of the implementation phase, the results achieved, and the lessons learned. This report will also provide recommendations for future projects.

## Reporting to the AE

During the disbursement period, AFD will receive reports from the EE, i.e., the LOC, to track progress and identify potential risks as well as improvement opportunities. In this document, the EE will report on a semi-annual or annual basis on performance indicators; these indicators will be detailed in a resulting monitoring framework developed to assess project implementation against the Project Development Document (PDD), while the Indicators will monitor the progress of each component over the lifetime of the Project.

Accounting and financial audits will also be conducted every year by an independent third party. AFD will report and communicate with the Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation (MRE) Officer to ensure that the lessons learned from the project after its full termination (at a terminal evaluation (TE)). The MTR and TE aim to improve future project implementation as well as other project programming by deriving lessons from experience in order to find ways to make aid more effective.

## Reporting to GCF

Annual Performance Reports (APR) will be duly prepared by AFD and shared in a manner compliant with the provisions of the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) and the Monitoring and Accountability Framework (MAF). The APR will provide an overview of project implementation progress based on the logical framework, considerations on the ongoing performance of the proposed project, updates on the indicators, and a report on environmental and social safeguards (ESSGs) as well as gender issues.

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

F.1 Risk factor and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

| Selected Risk Factor | Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational/Technical and operational | Medium/Medium | High |

Description

Project implementation could be affected by the limited capacity of NMRFs to operationalise the new/upgraded hydro-meteorological infrastructure. This risk is also relevant for the SWIO region, where NMRFs may not have appropriate experts to operationalise the equipment and tools, or do not provide adequate training to their staff members; ii) Staff members of the NMRFs are not involved in project implementation; or iii) Operation and maintenance costs during and after project completion.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

These risks will be mitigated through the following project interventions: i) the signature of a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with the SWIO EE to ensure that the NMRFs are committed to cooperation, collaboration and commitment; ii) institutional and technical capacity of beneficiary NMRFs will be strengthened to improve their operationalisation and retain skilled staff; staff will also be trained to operationalise equipment and tools, and new staff will be recruited as needed; iii) a training programme for NMRF staff will be designed in consultation with the NMRFs of each beneficiary country, which will be involved in project implementation planning and monitoring; iv) the SWIO EE will ensure that the NMRFs are adequately resourced to implement and involvement of these services during project implementation. The NMRFs will also be represented during the annual meetings of the SWIO EE to ensure their views on project implementation are heard (participation, input and engagement); and iv) modernisation of key equipment and tools, and training on their operation and maintenance will support increased capacity for project implementation.

Selected Risk Factor | Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | High |

Description

The proposed project could be affected by limited commitment of the government towards project implementation, for example, new appointments in the government – ministries or NMRFs management - could affect project implementation in the country.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

The risk is more significant in policy-inclined countries and/or to government re-shuffle. To ensure strong involvement of the beneficiary countries, extensive in-country consultations – with representatives of the NMRFs, relevant government ministries and other stakeholders – will be held at national level. In addition, workshops were held at national and regional levels) to provide opportunities for countries to comment and adjust requirements. As such, expectations from NMRFs and CS-C users have been incorporated where relevant to ensure greater buy-in and commitment towards project implementation.

The IOC will sign bilateral agreements with each Ministry of Foreign Affairs, which, in turn, will sign agreements with the

## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

### Description

Security issues and vandalism put hydro-meteorological equipment at risk of damage or loss. Tensions with the population located where the new hydro-meteorological equipment will be installed could result in costly population relocation, which is necessary if this results in a displaced population to the equipment.

## Mitigation Measures (4)

Several actions will be undertaken to avoid relocating population, engage with the population located nearby hydro-meteorological equipment and ensure the equipment is not damaged during and after effect of extreme weather events:

- No population displacement will occur as the new equipment will be installed in upland sites already used by such equipment and building extension/construction (for the MRNPs in Comoros and Seychelles) will take place at least 100 meters away from the existing equipment.
- Communities surrounding the newly established/ upgraded equipment will be informed beforehand of the purpose of the project and type of equipment to be installed.

## Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

### Description

There is a risk that climate-related hazards will damage the hydro-meteorological equipment during and after the project lifetime. For example, tropical cyclones and storms could destroy automatic weather stations and communication systems.

## Mitigation Measures (4)

The four beneficiary countries are affected by climate-related hazards which could cause damages to the newly established/ upgraded equipment. The risk of this will be mitigated by installing new equipment in e.g., avoiding low-land areas affected by floods. This will, for example, be achieved by ensuring the new equipment is similar location of previously set equipment if this location is not known for being affected by climate-related hazards and also by ensuring that the new equipment is designed to withstand extreme weather events. The new equipment should be efficient design to be resilient to severe and frequent tropical cyclone events, floods, high winds etc (future-proof). Furthermore, risk assessments will enable the NMRDs to protect or remove movable equipment when a warning is issued.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

## Description

There is a risk that innovative technology and techniques compromise the end result in terms of quality of observations and forecasts, as well as operations and maintenance, due to limited expertise.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

While the project will promote the use of innovative technology and techniques, observation systems and downscaling of forecasts, only well established and proven technology and techniques will be used; and therefore particular attention will be given to the development of the technical specifications and terms of reference to ensure, as well as to establish a monitoring plan to ensure that they meet this requirement.

## Selected Risk Factor 6

### Category: Governance
#### Probability: Medium
#### Impact: High

The proposed project could be affected by limited commitment of the government towards supporting continuously the required financial and human resources required for GCFM.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

As described in the GCFM Plan (Annex 21), efforts will be made throughout the project implementation to ensure that sustainable funding and human resource requirements are met. Implementation of the project and beyond project implementation, by applying the following mechanisms to support GCFM in the four countries:

(a) Introducing value-added services through cost recovery of meteorological services and commercial services.
(b) Promoting private sector involvement in the provision of meteorological services.
(c) Engaging with the UN and other international partners working in the four countries.
(d) Establishing business plans and revising the Hydromet Laws in each of the four countries for revenue generation and license fee.

(e) Leveraging funds from the SOFF-GCFM.

In summary, as for Mauritius and Seychelles, in order to complement government funding, the strengthening and enabling of the institutional framework and policy will be essential to achieve the availability and sustainability of the GCFM. The project will also contribute towards the development of a sustainable revenue generation model for meteorological services, as described in the GCFM Plan – revenue generation (Annex 21, pages 2 to 6). Such an improvement will enable the four countries to generate sufficient revenue to cover their operational costs and to support the SOFF-GCFM. A Commitment Letter from IOC, confirming country commitment post-project implementation period to operate and maintain the equipment, is attached in the project package as Annex II_IOC_commitment_letter. Letters are also being sent to the WMO and other international partners for their support.

It should be noted that the proposed project will not only benefit Mauritius and Seychelles but also Comoros. The project is considering a more secure alternative based on the mobilization of funds from the SOFF-GCFM in order to support the implementation of the project in Comoros, as well as Madagascar. In this regard, it should be noted that Mauritius, Seychelles, LDCs, Comoros and Madagascar are eligible to get such support. Thus, a request has already been submitted to the WMO, as a project technical partner, to consider these countries for the SOFF implementation, and a letter is being sent to the WMO requesting their support in this regard.

Additionally, agreements with ASECNA (see GCFM Plan, Annex 21, page 4) are being negotiated to extend the coverage of the meteorological services provided by the project beyond Mauritius and Seychelles and Madagascar; additional agreements with ASECNA (see GCFM Plan, Annex 21, page 4) is being negotiated to extend the coverage of the meteorological services provided by the project beyond Mauritius and Seychelles and Madagascar. Additional agreements under the ASECNA umbrella. It is recalled that IOC is a long-standing partner of ASECNA and has recently signed an agreement with ASECNA for the provision of meteorological services in the region.

Additionally, the PRIO (Red Cross network) in the IO region, which is already involved in the IOC project generally and ECA actions during extreme events, will play a crucial role in the implementation of the term-of-works and task list connectivity by ensuring that critical early warning information is delivered to a timely and efficient manner.

### GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

#### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The proposal for Hydromet project is predominantly for capacity building and institutional strengthening to deliver improved climate services.

There will be an associated provision of new hydrometeorological equipment and tools for monitoring and analysis of meteorological data in the form of automatic weather stations (AWSs) and Doppler radar units. The AWSs are intended to provide real-time meteorological and hydrological data to be analysed and disseminated to government services and end users.

### Environmental and social risks

As evaluated in the assessment, the project is expected to have minimal environmental and social impacts and is not likely to result in any significant adverse effects. The project has been classified overall as Category B. The safeguards risks are primarily associated with the installation of new meteorological equipment mounted on masts (e.g., new or replacement AWSs) and Doppler radar units, including the construction of new buildings and/or major structural alterations to existing buildings (also supplied will be work stations, computers, wiring, etc.) and installation of hydrological stations.

AWSs are typically installed within local government-owned properties. A very limited area is required for each weather station and no land acquisition or resettlement of local population is anticipated as the equipment will be installed on existing sites owned by local governments or research institutes. With the exception of river gauges, the equipment will be installed almost exclusively at existing stations.

It is anticipated that the Doppler radar units will be mounted on a base of 10 meters (33 feet) above ground level and will be located in areas where there are no residential buildings within 500 meters. The Seychelles government has relocated their main offices at their own expense and these will receive coverage from the Doppler radar unit.

In each of the countries it is anticipated that one Doppler Radar unit will be installed and these will be mounted on a base of a tower (up to some six meters in height). There will also be a small number of wave monitoring buoys and tidal gauges. The equipment will be installed at existing sites owned by local governments or research institutes, where no land acquisition is required.

The ESIFM all local permitting requirements will be adhered to and the PMU will facilitate and ensure such regulations are respected in each country. No resettlement of local population is anticipated as the equipment will be installed on existing sites.

Negative environmental and social impacts which would potentially occur from the installation works of AWSs include noise pollution from machinery and equipment, increase in generation of solid waste, and increase in dust emissions due to construction activities. The project will not result in any significant adverse effects on human health or safety as it will affect, albeit temporarily, occupational health and safety of workers to be involved during construction works and subsequent maintenance.

The ESIFM (ESAF framework) has examined all such potential impacts and suggests negligible potential impacts if basic codes of good health and safety practice are followed by contractors, including national and international suppliers. The project is not expected to result in any significant adverse effects on human health or safety due to potential physical impact and safeguards risk is not necessarily linked. The new physical infrastructure element at any location will be very small and is dispersed in siting so cumulative issues are not a relevant consideration.

### Environmental Management

The process of optimal site selection for facilities is addressed together with health and safety guidance for construction. Planning requirements for Doppler Radar installations and the wave height monitoring buoys will be subject to local permitting requirements. The project will also include measures to protect against third party damage. A 'no-for-purpose' approach is adopted in line with ESIFM (ESAF framework) guidelines.

The project will not result in any significant adverse effects on human health or safety due to potential physical impact and safeguards risk is not necessarily linked. The new physical infrastructure element at any location will be very small and is dispersed in siting so cumulative issues are not a relevant consideration.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 114 OF 119

## Gender Assessment and action plan (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The proposed GCF project includes a Gender Assessment and Action Plan (Annex B).

The analytical perspectives of the Gender Assessment and Action Plan are informed by both GCF and AFD gender policies, as well as the UN Women’s Global Strategy for Gender Equality (2014 edition)***and, the AFID Strategy on Gender and the Reduction of Gender Inequalities (2014), as well as its AFD Group Strategy on Gender Equality (2015-2020). The four countries’ national gender equality strategies are available in each of the four countries. These plans were reviewed along with other gender-related legislations such as the National Action Plan for Climate Change Adaptation, the National Water Act and others like the CEDAW and Maputo protocols. These serve to point out the national legal and institutional frameworks and the gender priorities of each country, which can be used to support gender equality through the proposed project.

The assessment is based on a desk review (alongside a regional approach). The assessment is based on rapid in-country missions as well as desk-review of available secondary literature and data. Particularly, the assessment identifies key gender-related factors that determine women’s vulnerability and exposure to climate change impacts and how these factors affect project benefits towards end-users of C-CS (most particularly with vulnerable communities).

Semi-structured interviews (with focus groups, and institutional representatives) were conducted during the in-country missions. The interviews focused on issues related to gender equality on climate change adaptation, climate services and hydro-meteorological services. The triangulated assessment was supported by data from project countries, taking into consideration their unique contexts and performances and across social indicators.

Gender-differentiated climate impacts and natural disasters: The World Economic Forum (WEF’s) report of the Global Risks Report 2020 finds that it is the top five among the primary risk factors facing the world population are:

1. **GCF** - Gender Climate Fund (2016), Updated Gender Policy and Action Plan, Policy Document.
   - Accessed: 22 April 2019. Available at: https://www.greenclimatefund.org/sites/default/files/atoms/files/GCF%20Gender%20Policy%20and%20Action%20Plan.pdf

2. **AFID** - Agence Française de Développement (2014), Strategy on Gender and the Reduction of Gender Inequalities.

3. **UN Women** - Global Strategy for Gender Equality 2014.

4. **CEDAW** - Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women.

5. **Maputo Protocol** - National Action Plan for Climate Change Adaptation, National Water Act, Madagascar: National Policy for the Promotion of Women (2006 – 2015), Mauritius: National Gender Policy (2014), Seychelles: National Gender Policy (2018)

**Page 118**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (V2.0) | PAGE 115 OF 119

environmental changes (in order of risk) extreme weather, climate action failure, natural disasters, biodiversity loss, human-made environmental disasters. Gender determinants such as consumption patterns and incidence of poverty, access to and control of resources and power, time-use patterns and economic activities when juxtaposed with the above-mentioned risks, have a significant bearing on the vulnerability of women in all four IOC member countries. Project countries. High burden of adverse impacts of climate change, especially with the lack of EWS, CS, and hydrometric data, which are critical for early warning systems and adaptation planning. Gender equality is an important component of climate resilience and gender equality has been achieved to some extent in all four IOC member countries. However, there still exists a gap between the legal framework and implementation. The project will address this gap and undo progress made towards gender equality in the island nations (with increased consequences especially in LDC contexts of Madagascar and Comoros).

### Gender-sensitive adaptation solutions: Site visits undertaken in the Comoros (Moroni) and Seychelles (La Digue, Praslin and Victoria) and engagement with government officials in all four countries revealed that adaptation solutions are gender-sensitive. The following are some of the key findings:

- **Hydrometric data:** Limited availability of hydrometric data is a major constraint to effective operationalisation of the Hydrometric project. It is important to consider that all rivers are not often allowed to access public spaces (where EWS may be announced – such as mosques in Comoros) or have limited to no radio access (in Madagascar). This means that women and girls, who are more likely to be responsible for water collection, are often controlled by men.

### Gendered limitations in decision-making institutions and representation: Decision-making institutions, power sharing arrangements, and overall representation tend to be skewed towards men in all four IOC member countries, excepting Seychelles. Yet, at the same time, women make important household decisions daily, in these countries. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation, disaster response, resilience during hazardous hydrometeorological events, and adaptation capacities. An adequate representation of women in decision-making institutions is critical for ensuring that policies and programs are tailored to specific needs and interests of vulnerable groups at decentralised and grassroots levels. Bottom-up, tailored approach will ensure project ownership, and sustainability at the community context.

### Background: The communities in the four IOC member countries have a long history of sustainable use of natural resources. The people of the SWD possess experience, history and knowledge in the management and use of natural resources as sustainable livelihood sources. Thus, self-help groups are formed to manage these resources. However, there is a lack of institutionalised access to resources and communal control of wastage during crises; building strategies – can be traced among these communities. The project, especially due to its focus on building resilience of communities, as well as ecosystems, will provide an opportunity for women to participate in decision-making processes related to climate change responses. Such a process will require vetting as well as emphasis on cross-community dialogue to tailor these provisions, considering the specific needs and interests of vulnerable groups.

Additionally, Annex 8 gives consideration to gender-based violence and time poverty12 experienced by women and men (in some contexts), and how these phenomena can also constrain the adoption of adaptation solutions, particularly in rural areas. In order to address the above-mentioned gender limitations, particularity women's participation in adaptation solutions, and to build on the potential opportunities of increased gender equality through the objectives and outputs of the project.

Key actions included in the plan are presented in the table below per project component:

| Project Component | Gender Action Point |
| --- | --- |
| Capacity Building, Institutional Development and Regional Cooperation | Include gender-responsive policy inputs in national and regional development frameworks developed for climate services (with reference to WMO Gender Strategy) |

134 An individual is time poor if he/she is working long hours and is also monetarily poor, or would fall into monetary poverty if he/she were to reduce his/her working hours by a given time period (e.g. 5 hours). ## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### Contracting Authority

The Implementing Trustee Master Agreement sets that forth, among others, the general terms and conditions between the Parties in connection with Funded Activity. AFD will enter into Funded Activity Agreement with the Government of each beneficiary country (the “EE”) and a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with the Project Agreement (SA) with the IOC as the Executive Entity, representing the beneficiary countries. This SA and its related documents will specify the mandates, duties and engagements related to IOC and related to national counterparts towards the implementation of the project.

For the implementation of the Project, the IOC (EE) will enter into contractual agreements with each of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MoFA) of beneficiary countries which. The Contractual arrangements can take the shape of a “Project Agreement” or a “Memorandum of Understanding”.

The contractual agreements to be established between the EE (IOC) and each of the four countries shall designate the NMHRSs, as lead implementation entities, in each of the four target countries, wherein all the activities and funds allocated under the Project will be implemented.

At National level, each MoFA will also sign an agreement with the Ministry of the National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHS) of each country, the Ministry of Environment, Forestry and Biodiversity and Commons, and other Disaster Risk Management Institutions, and each entity considered as national counterpart for implementing the project context. These agreements aim at clarifying the mandates, roles and commitments of each party.

### Procurement Rules

AFD’s Procurement rules are in line with international standards provided by the World Bank Group. The AFD Procurement guidelines will apply to the EE, in charge of implementing the Hydromet project. For a detailed presentation on the procurement process, see the Procurement Guidelines for AFD Financial Contracts in Foreign Countries available on AFD’s website.

[https://www.afd.fr/sites/default/files/2017-06/guide-des-procurements-engles-anglais-version.pdf](https://www.afd.fr/sites/default/files/2017-06/guide-des-procurements-engles-anglais-version.pdf)

All procurements will be carried out by the EE (IOC) and based on a procurement plan. The procurement process for the EE, will be supported by the Project Management Unit. PMU will be mandated to (i) define the scope of works, (ii) prepare the technical specifications, (iii) organize the tendering procedure, (iv) mobilization of qualified experts, procure, and assess the bids under the bids under EE supervision (it is supervisory in implementation). The EE will be the unique signatory of services and equipment contracts.

AFD supervision of the project

# GEF project implementation

1. Prior to the signing of the Contractual agreement between EE and MoFA and contractual agreement between MoFA and National line ministries or beneficiary entities,
2. On every step of the annual reporting Progress Reports, and on annual technical and financial work plan prepared by the PMU.
3. On every step of a tendering process according to its own procedures.

## Annual supervision missions will be placed, including mobilization of headquarters expertise. Environmental, social and gender equality issues could be assigned to the GEF EMP and Gender Action Plan.

### Financial Management

The financial management of the GCF resources allocated for this project will be conducted in the Framework Agreement (hereinafter referred as FAG) and MoFA. Resources will be provided by GCF to the FAG specifying among others: 1) the amounts already committed and disbursed by AGF to the EE; 2) the remaining amount of the current period, which is calculated based on the approved project implementation plan.

The Annual Performance Report (APR) will be submitted to GCF following the reporting requirements detailed in the FAG. These reports will include a narrative on the implementation progress based on the logical framework, considerations on the ongoing performance and any other relevant information. The first APR will be submitted within six months of the start date of the project and thereafter every year by 31 December.

The last APR for each period will be submitted to the Secretariat on the 30th day following the end of the relevant reporting period, i.e., 30 June or 30 September. The final APR will be submitted within six months from the end of the relevant reporting period.

The FAG provides that the GCF shall review and approve the project implementation plan at mid-term and upon completion of the project implementation end date. Mid-term and final independent evaluation of the project will be organised.


## Accounting and disbursements

The sub-agreement to be signed between AFD and GCF, will establish the terms and conditions of the cooperation agreement and responsibilities for both parties. The projects AFD, EE and GCF funds will be directly and exclusively managed by the EE (IOCO), through a dedicated project account to be open and managed by IOCO. No direct management or control shall be exercised over these funds by any other party.

The AE (AFD) is free to contract for works, supply of goods or services. There will be no direct management of the fund by any other party than EE.

Payments in the form of advances, except for the first advance, will be made under the following conditions:

- The result of the audit carried out on the previous advance is satisfactory to the AFD;
- These audits are carried out by an independent audit firm selected by IOCO after the AFD has given notice of no objection.

As the AE, AFD will disburse funding received from the GCF and the EE. AFD must open a separate bank account for this project; these accounts transparently regarding financial transactions and renders auditors more effective. The EE, as the main contractor, is responsible for all payments to suppliers and sub-contractors. All advance payments will be made by AFD on the request of IOCO based on the issuance of activity reports and according to the disbursement schedule of the contract. The advance payments branches will be used for:

i) payment of PMU staff members and PMU set-up and running costs (100,000 EUR);
ii) payment of PMU staff members, and PMU set-up and running costs (100,000 EUR);
iii) direct payments of goods and services with an amount superior to thousands euros (100,000 EUR); direct payments will be covered by AFD.

For all contract provides of goods and services with an amount superior to thousands euros (100,000 EUR), direct payments will be covered by AFD.

As an IO is in the process of acquiring GCF accreditation, it will benefit from capacity development in the project in which it is engaged.

During the disbursement period, AEF will receive semi-annual reports from IFC on implementation to track progress and identify any issues or areas for improvement. The IFC will liaise with AEF every quarter to provide a report on project implementation. AEF will also be represented at the Project Regional Steering Committee organized every six months by the project partners. The independent auditor will review the disbursement of funds continuously over the correct implementation of the project. Accounting and financial audits shall be conducted by an independent third party. At the end of the disbursement period, AEF will prepare a project completion report summarizing the project's achievements, the results achieved, and the lessons learned. This report will also provide recommendations for future improvement.

- **Annex 3**: Economic and/or financial analyses in spreadsheet format
- **Annex 4**: Detailed budgeted plan (template provided)
- **Annex 5**: Implementation timeline including key project/programme milestones (template provided)
- **Annex 6**: Description of the project, indicating its E&S category (A, B or C; or I1, II2 or III) and E&S disclosure form provided
    - Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) or
    - Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS)
    - Office of Indigenous Peoples’ Affairs (OIPA), Resettlement Policy Framework, Indigenous People’s Plan, Land Acquisition Plan, etc.)

- **Annex 8**: Detailed project/programme implementation plan (template provided)
- **Annex 9**: Legal due diligence (regulation, taxation and insurance)
- **Annex 10**: Procurement plan (template provided)
- **Annex 11**: Monitoring and evaluation plan (template provided)
- **Annex 12**: AE fee request (template provided)

- **Annex 14**: Term sheet including a detailed disbursement schedule and, if applicable, repayment schedule

- **Annex 16**: Maps(s) indicating the location of proposed interventions

- **Annex 19**: Procedures for controlling procurement by third parties or executing entities undertaking projects
- **Annex 20**: First level AML/CFT (KYC) assessment
- **Annex 21**: Operations manual (Operations and maintenance)
