Secretariat’s assessment of FP161

Proposal name: Building Regional Resilience through Strengthened Meteorological, Hydrological and Climate Services in the Indian Ocean Commission (IOC) Member Countries

Accredited entity: Agence Française de Développement (AFD)

Country/ies: Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius, and Seychelles

Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Four South-West Indian Ocean countries will benefit from substantially strengthened ability to generate and disseminate climate data. | The financial sustainability of operations and maintenance will be critical in the proposal's success after the project implementation period. |
| Reaches total population of the project countries; improvements to early warning and early action, impacts-based forecasting, forecast services. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVII, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations," respectively.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

1. Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles are all highly vulnerable to natural hazards amplified by climate change factors. The four countries, all located in the South-West Indian Ocean region, face a range of extreme weather events including cyclones, tropical storms, floods, flash landslides and drought. Rising sea levels also increase coastal vulnerability. Tropical cyclones are particularly damaging as they can cause significant historical economic losses and casualties. In order to better manage and prepare for these threats to the lives and livelihoods of these four countries, the proposed project will support the development of a regional early warning system (EWS) for meteorological and services, including early warning systems (EWS), at the national and regional levels. These products will be tailored to meet the needs of the region, specific, and user-tailored, and they will increase lead time to prepare for climate-related hazards.

2. Currently, Comoros and Madagascar have low capacity to deliver weather, climate, and hydrological services. The proposed project aims to provide technical assistance and support to achieve the "Basic" level of hydromet services as defined by World Meteorological


---
**Page 132**
---

Organizer (WMO) standards. Mauritius and Seychelles are at the “Basic” level. This project proposal will raise the capacity of the respective National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHSs) of Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles to the “Essential” level, meaning that they will be able to provide climate services for their countries including seasonal climate outlooks, work with users in various sectors to identify their weather and climate data requirements, and provide advice on climate information and products.

The project is proposed by a consortium of four African countries. Each country is represented by an entity (AG) acting as the lead implementing partner. The AG serves as the focal point for the project activities within its respective country. The four AGs will work together to ensure that the project activities are implemented in a coordinated manner and that lessons learned from one country can be shared with others.

The project is aligned with the objectives of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the African Risk Capacity (ARC) Climate Services Program. Project countries will contribute USD 6.9 million in a mix of cash and in-kind contributions.

The Secretariat team has assessed the proposal’s environmental and social safeguards (ESS) and concurs with the AfD Category B rating.


### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Capacity building, institutional development, regional cooperation and public-private engagement (total cost: USD 8.5 million; GCF cost: USD 7.5 million)

Component 1 aims to strengthen regional cooperation through targeted investments in capacity building and institutional strengthening. A Regional Climate Centre Network (RCC-Network) will be established among the Indian Ocean Commission (IOC) member states, with a focus on climate services for agriculture. The establishment of an RCC through the project should enable the development and delivery of climate services at the respective levels. Institutional strengthening, including the business models/plans for the respective NMHSs, will also be supported by this component. A dedicated technical staff HydroMet regulatory material will be reviewed to explore opportunities to establish or strengthen a national regulatory framework in the sector.

Key strengths of Component 1 include the exploration of new business models with a cross-recovery plan for each RMHC (Activity 1.2). Each business model plan is key to ensure that long-term sustainability and financial viability are achieved through the provision of services, software, and yearly funding for the National Climate Outlook Forum. While the RCC-Network will be established in the region, its effectiveness and usefulness is somewhat difficult to assess.

#### Component 2: High-quality climate-related data; improved multi-hazard early warning systems (total cost: USD 10 million; GCF cost: USD 9.34 million; co-financing cost: USD 28.9 million)

Component 2 represents the project’s core hydro-meteorological infrastructure and system investments as well as the capacity-building needed to successfully operate that infrastructure. It also represents the majority of GCF funding requested. Under Component 2, observation and monitoring systems will be established in accordance with WMO standards and other relevant technical standards. A regional maintenance and calibration laboratory (WMO Regional Instrumentation Centre) will be established for NMHSs to maintain and calibrate equipment. In addition, the technical capacities and expertise of NMHS staff members will be


---
**Page 133**
---

### Improved access to specialized training

Data centres will also be established at regional and national levels and a quality management system will be put in place.

10. While the proposed project has put in place arrangements for the operation and maintenance (O&M) of the equipment and systems, this has been an issue for previous investments in meteorological and hydrological observation networks. The reliability and usefulness of these investments will hinge on the ability of the NIMS and the IOC to successfully operate and maintain the new systems as well as the capacity development and project management more broadly.

**Component 3: Enhanced accessibility and use of climate services for climate change adaptation, and improvements in implementing a people-centric MRV framework for greenhouse gas (GHG) emission reduction (total cost USD 17.2 million; GCF cost: USD 16.8 million)**

11. Through Component 3, climate products and services will be used to reduce vulnerability of communities to climate change hazards. The project will support value-added information services for the four NIMS and their national meteorological groups. NIMIs will also be able to consult with end users to improve the quality and usefulness of their climate-related information services. This will enable NIMIs to better serve decision-makers, including policy makers and communities with the tools they need to prepare for climate-related hazards and to adapt to them.

12. The project will support the development of a national climate change adaptation plan that will be updated in line with new results from the climate change projections supported under Component 3.

**Component 3 is critical to ensure that the climate information and data generated reaches the end users most vulnerable to climate change hazards. In addition, improved daily weather forecasts and warnings issued by NIMIs through national early warning advisories will add value and build resilience among key climate-vulnerable sectors.**

Operations and maintenance and contingency plans (total cost: USD 9.2 million; GCF cost: USD 3.4 million)

14. During the project implementation period, Madagascar and Seychelles will fully finance their respective O&M costs, while a combination of GCF grant funding and government-in-kind contributions will be used to cover O&M costs in the other four NIMS.

After completion of the proposed project, O&M costs will be supported by government funds. Alternatively, the project country governments will seek prospective O&M funding from the new climate change adaptation fund (CCAF) established under the Green Climate Fund (GCF)/NDFP initiative and/or cost recovery from the commercialized new climate products and services.

16. Project management (total cost: USD 2.9 million; GCF cost: USD 2.1 million)

**GCF is the primary funder of project management costs and the expected share is within GCF policy limits.**

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

This is a large, ambitious project with many expected outcomes and beneficiaries. The principal investment will be in the meteorological and hydrological observation networks, for which significant capacity development will be required to ensure that the climate services being delivered by the NIMIs of the four countries as they address the issues and complexities of a new climate reality.

One of the key issues likely to emerge from the project in the longer term will be capacity development and subsequently the project's sustainability if that capacity development

# Impact potential

## Section 3.1: Potential impact Scale: High

The South-West Indian Ocean African small island developing States of Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles are extremely vulnerable to natural hazards and climate change impacts such as cyclones, droughts, floods, heat waves, sea level rise, desertification, landslides, drought, increased temperatures and sea level rise. Scientific observations confirm that the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events have increased in recent decades (both directly and indirectly associated with tropical cyclones) and drought, based on a comparison of the periods 1961-1990 and 1991–2020, as well as increased temperatures. Climate change is projected to increase the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events by about 14.4 million people in the period 1964–2014, with a physical damage estimated at USD 13.1 billion, while the economic damage could be as high as USD 58.7 billion.

Improvements in services warning of high-impact climate and weather events are imperative in order to increase the resilience and safety of nations in the future. Without this proposed project, these countries will continue to experience extreme weather events leading into a vicious cycle of extreme poverty among the most vulnerable communities as they struggle to recover from the impacts of such events.

The urgency and benefits of building resilience through anticipatory action to improve their critical services, infrastructure and institutions are clear.

The total number of beneficiaries (combined direct and indirect) of these interventions is estimated to be 29,341,477 across the four countries, of which at least 50 per cent will be women. Beneficiaries from the direct component are estimated to be 16,875 people representing 67 per cent of the total population, with indirect beneficiaries amounting to 9,768,191 people.

# Paradigm shift potential

## Section 3.2: Potential for paradigm shift Scale: Medium-High

The proposed hydromet project is designed to strengthen the resilience of the most vulnerable communities in the four countries by improving their capacity to anticipate extreme weather events, strengthening national and regional institutional frameworks; fostering regional cooperation; and expanding climate knowledge and awareness. The regional approach is key to underpinning improvements in services and infrastructure at a scale that can be sustained over time.

At this scale, as the four target countries are vulnerable to similar climate-related hazards and socioeconomic conditions, it will be possible to achieve economies of scale through the optimization of infrastructure costs, together with a higher level of harmonization, integration and complementarity between the four countries.


---
**Page 135**
---

planning and action. Collectively, these actions will reduce the cost of both the production and delivery of climate information and early warning services.

25. The proposal also seeks to establish a regional training centre for capacity development as an important initiative that requires significant investment in order to attract and sustain appropriate numbers of staff with the necessary skills. It is envisaged that most, if not all, of these staff will be recruited or trained. The regional training centre will help to build the capacity of national meteorological services and other relevant institutions to develop climate information and engineering solutions to build resilience in the region.

26. The proposal directly addresses several GCF outcomes for adaptation, namely: (i) A35: strengthened disaster risk management systems; (ii) A40: improved disaster risk reduction planning and development; (iii) M6.0: increased generation and use of climate information in decision-making; (iv) A72: enhanced capacity to identify, assess, monitor and manage climate risks and impacts; and (v) M8.0: strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk-reduction processes.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: Medium–High

Each year, Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles collectively face up to USD 251.45 million in losses due to natural hazards. Related threats to livelihoods and economic growth in these island countries are increasing, exacerbating the baseline situation.

The proposed project will contribute significantly towards achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) for location-specific weather forecasts will enable better agricultural planning and decision-making, such as accurate crop planting dates and irrigation scheduling. The project will also facilitate risk-prevention measures, and timely early warnings will enable the protection of lives, buildings, fisheries, tourism, agricultural equipment and harvests, among other assets.

The proposed project will directly contribute to the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): (i) SDG 1: End poverty in all its forms everywhere; (ii) SDG 2: End hunger, achieve food security and improved nutrition and promote sustainable agriculture; (iii) SDG 3: Ensure healthy lives and promote well-being for all at all ages; (iv) SDG 11: Make cities and human settlements inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable; (v) SDG 12: Responsible consumption and production patterns; (vi) SDG 13: Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts; and (vii) SDG 14: Conserve and sustainably use the oceans, seas and marine resources for sustainable development.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipients Scale: High

Comoros and Madagascar are classified as least developed countries, while Mauritius is classified as a developing country and Seychelles as an upper-income economy.

The four countries must deal with tropical cyclones and heavy rainfalls, which in turn cause floods, flash floods, landslides and drought. Rising sea levels also increase coastal vulnerability to flooding and erosion. These natural hazards have been responsible for significant historical economic losses and casualties. There is clear evidence that all four countries are vulnerable to climate change, and this will have a significant impact on their gross domestic product.

All four countries are developing national adaptation plans to climate change, as well as other relevant policies. They have all adopted national development strategies. Comoros and Madagascar have submitted their national adaptation programmes of action. All countries have also developed disaster risk management frameworks and early warning systems. Other relevant domestic policies include Comoros' National Strategic Plan 2017–2020, Madagascar's National Climate Change Management Policy (2012), Mauritius' National Climate


---
**Page 136**
---

3.5 Country ownership

The project was endorsed at the political level by the IGC Council of Ministers. It is well aligned with national policies and strategies currently implemented in the IGF scale: Medium level and in four countries, in particular their nationally determined contributions, the SDGs, the Africa Adaptation Programme (AAP), and the Green Climate Fund Strategic Directions 2018–2030. However, the proposal will benefit from additional evidence in the form of a high-level commitment towards contributions to the operation and maintenance of the investments in each of the four countries.

Strong community engagement will be necessary to enable the advances made by the RMIS to be sustained over time. Some references in the funding proposal indicate that national emergency management services are involved (e.g., “The government of these four countries, as well as users and end-users of Climate Information Services (CIS) and other stakeholders such as disaster risk managers and affected local communities, have been consulted during the process of preparing this proposal”); however, there is a need to ensure that the project is reaching out to all relevant actors and communicating the messages to the most vulnerable populations. The Secretariat highly recommends that the project team should continue to build trust and strengthen relationships among the institutions involved in disaster management, both at the national and regional levels. Also, as the project focuses on resilience-building, equal attention should be placed on disaster management and humanitarian services.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The proposal indicates that the project will hold on, scale up and complement past and ongoing climate change initiatives and projects. To ensure complementary synergies with existing and planned projects, meetings have been conducted in the four countries to identify potential partners and areas of collaboration, particularly focused in the region, specifically in developing hydropower projects.

It is estimated that the benefits resulting from the intervention will be improved agricultural productivity and increased revenue generation for the IGF USD 29.8 million per year for the period 2016–2030 and up to USD 59.8 million per year for the period 2031-2035, with a corresponding increase in the number of jobs created in the agricultural sector using agricultural services. The net present value (NPV) of the project is estimated at USD 274.6 million and the internal rate of return (IRR) is 8%. The expected revenue from the commercial services of the NIMIS can be expected through the marketing of climate information and only partly through the sale of data. It is estimated that the share of the revenue generated by the public funding of NIMIS, covering from 10 per cent (Comoros and Seychelles) to almost 30 per cent (Mauritius) of the budget for those services. The grant investments could lay the groundwork for a sustainable business model in the long term.

The proposal explains that the four countries will be able to develop sustainable long-term business models through revenue generation from the sale of climate information and data, O&M through revenue/generation/cost recovery from commercial revenues, and through national budgetary support. The regional costs will be shared among the RCG Network member countries in proportion to their contribution to the project. This is expected to benefit other countries under activity 1.1.2 of the proposed project. Moreover, in the long term, the development of commercial revenues may complement and eventually replace part of


---
**Page 137**
---

current public funding of NMHS. This indicates that the hydromet project-related interventions will no longer need to rely on international funding in the long term.

iii. The executing entity (EE) will be the IOC, which will manage the project in close collaboration with AFD, which is the AE. The IOC will support four national project coordinators based within the NMHS of Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles.


## Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The proposed project is predominantly geared towards providing capacity-building and institutional strengthening interventions in the four target countries to improve climate-related services. The project will also include the installation of new meteorological and hydrological stations for monitoring, data storage, data-processing, modelling, forecasting and service-delivery systems. This will be complemented by the construction of new radar units at existing weather stations and installing facilities for weather monitoring equipment.

The AE assessed the project as potentially having environmental and social risks and impacts that are expected to be mostly mitigated through the implementation of appropriate safeguards and mitigation measures with respect to construction-related activities. The accredited entity (AE) has classified the overall risk level of this project as low, based on the information provided in the project documentation and confirms that it is within the risk level which the AE has been accredited.

The AFR has reviewed the project documentation and provided general guidance and specific guidance on the actions to be applied during project implementation to manage the identified risks.


### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

a) The project's environmental co-benefits include reduced pressure on the extraction of wood for charcoal production that occurs when farmers look for other sources of income to compensate for the loss of forest resources due to climate change. This will also reduce pressure on water extraction because the improved climate-related data will result in the establishment of more efficient irrigation systems and better land use management, leading to more efficient crop yields.

b) Social co-benefits include better health and nutrition among the countries' populations as a result of a more secure food supply due to improved agricultural productivity. This opportunity is also expected to lead to finding new employment or remuneration as a result of the enhanced early warning systems in place.


### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The project will be implemented with the installation of new meteorological equipment such as the mounting on mains of new or rehabilitated automatic weather stations, the construction of Doppler radar units at small rural meteorological stations, and the installation of computers and data-processing equipment.

Environmental and social impacts could potentially occur from construction-related installation works for the automation of existing weather stations. These include increased noise levels during the machinery and equipment, an increase in generation of solid wastes, and an increase in dust emissions due to the construction activities. The project will also have a potential impact on various occupational health and safety hazards. These issues will also be applicable to the installation of new meteorological equipment at existing weather stations.

The environmental and social impacts associated with the extension of existing buildings that house offices and weather station facilities are also expected to result in the generation of solid wastes. Some electronic-related waste may also be generated as a result of the project, including during the installation of computers and data-processing equipment. The use of solar panels as a source of electricity may also generate potentially hazardous wastes, which will need to be disposed of


---
**Page 138**
---

through the competent authorities. The adverse impact of installing a number of wave monitoring buoys and tidal gauges will be avoided by selecting appropriate sites where no impacts are expected on the marine flora and fauna.

### Gender policy

The accredited entity (AE) provides a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.


---
**Page 139**
---

per World Bank estimates, had the highest gross domestic product per capita in Africa in 2016, and consumption patterns increased substantially despite high poverty levels in 2016.

The assessment highlights that women and men are affected differently by climate-induced disasters and respond disproportionately to climate services and early warning systems (EWS). Women are more likely than men to be employed in agriculture, which is often underemployed or not, in targeted countries, irrespective of their employment status. Another dimension of time-poverty for women is the burden of unpaid care work, which limits their ability to participate fully in economic activities. Therefore, implementing gender-responsive measures involving women and girls in disaster risk reduction (DRR) and climate change adaptation (CCA) efforts will be crucial in the long run. In the targeted countries, women's engagement in entrepreneurial activities is highly restricted due to a lack of human capital in science, technology, engineering and mathematics (STEM) areas and gender norms that limit their access to education and career opportunities. Where women are employed, they occupy low-ranked positions and/or temporary jobs with poor working conditions. Women's rights to land, credit, finance, and property rights are precarious. Ownership of property is in the name of the head of household (usually men) or a male family member, which limits women's ability to access resources.

Men control most economic and productive resources and have easier access to capital, assets, and information. Women's limited access to these resources is considered crucial to achieving resilience, boosting adaptive capacities, responding to climate-induced disasters, and increasing their capacity to capitalize on adaptation opportunities.


The gender assessment identified gender-based violence (GBV) and intimate partner violence (IPV) as the most significant gender vulnerability in targeted countries. GBV persists as a shadow pandemic in the Indian Ocean region, including in the four targeted countries. The prevalence of IPV is high among women living with HIV/AIDS. Gender-based violence (GBV) is a constant theme in post-disaster contexts. In the Indian Ocean region, greater incidences of GBV and IPV usually result from negative shocks: loss of family members, livelihoods and homes; economic hardship; and lack of access to basic services. Women are more susceptible than men; women's economic dependence on her husband; and tolerance of violence. With increasing urbanization in the Indian Ocean region, particularly in the Horn of Africa sub-region, GBV is expected to increase exponentially, reiterating the importance of resilience mechanisms that address gender-based vulnerabilities. The implementation of gender-sensitive (GS) interventions in targeted countries, GBV, including its causes and impacts, are fleshed out well in the gender action plan. The plan includes a specific section on GBV and IPV, which will reduce gender-related vulnerabilities and mobilize partners to implement awareness-building campaigns and capacity development measures on GBV prevention for relevant ministries and entities at national level.


The AfD has provided a gender action plan that contains relevant outcomes, outputs, activities, and indicators. The plan includes a specific section on GBV and IPV, which will reduce gender-related vulnerabilities and mobilize partners to implement awareness-building campaigns and capacity development measures on GBV prevention for relevant ministries and entities at national level. Costs for gender experts have been included in the plan. A gender expert will be recruited at the regional and country levels to support a gender action plan across the targeted countries.


Activities identified in the gender action plan across the targeted countries are: (a) promote gender-informed capacity development through the design of training modules to support transition and development plans of the national ministry for hydronet services,

# 4.4 Fiduciary

AFD will be the Accredited Entity (AE) for the project and be responsible for overseeing the implementation, financial management, evaluation, reporting and closure of the project's activities in accordance with the terms of the Grant Agreement and the terms of the FEA Agreement and the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA).

The National Environment Agency (NEA) will be the executing entity (EE). Thus, it assumes the overall responsibility for the effective delivery of project inputs and interventions to achieve the expected results. The NEA will also be responsible for ensuring that the Grant Proceeds are utilized according to the terms of the FEA Agreement and the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA).

The IOC will be the only entity mandated to engage, supervise procurement processes, and manage the funds. IOC EE, and the regional organization mandated by the member countries to develop and implement the project in their respective countries, will be responsible for working with the Ministries of Foreign Affairs (MoFA) of each concerned country member, which are the official recipients of the funds.

The National Meteorological and Hydrological Services (NMHSs) of each beneficiary country are expected to be designated by their respective Governments as the lead implementing national entity (NE). The NE will be responsible for ensuring that the project is executed at the national level. NMHSs, as the NIP and other NSPs, will not be mandated for the use and management of funds.

The IOC and AFD will directly and exclusively manage the project's funds. Disbursements will take the form of advance payment to the EE (IOC) or direct payment by the AE (AFD). The NEs will receive payments from the EE (IOC) or the AE (AFD), respectively, with no direct management of the funds or any disbursements to the National Entities or NMHS.

The accounting and financial audits will be conducted by an independent third party. At the end of each project phase, a progress report will be submitted to AFD summarizing the implementation phase's main events, the results achieved, and the lessons learned. This report will also provide recommendations for future improvement.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP161

## Proposal name: Building regional resilience through strengthened hydrometeorological-climate information services in Indian Ocean Small Island Developing States (SIDS)

### Accredited entity: Agence Française de Développement (AFD)
### Country(s): Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius, and Seychelles
### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential - Scale: Medium to low

#### 1.1 South-West Indian Ocean (SWIO) region
The SWIO region is represented by four small island developing states (SIDS): Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius, and Seychelles.

- **Location**: Close to the southern part of Africa.
- **Climate**: Subjected to high intensity wind speed and subsequent storm surges. Also subject to heavy rainfall and seasonal flooding in some areas.
- **Exposure**: Vulnerable to climate-induced hazards that not only threaten lives but also adversely affect livelihoods, particularly in fishing communities.

#### 1.2 Climate-related events
The SWIO region has faced over 100 disastrous climatic events over the past 50 years. The adverse impacts were felt by 184 million people while the cumulative economic losses amounted to $3 billion. These events have been a nightmare for seagoing fishermen due to the absence of reliable forecasting mechanisms, even though there has been tremendous advancements in both model-based forecasting and communication technologies.

- **Fisheries**: Severe impact on fishing communities.
- **Infrastructure**: Infrastructure damage from heavy rainfall and seasonal flooding.
- **Tourism**: Disruption of tourism infrastructure.
- **Agriculture**: Significant loss due to heavy rainfall and seasonal flooding, especially in larger islands such as Madagascar and the Comoros.

#### 1.3 TCGs
TCGs are more frequently occurring in the SWIO region. The Southwest Indian basin is one of the most active areas for cyclone activity worldwide. On average, as many as 13 TCs with a threshold intensity of 63 kilometers per hour are formed each year between April and October. A total of 947 TC events have occurred in the SWIO region since 1950 to 2014.

#### 1.4 Historical records
Historical records suggest that about 84% of these TCs occur during the months from April of the following calendar year. Significant loss and damage, especially in the fisheries and agricultural sectors.


---
**Page 147**
---

4. In addition to TCs, there are non-tropical cyclone (NTC) induced rainfall, which is significant throughout the SWIO region. Such events generally cause heavy rainfall, which triggers flooding and, often, landslides. Sometimes NTC events occur with very high intensity rainfall (HRR). For example, in 2016, Mauritius experienced a record-breaking year for NTC-related flooding events at 35, while Madagascar had the highest number of NTC-related flooding events at 20 and 22, respectively, of which 9 in Mauritius were associated with TCs. In addition to these two countries, during this period, Madagascar and the Comoros experienced nine and two catastrophic events, respectively.

Unfortunately, the land-based as well as oceanic temperatures in the SWIO region are found to be increasing at a faster rate than global averages. This is partly due to the fact that its seasonal change beyond a threshold triggers TCs to occur during cyclone season and NTCs outside of this period. The increase in sea surface temperature (SST) also leads to variability in the SWIO region. Comparing the two periods, 1961-1990 and 1991–2016, an increase in precipitation during the rainy season and a decrease in precipitation during the dry season were observed for most of the countries in the SWIO region.

Based on all the available science, it is generally understood that both TC and NTC events will increase in the future. The projected changes are likely to be more severe for the four countries.

It is proven that timely and precise climate information, through effective weather warning (EWS), can significantly reduce loss and damage burdens. Many countries across the world experiencing multiple hazards (MHS), such as the countries in the SWIO region, have incorporated EWS into their disaster risk management strategies to mitigate impacts of meteorological and hydrological forecasting. Impact-based forecasting (IBF) demands a complete understanding of the relationship between weather events, climate change, its causes and subsequent effects in physical terms so that computational models can be customized through calibration and validation. The development of IBF requires a strong foundation on which hydro-meteorological hazards have the prerequisites to develop MHI-IBF EWS. While the need for stakeholder-tailored forecast messages is paramount for the SWIO, and the change in climate will likely increase this need, there are currently no such systems in place.

The meteorological and hydrological services (MHIS) in each of the four target countries have little infrastructure to support IBF EWS. The development of IBF EWS requires immediate mobilization of resources to acquire data handling and processing capacity as well as modeling capabilities. This is a major challenge for the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) and various centres of excellence on other issues including hydrology. Not only financial resources are required, but also human and institutional capacities are required so that human and institutional capacities are built and retained with the help of such centers of excellence.

The WMO does not claim to be part of the development of NMHS. However, the proposal hardly has any activity on the hydrology aspect and it based in its approach towards enhancing meteorological services. The proposed project is expected to provide support for the development of regional capacity required for IBF EWS. This will require a strong foundation on which hydro-meteorological hazards have the prerequisites to develop MHI-IBF EWS.

The four SWIO SIDS have come forward to develop projects aiming at building capacities at the local level in order to enhance their ability to provide IBF EWS. The aim is to understand the physical relationships among ocean, land and atmosphere at regional scale by forming a regional centre of excellence on their own by means of extended cooperation and coordination. However, this will not be sufficient for the four countries as they are small island states in the region, especially when the larger two of the four islands belong to Africa's least developed countries (LDCs). The proposed project is expected to provide support for the development of their collective effect. WMO is expected to provide support for the development of regional capacity.

The proposed project consists of three components, which are as follows:

5.


---
**Page 148**
---

### Component 1: Capacity-building, institutional development, regional cooperation and public-private engagement

#### (a) Component 2: Generation of high quality climate-related data, improvement of MiH-IBF and EWS, climate risk assessments and climate change projections; and

#### (b) Component 3: Development of accessible early warning services for climate change adaptation, and improvement of capabilities in implementation of a people-centred MiH-IBF-EWS for a link at national/regional level.

14. Capacity building will be the main development, a key pillar in delivering IBF and early warning services in a sustained fashion. Institutional development will be achieved by building capacity on climate-related data management, including the collection, processing data, and generate and disseminate hazard-specific forecasts following internationally accepted standards. To this effect, instructor courses will be developed and trained imparted in cooperation with WHO. As an effort to build capacity on hydrological modeling and handling. Basic level training sessions will not be helpful in expertise development. It is recommended that the NMOs should be provided with a comprehensive training course at a globally recognised centre of excellence on hydrology and hydraulics so that professional courses can be designed for their needs. The training should cover all aspects related to data handling, modelling and generation of climate bulletins/forecasting.

15. Under the same component, additional efforts for institutional development will be considered in terms of strengthening the NMOs’ capacity on climate-related data archival systems; measures to calibrate models as necessary; ground truthing (particularly for hydrological model calibration); and the establishment of a national-level centre of excellence with sufficient capacity to cater to the need for the delivery of legal mandates. Two other important functions under component 1 are to ensure that there is adequate public-private sector engagement so that the private sector can receive tailored climate information to protect their businesses, which in turn will enable the NMOs to earn revenue that will partially cover their recurrent O&M costs.

16. Following the capacity building efforts under component 1, the application of advanced modeling and forecasting techniques will be initiated with a view to ensuring that the country will be able to deliver MiH-IBF-EWS. At the national level, it is expected that climate hazard maps will be generated and vulnerability assessments will be conducted through consultations with various stakeholders. The NMOs will also develop a climate risk assessment framework, which will establish wherein the regional laboratory will be hosted in order to aid in further regional cooperation.

17. The project is expected to deliver the much-needed country-specific and region-wide capacities to deliver MiH-IBF-EWS within its five-year implementation period. Much needed is the training of NMOs on climate-related data management, including the collection, processing data management and application of information and communication technologies, as included in the proposed work plan. The training will be imparted at a globally recognised centre of excellence and include other aspects that are very much needed on the hydrological aspects, namely the acquisition of ground truthing data for model calibration, and the development of advanced hydrological models (which is even more expensive, since it requires micro-catchment level


---
**Page 149**
---

calibration and subsequent validation using observational data). Without these fundamental enabling steps, the operational use of hydrological models and the development of specialized hydrological information needed for addressing floods (feeding into possible design of protective measures) will not be possible in the short term.

Moreover, the guidance of a centre of excellence on hydrology is needed. Reliance on NMHS-built capacities alone would not be sufficient to deliver flood forecasting, flood risk assessment and other hydrological services.


15. Assuming that the project will deliver its objectives as envisaged in the theory of change, the project will have an impact on the livelihoods of about 20 million people (the total urban and rural population of the four target countries). Following the issuance of early warnings with adequate lead time, these populations would be able to take appropriate actions. Fishermen who are required to go to open sea for fishing The tourism sector in all four countries and the agriculture sector in the Comoros and Madagascar will benefit the most. In terms of its impact potential, the project is expected to have a medium-high impact (scale 5/7). It should be noted that about 50 per cent of the large number of beneficiaries will be women, although in reality an effective gender balance can only be achieved if the project is implemented in a participatory manner.


16. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) understands that the aspects related to hydrology in the proposal are given lesser priority compared to that of meteorology and that there has been no specific assessment on the impact potential of the proposed activities aimed at providing guidance on calibration and modelling of hydrology and flood hydraulics. Considering this shortcoming, TAP recommends that the NMHS should be supported by the AE (or an accredited entity (AE)) considers corrective measures, engages a centre of excellence on hydrology, and develops adequate capacity in terms of human resources (including for meteorology), and finds other sources of funding to develop adequate NMHS capability, particularly in the Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Mauritius (where no hydrological models are available). The impact potential can be higher.


1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale Medium high


23. Advanced technology-based probabilistic modelling and forecasting techniques are not new anymore; therefore, one may not find innovation in the approach of the project. However, building modelling capacities to generate precise and useful forecasts through the application of models that are calibrated at micro-catchment level is a major challenge. The cost of such capacities means taking a huge leap, given the rudimentary state of affairs involving IFR. Once the existing NMHS capacity is upgraded with the help of this project, it will be possible to use advanced climate information; the outputs will automatically find clientele in the private sector. This will lead to an increase in the demand for such services and hence the generation of model-driven hazard forecasts and their uptake within the private sector.


The project will introduce a much enhanced capacity to prepare and deliver forecasts on the basis of micro-catchment level calibration, which is not currently available anywhere. This will enable institutions to stay away from traditional methods of hazard advisories, which generally do not provide any information about the likely impacts on infrastructure and livelihoods. In technical terms, the project will make efforts to use advanced science-based and technology-driven climate information so that, instead of deterministic forecasts, reliable probabilistic forecasts can be generated. This will help in better understanding of risks and properties of affected communities in the participating SIDS. The above indicates a significant paradigm shift both approach and delivery of EWS in such of the participating countries in the SWIO region.


1 Calibration at micro-catchment level is also time consuming.


---
**Page 150**
---

19. The absence of hydro-meteorological data in the desired level of resolution and time-step is the first forecast deficiency being faced by most developing countries. The situation is even worse in SDS. In the absence of observational data, computer-driven models cannot be calibrated to produce reliable forecasts. For example, a 2015 study showed that as a doppler radar can capture and transmit on time meteorological data within a 200 km radius, such data can be used for calibration of numerical weather prediction (NWP) models to generate better forecasts. Once the physical relationships of various climatological parameters are understood and calibrated for the SWIO region, strengthening of modelling exercises and replication will become possible. The capacity to calibrate NWP models is a function of both hardware as well as software capability with human expertise being the most critical factor.
20. The project aims at knowledge sharing and learning in a number of modalities: (i) specialized training by hydro-meteorological specialists will be engaged in the proposed Regional Training Centre (RTC) for capacity building, (ii) regular interaction through online forums, and (iii) UPI will be strengthened to facilitate information and risk management experience across the region. The RTC will provide a platform for sharing of best practices regarding model calibration and downscaling techniques and development of numerical weather prediction models. It is envisaged that the RTC will also serve as a hub for interaction with NMHS experts to cross-check their modelling-related learning and subsequent sharing under the guidance of UPI. In addition, it is expected that the RTC will provide a platform where stakeholder-related information will be treated so that they reach the "last mile" information recipients in the most effective manner in each of the four countries.
21. The deployment of a new doppler radar and upper air observation network in the SWIO region will continue to generate data that are critical to calibrating the models as well as for downscaling purposes. It is envisaged that the participating countries will retain both the instrumentation and human resources, and the O&M costs are committed including the salaries of personnel to be engaged and trained; it is expected that the regional meteorological services will continue to operate these facilities beyond completion of the project intervention.
22. The project aims at contributing towards the creation of an enabling environment across all aspects of MH-IBF-EWS involving key institutions in all four countries. Strengthening institutions with data acquisition and processing equipment, trained human resources, and institutional arrangements for data sharing and dissemination purposes will enhance the enabling capacities of NMHS institutions. The guidance of WMO will contribute significantly to this objective as it is envisaged that the project will be aligned with the goals of MH-IBF-EWS. Moreover, policy harmonization and the standardization of output data and risk information dissemination procedures in the four countries through institutional reforms will greatly contribute towards an enabling environment that will pave the way to meaningful forecasting.
23. However, efforts on capacity in hydrological and hydraulic aspects of a multi-hazard forecasting and EWS appear neglected in the proposal. The involvement of potential centres of excellence in these areas is not mentioned in the proposal, which may limit progress towards the covered goal of MH-IBF-EWS.
24. In view of the above-mentioned discussion, the independent TAP finds paradigm shift potential to be medium-high.

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential

Scale: Medium to high

Since the overarching goal of the project is to build hydro-meteorological technical and institutional capacities in advance MH-IBF-EWS, the project may not have immediate direct


---
**Page 151**
---

### Economic Co-benefits

Provided that hydrological and hydrological modeling (which is not part of this project) is developed and integrated into MH-IFB, the project is expected to help end users plan early and safeguard their lives, vulnerable assets and livelihoods in the wake of every extreme event. In addition, by reducing the vulnerability of households and communities to climate risks of loss and damage could be quite substantial, as has been seen in many hazard-prone climate change scenarios. The economic co-benefits are expected to be significant; it is also expected to drastically reduce the burden on governments spending in post-hazard recovery efforts.

Moreover, governments in impacted states are also required to invest in refreshing damaged infrastructure following a disastrous hydro-meteorological extreme event, the cost of which may be substantial and could be avoided if the project were implemented well in time so that institutions can take adequate precautionary measures. All these possibilities are expected to contribute to the economic co-benefits that is likely to accrue from the project outcomes.

### In the SWD States

In the SWD States, there will be significant opportunities with the tourism sector and agriculture sector, which are critical for realization of economic co-benefits in inland states. However, the latter can only materialize if airport operations are adequately guided through the development of a robust climate information system under extreme weather conditions, especially in SIDS. The importance of advanced early warnings in tourism development is also significant as it will help to ensure that there is no disruption at regional airports and therefore regional tourism-related economic growth potential. In turn, the post-project long-term O&M of the hydro-meteorological capacity will benefit profoundly from the provided climate information system. This will not only materialize the economic co-benefits but will also foster a public private co-benefits environment.

### Social Co-benefits

One of the outcomes of the project will be increased lead time for people to take precautionary measures against sudden onset hazards, ensuring greater household resilience and reducing the number of deaths. This will result in a near-zero mortality rate down to zero, especially among fishing livelihoods, will be great a outcome. All these social co-benefits are expected to be realized through the project implementation and continues efforts with the help of global centres of excellence such as WMO.

### Agro-meteorological Information and Advisories

Agro-meteorological information and advisories can be generated, which then provide for economic co-benefits. This will result in a significant improvement in agricultural productivity, despite impinging agro-climatic hazards. There have been many instances where the application of climate information has not only resulted but to effective measures at farmers’ levels that culminated into avoidance of food insecurity and/or market price volatility. The provision of agro-meteorological information and advisories can trigger index-based insurance products for relevant private sector entities, which in turn will provide a significant economic co-benefit. The integration of climate and hydrological and hydrological inputs into adequately calibrated and customized models underpins the development of climate information that generally contribute to the latter


---
**Page 152**
---

### Environmental co-benefits:
As such, there is no direct co-benefit that may be accrued from the project. However, prevention and/or reduction of loss burden and damage might influence the resilience of ecosystems to climate change through destruction of vegetation, terrestrial and marine ecosystems and, particularly, biodiversity.

#### 1.2 Project implementation:
The TAP has identified a number of critical issues following issuance of warnings/advisories. However, given the weak integration of hydrological aspects of modeling, including calibration and validation of hydrological models, such potential benefits may not be fully realized in the short term. The TAP also notes that the short time frame for project implementation could also put limits on fully integrating critical hydrological aspects into the project design.

The independent TAP also found that the importance of hydrological aspects with respect to meteorological and climatic conditions has been given less attention than it deserves, which may slow down the likelihood of achieving MH-IBF on hydrological modeling.

#### Gender-related co-benefits:
Issuance of early warnings can potentially save lives during extreme weather events. It has been found in many countries that morbidity rates due to cyclonic storm surge and flash floods are higher among women than men, as women are more likely to be affected by the strengthening of EWS in a hazard-prone climate hot spot is always gender-friendly.

Since the project will integrate social response towards early warnings and advisories, the process of integrating gender into the project design will also include the collection of information and advisories. The policy and regulatory measures, as proposed, might re-examine the existing policies and regulations to ensure that they are in line with the proposed gender-responsive measures.

The gender assessment and action plan (area 6 of the funding proposal package) elaborate on such possibilities of gender mainstreaming throughout project implementation.

#### Impact on sustainable development:
The independent TAP found that the sustainable development potential of the proposed project is medium to high.

### Needs of the recipient
Scale: High

In recent years, a number of cyclones have hit the SWID countries, causing devastating due to high intensity winds, storm surge and wide-spread flash floods. While physical immobile infrastructure have been damaged in all four countries, agricultural crops have also been affected as agriculture is the main pillar of economy on the islands of the Comoros Islands. The future outlook of these islands in the wake of climate change is also very bleak, since higher level of climate variability and extreme weather events are expected to increase in frequency and the frequency of occurrence of high intensity cyclonic events in the SWID region. According to globally available data, the number of cyclones that have affected the region has increased.

The four target countries are not homogeneous in terms of economic and economic structure of their inhabitants. While the key drivers of advanced economies such as Mauritius and Seychelles are tourism and financial services, the main drivers for Comoros and Madagascar are agriculture and fishing, particularly in cyclonic storm surge and flash floods, leaving unfavorable conditions for tourism to thrive. Moreover, the countries have a high dependency on climate-sensitive sectors which are also heavily affected by climate change, limiting income opportunities from tourism and offshore recreational activities.

The situations is relatively larger islands such as the Comoros and Madagascar are even worse in terms of their economic structure and dependence on agriculture and fishing. Since these two LDCs have high poverty (on the basis of per capita income) and the poor are dependent on agricultural productions and sea-bound fisheries, climate-induced extreme events

## 1.5 Country ownership

### Scale: High

* The African Union (AU), in collaboration with WMO, made a call in 2010 to provide support at scale to NMHSs in the wider Indian Ocean region.
* This project can be considered as a response to the call of AU.
* The Indian Ocean Commission (IOC) has been instrumental in developing and implementing the regional climate services strategy for the Regional Council.
* The outcome of the project fits into the regional vision titled AID India Agenda 2050.
* At a global scale, this project contributes to the implementation of the Sendai Framework, with an aim to reduce disaster risks in the participating countries.

This project therefore resonates well with regional and global policies and strategies on relevant issues.

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## Scale: Medium to low

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The hydro-meteorological project proposes a budget of USD 71 million of which about USD 54 million (i.e. 74.23% of the total) is required from GCF as grant. The AE and the European Bank for Reconstruction and Development (EBRD) are expected to co-finance. The total co-financing amount is USD 16.75, which gives a ratio of 1:0.387.

In absence of the requested GCF financing, the available financing will enable the project to progress only in terms of capacity building and dissemination of climate information products in small pockets in the four target countries. However, such disjointed efforts are unlikely to be effective as they do not address the specific and complex climate needs of overall capacity-building, including modern equipment, computational requirements and automated systems. The private sector is also not likely to invest in this area without the participation and active support of GCF; hence the financial requirements cannot be met by the SWD countries. This is why the public sector drives action needing finance from GCF.

The objective is to generate accurate climate forecasting with adequate lead time, which in turn will enable the private sector to make informed decisions. However, historically, the private sector has not been seen as the lead actor in such an endeavor anywhere in the world. As a result, without the active support of GCF and the public sector, without participation and active support of GCF, the financial requirements cannot be met by the SWD countries. This is why the public sector drives action needing finance from GCF.

The Comoros (Comoros and Seychelles) have healthy per capita income. However, the other two countries (the Comoros and Madagascar) not only have much lower per capita income, but also a significant proportion of poor people who are climate vulnerable. Since all four countries belong to SIDS, the proposal

# Introduction

This Gender Assessment and Action Plan has been prepared to assist the design of a Green Climate Fund (GCF) funding proposal by Agence Francaise de Developpement (AFD) and Indian Ocean Commission (IOC) for Strengthening Regional Resilience through Strengthened Meteorological, Hydrological and Climate Services (SRS-Met/HydroMet).

This project aims to bolster climate resilience and adaptive capacities among communities by diversifying national hydro-meteorological services, strengthening regional cooperation and climate knowledge sharing, and with an overall focus on improving and scaling up information services (IS) delivery and early warning systems (EWS) in the IOC countries. These countries have been identified as being particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased water exposure to coastal erosion, natural hazards, sea-level rise and saline intrusion, temperature variation, etc., to the extent that they are characterized by high levels of poverty and deprivation from the beginning of life, low income and assets, small domestic markets and heavy dependence on a few external markets, high volatility of economic growth, fragile natural environments, and socio-economic systems as well as weak governance.

With three components: 1: capacity building; institutional development; regional cooperation; public-private engagement; 2: high-quality climate-related data, improved multi-hazard impact-based forecasts and EWS; and climate risk assessments; and 3: enhanced use of climate services for decision-making in the four IOC countries. By focusing on gender equality and women's empowerment (GEWE) and IBF-EWS for DRM, the proposed project can reduce towards greater gender time poverty in the four countries by:

- Maintaining gender-responsive approaches for the design of CS products, hydro-meteorological systems, and EWS.
- Promoting gender balance through the technical and maintenance capacity-building activities as well as institutional development targeted for hydro-meteorological networks, equipment, etc., and
- Establishing gender-aware policy frameworks to inform collaboration between key sectors and national/regional hydro-meteorological services; and
- Pioneering gender mainstreaming analysis and praxis regarding hydro-meteorological systems, EWS and CS in the Indian Ocean region.

A kaleidoscope of overlapping cultural, economic, social and political roles form gender relations in the Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles. Given the expanse of the region and the unique markers of Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles societies, these roles have multiple facets, broadly reflecting the:

| UN OHRLS - Office of the High Representative for the Least Developed Countries, Landlocked Developing Countries, and Small Island Developing States (UN-OHRLS) |  |
| --- | --- |
| Global Gender Assessment Report: Strengthening Climate Resilience in the Indian Ocean Region |  |
| Accessed 16 July 2019. Available at https://sustainabledevelopment.un.org/content/documents/1785SDG on NUMBERS: SUMMIT OHRLS, 2013.pdf |  |


---
**Page 165**
---

Geographical and geopolitical specificity of the region: Comoros* and Madagascar** are identified as Least Developed Countries or LDCs – UN-designated countries for aid, preferential market access, and special technical assistance due to low income (calculated by a three-year average of Gross National Income or GNI), human assets, and economic vulnerability. The Indian Ocean Islands (IOIs) are also recognized as Small Island Developing States or SIDS, which is a distinct group of developing island countries facing specific social, economic and environmental vulnerabilities. SIDS were recognized at the 1992 Earth Summit in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.

Traditional and faith-based norms within intrinsically societal roles of the four countries: the IOC countries are culturally diverse, with each country having its own unique system in the region reflect distinctly among cultures, communities and customs. Seychelles, among the four island nations, has been distinctively a matrilineal society with greater access to women’s rights and gender equality compared to other IOIs. On the other hand, Comoros, Madagascar and Mauritius have historically been traditionally patriarchal societies with varying amounts of decision-making according to women in the public and private sectors.

Gender gaps in economic roles and political representation: the data collated from the four countries, explored in detail – see Section 3, reveal the different baselines in the IOC countries.

At the outset, the project recognizes that the lack of a gender-sensitive approach to address the above baseline (see Section 3 for details), particularly stemming from:

- Supposed “common sense” notions that climate services are gender-neutral;
- Side-lining of gender needs or ethnic vulnerabilities in adaptation design, resilience capacity-building and mitigation services; and
- Lack or scant allocation of financial means, gender budgets and dedicated resources towards the inclusion of women in decision-making processes.

Without gender analysis and mainstreaming in IOC Hydromet, the benefits of increased support access to climate services, in tandem with awareness-raising and capacity-building, may accrue to better-off individuals or more dominant groups who are able to capitalize on new opportunities.

2. UNDER-5: UN List Country Profile: Comoros. Brief Report. Accessed 1 August 2019. Available at: https://www.un.org/esa/socdev/unpfii/documents/Comoros_Profile_Comoros.pdf


Climate change is a complex phenomenon, and its effects are increasingly being documented in scientific circles. Yet, there is increasing evidence that adopting social science methods, and building resilience and adaptation practice with the involvement of women and other vulnerable groups can lead to more sustainable and socioeconomic co-benefits. Particularly, this could better prepare communities to avoid resource strain and help the region adapt to climate change. This paper explores how gender equality and women’s rights into resilience – meaning capacities of climate risks and social conflict in Disaster Journal Article.


Prior to 2015, there was a lack of evidence that women were able to influence decision-making processes depending on women to assume a central role in their coping strategies, which may not be the practical reality for many vulnerable communities. However, recent studies have shown that women are more likely than men to be involved in disaster risk reduction and management (see: Meadmore, E., Carson, T., Morton, J., & Martin, A. (2018). Uncertain predictions, invisible impacts and the need to mainstream gender in climate change adaptation. In Gender and Development Journal Article.

A 'gender lens', thus, is both necessary and relevant for the project to maximise its outcomes, particularly creating hydro-meteorological EWS and CS capacities for observation of weather phenomena and climate change impacts. This will enable communities to take preventive action against natural and climate-induced hazards, disasters and weather variations that cannot be avoided. This gender-responsive approach is also crucial for establishing institutional structures and broad-based policies that can ensure the sustainability of medium- and long-term climate change adaptation action as well as regional cooperation.

# METHODOLOGY

## Appraisal of Gender Mainstreaming Priorities of GCF and AFD

The analytical pre-emptives of this Gender Assessment and Action Plan are informed by both GEF's and AFD's respective gender policies.

### The GCF adopted a revised version of its 2014 Gender Policy and Action Plan on June 8, 2016 in Kigali, Rwanda. This policy document is the first to explicitly define gender mainstreaming as an approach of gender mainstreaming beyond the presence of "women's issues", and the identification of synergies with the in-house Indigenous People (IP) Policy as well as the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW). Overall, the Policy and Action Plan reinforce the responsiveness of GCF to the multiple, heterogeneous needs of women and girls in developing countries and account for the links between gender issues and climate change – a perspective that has been mainstreamed in the development of the funding proposal for IODC Hydromet.

The AFD recognizes the important contributions made by increased gender equality to development outcomes. The AFD's strategy is based on promotion of gender equality as a priority through the adoption of the French Gender and Development Strategy 2013 – 2017. In 2014, AFD defined its gender policy framework with three main objectives: promote gender equality priorities; prevent gender inequalities in the operations financed by AFD; promote gender as one of the objectives of AFD's operations; and support progress on issues related to gender.

Further, the AFD defines itself as a "100% Social Link" agency in its Strategy 2018-2022: "All AFD Group actions will aim to bolster social link – or at least not weaken social link – by reducing inequalities and increasing access to essential goods and services such as education, health care, water, housing, jobs, credit, and information. This is particularly true in terms of economic participation by facilitating access to property ownership, employment, and credit, and by connecting isolated areas to the rest of the world. This multi-dimensional development will benefit all actors involved: women, men, children, youth, and older people, and SDGs are included in it. It also includes all AFD actions that contribute to SDG 5 (zero hunger), SDG 3 (good health and well-being), SDG 8 (decent work and economic growth), and SDG 10 (reduced inequalities). It also includes the SDGs that address urban and rural challenges: SDG 6 (water and sanitation), SDG 7 (clean and affordable energy), and SDG 11 (sustainable cities and communities)."


---
**Page 168**
---

The IOC, as of January 2020, does not have a regional gender policy in place. However, a GCF accreditation and readiness proposal put together for the IOC by Seychelles stipulates the identification and development of regional needs for equal representation of women in environmental projects.

## Concurrent Triangulation and In-Country Missions

This Gender Assessment is informed by a concurrent triangulation research design: firstly, semi-structured interviews; with community focus groups, key informants within governments and technical agencies; secondly, desk review of secondary data gathered from the review of existing literature (see bibliography under chapter 6). Participants and stakeholders were identified using a combination of purposive and snowball sampling. The assessment aimed to identify gender-related issues related to vulnerability, exposure and access on climate change, early warning systems, and gender baseline and inequality issues to prioritize and act upon information and data received.

The concurrent triangulation research design was informed by the IOC’s Hydrothem gender assessment; given: the flexibility to convert secondary quantitative and qualitative findings into narrative data for the comprehension of the studied phenomenon, and the accuracy it can provide to understand the context. The in-country mission, which took place from 24 June to 16 July 2019, was also formative in nature and far too mandate, necessitating the prompt methodology to establish an inventory of information related to gendered aspects of EWS, CS and hydrometeorological services. A summary of outputs identified is the results framework.

The methodology, thus, informed the in-country mission for gender data and information scoping, described in chronological order below:

**Madagascar (24 – 16 June 2019)**

The week-long in-country mission in Madagascar, based out of Antananarivo, primarily focused on identifying and interviewing key informants (particularly government stakeholders such as representatives from the Ministry of Environment, Climate Change, Water Resources, Social Protection and the Advancement of Women) and experts (international organizations and NGOs) to understand the country context the content gender baseline, especially gauging the availability of gender data and information. The mission also included a focus on climate-vulnerable sectors such as, participation of women in agriculture.

Multi-stakeholder meetings with different government wings (e.g., Climate Change Unit) and other organizations (e.g., Ministry of Environment, Social Protection and the Advancement of Women), civil society actors, and experts from international organizations (e.g., IUCN, World Bank, FAO, etc.) were held. These meetings provided an overview of gendered labour force participation and potential beneficiaries in these sectors. A report on women and environment for the National Adaptation Planning process, finalized in February 2019, was also obtained to inform this analysis.

Additionally, meetings were held with and reports were received from:

- GCF (2017). Readiness Documents with the Indian Ocean Commission (IOC) of the Republic of Seychelles. Project Document. Available at: <https://www.greenclimate.fund/documents/20164/MAP019Readiness_project_document_Seychelles__India_Ocean_Commission__GCF_20170518.pdf>. DOI: 8731-MAP019Rd

# Comoros (21 – 05 June 2019)

The week-long in-country mission in the Comoros archipelago was structured to cover three all islands: Anjouan, Grande Comore, and Mohéli. Gender-related data sourcing and information collection were conducted through a series of workshops held across these islands.

* **Engagement with key government stakeholders such as Ministry of Civil Aviation and Meteorology, Ministry of Civil Protection, Ministry of Fisheries, etc.**
* **Consultation with NGOs and volunteer associations such as International Red Cross and Red Crescent Society (ICRCS).**

Key-informant interviews with UN organizations active in the islands – Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) and United Nations Development Programme (UNDP).

Additionally, site visits (organized through the project focal point in the meteorological department) in Grande Comore were conducted to gather information on climate change adaptation strategies. Complementary studies team. Focus group discussions in coastal communities of north Ngazidja were held with women’s representatives and village elders. These were conducted in a semi-structured interview format, using both open-ended and closed questions to gather information regarding:

* **Current and past livelihoods in the community, particularly fishing practices (dominated by men), yang ylang plucking (dominated by women).**
* **Gender access to government and NGO aid, resources and capital in the selected sites;**
* **Information sharing characteristics of different groups within the communities (particularly, public spaces such as mosques and women’s limited access to these);**
* **Importantly, the ex-ante (the EW) was received mostly by men, and women were uniformed/underinformed about the cyclone), during it was revealed that due lack of information dissemination, many women did not receive the warning. The ex-post (the EW) was received in last-minute evacuations) and ex post coping mechanisms taken by men and women, in response to the cyclone, such as evacuation, sheltering, and sharing of resources – many families were depending on familial and neighbour networks as their houses were yet to be repaired, losses of agricultural land were yet to be reversed.**

# Seychelles (22 – 26 July 2019)

The week-long in-country mission in Seychelles was based out of Mahé Island, the largest island and governance centre of the archipelago. At the outset, a multi-stakeholder meeting organised through the Seychelles National Meteorological Authority (SNMA) with key representatives from Ministry of Tourism, Civil Aviation, Ports and Marine, Ministry of Fisheries and Agriculture, Seychelles Fisheries Authority, etc., this helped identify the key stakeholders and existing CS, EWS and early warning systems in place. The mission also facilitated discussions on potential users and beneficiaries. Field visits, organised through the meteorological services to Praslin and La Digue islands, facilitated rapid assessments of coastal erosion sites and engagement with private enterprises (potential users of climate services) that have experienced sea intrusion-related losses.

A gender-focused meeting was organized with the Gender Secretariat housed within Seychelles' Ministry of Social Affairs, Community Development and Sport. Key informant interviews with the Principal Secretary and other operatives of the Secretariat highlighted gender- and youth-related issues that inform the climate- and disaster-readiness of the country: primarily market segmentation in the labour force, drug epidemic and gender-based violence (GBV) among certain communities.

Mauritius (29 July – 02 August 2019)

The week-long co-location meeting in Mauritius was centered around Port Louis, the capital city and administrative hub. It focused on presenting national stakeholders with an overview of climate-related losses, and institutional collaborations for climate and hydro-meteorological services in the administration of disaster risk management (DRM). The participants included representatives from the following ministries: including the Mauritius Meteorological Services, Ministry of Agro Industry and Food Security, and Ministry of Ocean Economy, Marine Resources, Fisheries and Shipping. Following the identification of potential gender-sensitive issues, a meeting was held with key informants (Gender Coordinators) in the Ministry of Gender Equality, Child Development and Family Welfare was organized.

This meeting was crucial for mapping the current gender baseline in Mauritius, and understanding gender-related context with cross-cutting factors such as socioeconomic performance and caste determinants. Further, it shed light on gender roles in Mauritian societies and how these relate to climate change preparedness, and potential adaptation through hydro-meteorological services.

The information gathered from the meeting was combined with secondary data collection and policy appraisal through the existing knowledge products of the Ministry of Gender Equality, Child Development and Family Welfare. The focus areas included: environment; and climate change-related funding proposals on solar energy, land management, etc.
