# GENDER MAINSTREAMING, SECTORAL ISSUES & HYDRO-METEOROLOGICAL INFORMATION SERVICES

The World Economic Forum (WEF)'s report The Global Risks Report 2020 finds that the top five among the primary ten risk factors facing the world population are environmental in nature: (in order of risk) extreme weather, climate action failure, natural disasters, biodiversity loss, human-made environmental damage. These risks are exacerbated by other “systemic drivers” such as precarious economic status and lack of political access (including decision-making bodies); performance gaps; and infrastructure gaps (which are often worsened by extreme weather events and natural disasters). Limited access to productive resources, networks, skills (which work as buffers during ex-xtremer disaster situations), among others.

Employing a gender mainstreaming perspective will highlight these complex social development phenomena and their interconnections in the context of the 17 Sustainable Development Goals. Once identified, this will form the gender and social development context within which the IOD Hydromet has to be operationalized. In doing so, the project’s change narrative can also amend some of the existing gender inequalities and unequal access, which often result in specific and entrenched vulnerabilities in the IOD countries.

Vulnerabilities, thus, can be defined as a set of general characteristics that impair the ability of a social group or individual to adapt to climate change. Vulnerabilities are not only those arising from climate changes, natural hazards, and disasters. This perspective is an important point of departure for the IOD Hydromet project, as vulnerabilities (in the background of risks and threat multiplier) can be seen in terms of gender roles, social norms, economic activities, as well as as impediments for coping capacities against climate risks and impacts.

Further, these vulnerabilities, whether compounded or individual, can reverse the progress achieved in securing household economic stability (with spill-over effects on food, energy and water security), and thus undermine the achievement of the SDGs. These are the same goals that were made on developmental goals and social change, particularly in the previous contexts of both LDCs and SDCs. On the other hand, gender equality is a key element of the Gender and Development (GAD) approach (as mentioned above), all who will recognize the role that different social groups can play, and not simply style them as victims or ‘prisoners’. With an inclusive project design, they can be seen as active agents in their own development. This calls for gender-responsive planning strategies, which can either be strengthened and mainstreamed as well as used a basis for further capacity development.

This section presents a synthesis of gender, environment and climate change issues, as these pertains to the IOD Hydromet project. It provides a broad background information the gender-responsive perspective of IOD Hydromet. Then, it identifies sectoral recommendations in relation to gender-responsive planning for CS that have been identified for the project’s design and operationalisation.

The GAD approach seeks to correct systems and mechanisms that produce gender inequality by focusing not only on women’s empowerment, but also on men’s empowerment. This is because men are equally important in resolving gender inequality, and plays importance on the empowerment of women, who are placed in a socially and economically weaker position than men.

3.1 The Gender-Climate Change-EWS/CS Nexus

In December 2019, the 25th Conference of the Parties (COP) was held in Madrid, Spain and organized by the Government of Chile, bringing together all the signatories to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The importance of gender mainstreaming and gender-responsive climate change adaptation were highlighted by the Women and Gender Constituency—particularly the inadequacy of transformative action [and policy gaps] on gender equality in relation to climate change.

COP 25 and other high-level policy engagements, between states and international organizations, reveal that gender and climate change issues have been recognized in adaptation and mitigation policy frameworks. Yet, despite having common concept and perspective, the operationalization of gender-responsive measures in adaptation and mitigation remains scarce.

Further, resource mobilization and commitments by national governments, in tandem with local actors, do not exhibit the penetration of these issues—especially at grassroots level where gender-responsive climate action is crucial in determining overall resilience and adaptive capacities of communities.

As elaborated in the funding proposal, this Gender Annex concurs that climate impacts in the IOC countries will be on demographic groups that are [primarily] reliant on natural resource-based livelihoods and enterprises, as well as those most vulnerable to these sectors. In each IOC country, resources capable of being managed changed climate phenomena, vulnerabilities and hazards, as well as increasing and intensifying weather events. It has been generally observed that female-headed households are more likely to be affected by climate risks and greater burdens from these impacts, particularly compounded by incidence of poverty, unequal participation in decision-making processes, disparities in labour markets, health risks, etc.

The following table identifies the main risks and challenges relevant for gender mainstreaming in a multi-country EWS and CS adaptation project like IOC Hydromet.

# MAJOR BURDEN SHIFTS AND ENHANCEMENTS IN THE GENDERED IMPACTS OF CLIMATE CHANGE

## DIFFERENTIATED IMPACTS OF COMBAT EFFECTS AND NATURAL DISASTERS

The most prominent hurdle for the project is the gender-specific differences in consumption patterns and incidence of poverty, access to and control of resources and power, time use patterns and economic activities, etc. that result from the lack of overlap to create gendered vulnerability to climate change in each IOD country.

This high burden of adverse impacts of climate change, especially with the lack of adaptation capacity, is further exacerbated by the fact that women have less time, but also have intergenerational effects and undo progress made towards gender equality in the island nations (particularly in SIDS and LDC contexts).

## GENDERED ACCESS TO ADAPTATION SOLUTIONS IMPLEMENTED BY THE PROJECT

Field visits and engagement with government officials revealed that adaptation solutions are often not gender-sensitive. For example, women face gendered impediments in percolation (where they are rarely mobilized). In some cases, women are excluded from the decision-making process (to be announced), for example mosques in Comoros. They may have limited access to information and resources, and their voices are often not heard.

In many regions, discrimination of information are limited to channels primarily accessed by men. Additionally, time poverty (explored below) experienced by women limits their ability to participate in decision-making processes on adaptation solutions, particularly in post-disaster and other vulnerable contexts.

## SHELTERING LOCAL ADAPTATION MEASURES AND INFORMATION CHANNELS

Communities have long histories and praxis of engagement with and knowledge of local climate change impacts. Traditional methods, information channels, and disaster strategies (albeit discrete and reactive) have been developed over time to address these challenges. However, through the incorporation of these primarily reactive techniques into broader, more employee WLS, CS and territorial services with an emphasis on cross-sectoral collaboration.

## GENDER-BASED VIOLENCE (GBV)

Despite mounting evidence for interlinkages between GBV and environmental factors, academic enquiry and practical integration of this perspective remains limited. The linkages are often not recognized or addressed in policy, usually exacerbated during post-crisis situations of climate impacts and losses, and extreme weather events.

In the Pacific Islands region (GBV rates are high before normal), and often go unaddressed (with, for instance, Madagascar taking a recent nation-wide survey). In the least-developed countries, men also experience high levels of violence in their homes (often linked to alcoholism and drug use) alongside a persistent drug addiction epidemic, creating further barriers in the implementation of GBV interventions.

## GENDERED LIMITATIONS IN DECISION-MAKING INSTITUTIONS (RULES & REGULATIONS & HIERARCHY & REPRESENTATION)

Decision-making institutions, power-sharing mechanisms and overall representation tend to be skewed towards men in all four IOD countries, barring Madagascar. Women are often excluded from the decision-making process daily, in those countries that pertain to domestic food security, water provision and disaster risk reduction. Without the integrated integration of these complex and gendered phenomena, it will be difficult to address the intergenerational impacts of vulnerable groups at the local level. In such a context, interventions are often top-down, which can lead to limited success and lack of ownership at the community level.


---
**Page 175**
---

3.2 Mainstreaming Gender within Climate Information Services in the IOC Countries

The countries identified by the project are uniquely vulnerable to aggregate shocks from both economic and environmental stresses due to their small size, isolation, and other geographic, socioeconomic, and political factors. Despite this vulnerability, there is a lack of understanding about how gender influences the well-being of populations and the effectiveness of prevailing risk management mechanisms. This is particularly true in the context of climate change, where the impacts are expected to be exacerbated by rising temperatures, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, and sea-level rise.

Given this lack of understanding regarding the effects of climate-related risks, natural hazards and disasters on gender equality, it must be empirically quantified but can be hypothesized to produce unevenly distributed impacts within a population. The impacts and exposure compound with current incidence of impoverishment, gender inequality and lack of access to social safety nets, which are exacerbated by climate change. Gender is not only an outcome of vulnerability determined by the needs, opportunities and perils facing men and women. In this context, gender-responsive CS will play a unique and positive role in increasing adaptability and resilience of communities and countries.

At the same time, mainstreaming gender in CS through the project will involve the consideration of the following factors in activities:

- Division of labour (activity/place);
- Visibility of labour (formal/informal sector);
- Poverty levels (incidence/intensity);
- Human development factors, including:
  - nutrition pattern (current levels of health/morbidity and mortality rates),
  - literacy levels (access to information/ability to act on information and knowledge),
  - recourse to legal protection (inheritance/land rights),
  - Access to and supply of information;
  - Access to critical resources (emergency aid/loans/insurance);
- Influence over decision-making processes (authority/power over domestic, community and local management).
- Household responsibilities and time-use (domestic and unpaid care as well as reproductive work, fuel and water provisioning).
- Incidence of physical, social and psychological violence in the household (especially how gender-based violence and intimate partner violence trends are affected in ex post disaster contexts)


---
**Page 176**
---

3.3 Tailoring Products Towards End Users in Climate-Vulnerable Sectors

The IOC countries have faced consequences of disasters (including climate-induced disasters), extreme weather events and the pitfalls of limited meteorological capacities in recent years. For example:

- Comoros is estimated to have lost between 60–80% of staple crops, water facilities and housing due to Cyclone Kenneth.18
- The Climate Vulnerability Index (CVI)24 calculated by Germanwatch et al. identifies Madagascar as the 7th most vulnerable country in recent years and Comoros as the 9th.

The Southern Indian Ocean Risk Assessment (SIO RA), conducted by the Global Facility for Disaster Risk Reduction and Recovery (GFDRR) and World Bank, finds that Mauritius experiences a combined USD 110 million in combined direct losses from earthquakes, floods and tropical cyclones.25

- In 2013, a Damage, Loss, and Needs Assessment report26 by the Government of Seychelles (supported by the European Union or EU and the World Bank) found that Cyclone Felling caused an estimated loss of USD 84 million, equivalent to 0.7% of the country’s GDP.
- The above examples highlight the importance of context-specific climate information products27 which highlights how the extent of exposure to the impacts and losses of climate events is determined mainly by three factors:
    - Ex ante and ex post coping mechanisms;
    - The resources and information available to a community; and
    - The frequency and intensity of shocks/impacts that determines income shortages and asset loss, as well as the resilience of communities built up over time.

The reports reiterate the importance of hydroclimatic data28 as well as a robust EWS in the Indian Ocean Island countries, and how these cannot simply be limited to technical capacities and tools, particularly because expected and unavoidable variations in climatic patterns, climate-induced disasters, and unpredictable climate risk changes can create different exposure patterns among communities.

To address these differential exposure patterns, CS products, such as impact forecasting (analysing and disseminating implications of the forecast and specifics of the forecast information) and nowcasting (short-range weather forecasting), particularly from ground-based remote sensing


---
**Page 177**
---

3.4 Utilising Community Information Channels and Local Knowledge

A complementary approach to creating user-friendly and gender-responsive climate service products is sourcing local knowledge, artisanal practices and traditional know-how on how to inform EWS and CS products for women and men in different contexts. This can be done through participatory mapping exercises, informed by participant observation and a consultative approach, will be key to: understand how local information and warning systems work; identify community engagement strategies that are appropriate for the context; and ensure gender-sensitive product design.

Further, the consideration of gender access to technologies in different countries is an important factor in the delivery and efficiency of CS products. The importance of understanding differences between women’s and men’s needs, preferences, and decision-making roles and processes, as well as variations in usage patterns and desired-value-added services have been highlighted in recent research, particularly for climate-smart agriculture.10 The data reveals that often women are less likely to use mobile phones or other communication technologies than their male counterparts, a significant gender gap that can determine the effectiveness and perception of CS products and EWS. Additionally, gender-disaggregated data is often available for the national level - concealing large variations within countries. This makes it difficult to design appropriate interventions.

Addressing these factors will assist in empowering women in their roles as key actors in climate-vulnerable sectors, encourage active participation and involve them as information producers, and draw out their knowledge and experience into climate-smart products and services.

A CGIAR report finds that inclusion of traditional methods and gender-responsive design in community engagement could substitute for where modern innovations are lacking in gender awareness or accessibility, as these utilise strong social networks that have historical continuity within the communities.11 However, it is important to note that the relationship between technological changes is not linear; hence these traditional methods can be easily hybridised with modern technology such as mobiles (including radio), television and short message service (SMS) through telecommunication companies. The challenge lies in ensuring that these messages are disseminated effectively. The GAP report shows that, in some contexts it is important to build on the existing socio-political networks that form the basis of information sharing, EWS, DRM. Since


---
**Page 178**
---

project is expected to introduce newer technologies, to encourage uptake, it will be important to study how these new technologies can be used in a way that increases access to electricity, mobile phones, and radio usage in Madagascar. At the project onset and during implementation period, the reassessment of the baseline will be tasked for each country with the support of the last mile partners. The baseline assessment should focus on: what kind of infrastructure and services can be built on; how these same different gender interest groups, and then to figure out how improved hydromet and early warning services can build on this to improve outcomes. The consultation process also drew attention that sometimes traditional remedies can exacerbate extens, as it was observed in Comoros.

Feedback loops, by which community members, specifically women in vulnerable groups such as subsistence agriculture-dependent smallholders, can reflect upon which methods work best for their own communities. This is because the project will be working with a wide range of local networks and means of communication, climate information providers, such as field workers from met services, may also be able to extend the reach of their climate information services and help more vulnerable communities to adapt to environmental and climate changes.

### 3.5 Creating Opportunities to Overcome Gendered Barriers

Section 3 below presents detailed statistics related to poverty and hardship, labour and education, and health and social indicators that demonstrate the gendered realities of the Indian Ocean islands under discussion. The following section also highlights the importance of understanding intra-household dynamics between men and women pertaining to culture- and faith-based socioeconomic practices. Regionally, it is common for women to participate in the bulk of domestic work, including food preparation, childcare, and other household chores. This is not unusual due to high levels of male out-migration for seasonal work. Thus, it can be surmised that gendered time poverty in the project countries is high.

Standard definitions of poverty often describe an individual as "time poor" if he/she works simultaneously monetary poor, or facing the risk of monetary poverty if he/she were to reduce his/her working hours below a given time poverty line. Thus, time poverty results from the combination of two dimensions:

* Firstly, the individual does not have enough time for rest and leisure once all working hours (whether spent in the labour market or doing household chores such as cooking and fetching water and wood) are accounted for; and
* Secondly, the individual cannot reduce his/her working time without either: increasing the level of poverty in which he/she lives (if the household is already poor); or leading his/her household to fall into monetary poverty due to the loss in income or consumption associated with the reduction in working time (if the household is not originally poor).


---
**Page 179**
---

An additional implication of time poverty is the lack of feasibility/opportunity to change the current time usage pattern within the household to explore other economic activities or, as in the case of this project, to explore autonomous or technical adaptation options against climate-induced weather variation, natural disasters and hazards such tropical cyclones, and other climate risks.

In addition to facing the time poverty-related risks, GBV persists as a serious epidemic in the Indian Ocean Region (IOR) countries. The International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies (IFRC), upon conducting in-depth research[1] on its designated regional zones and national societies, found that GBV is a constant theme in post-disaster contexts. Given the negative shock of climate change, it is expected to increase the vulnerability of women and girls to GBV, leading to greater incidence of GBV and IPV, among other effects on gender relations. With the increasing frequency and intensity of climate-related hazards and risks, GBV can be expected to witness an upward trend in the IOR region, with a significant impact on women and girls living in vulnerable communities through CS in the beneficiary countries. Further, recent research[2] undertaken by the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) and IFAD has highlighted the existence of strong connections between environmental climate-related stressors and increased GBV, which are both detrimental to the well-being of survivors as well as impediments for inclusive climate change adaptation and resilience.

[1] IFRC = International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies (2015). Unseen. Unheard: GBV in Disasters. Report

[2] Research connecting environmental and climate-related stressors to GBV is nascent. AGENT – Advancing Gender in the Environment (IUCN-IFAD-IJCN partnership) is at the forefront of collating, analysing and identifying these stressors in different contexts.

# SOCIOECONOMIC AND GENDER BASELINE IN THE IOC COUNTRIES

The previous section underscored the important nexus issues of gender, environment, climate risks, and threat multipliers, which are relevant towards the successful implementation of the project.

Gender inequalities are a complex phenomenon that is influenced by multiple factors. These include poverty, education, health, and social indicators. AFTV in house gender profiles (1) for Comoros, Madagascar and Mauritius (not available for Seychelles), similarly, establish the gender gaps by analysis the health, access to water and sanitation, education and professional training, etc.

## 4.1 National aggregate statistics and data

The national-level data points (divided into three categories: poverty and hardship, labour and education, and health and social indicators) presented below are not comparable across countries, given their different economic and social circumstances.

These broadly speak to issues(2) identified by the GCF as crucial in the project design stage (such as: poverty levels; participation of men/women in formal/formal labour markets; gender disparities in education; access to water and sanitation; maternal health; morbidity and mortality statistics, etc.) to map and mainstream existing vulnerabilities.

A World Bank Poverty Assessment report** finds that although Comoros has slow GDP and GNI growth, available household surveys and national accounts show important increases in consumption patterns. This highlights the importance of the informal sector and heavy reliance on remittances which are often informal channels, as well as the lack of data and enumeration capacities.

The pervasiveness of the informal economy and reliance on income earned in cash crops has influenced household decision-making. This, in turn, plays an important role in determining access to adaptation and migration opportunities. The proportion of female-headed households (FHHS) has reached 27.7% (2014), showing a 6.2 percentage point increase from 2004 figures.

Furthermore, the share of FHHS in the total number of HHs has increased by almost 5 percentage points, from 18.6% in 2004 to 23.9% in 2014. Overall, however, there are fewer FHHS in the archipelago as compared to male-headed households (MHH).

Thus, it can be posited that FHHS have not witnessed a proportionate increase in income and consumption with the advances made in household expenditure and consumption patterns (with the caveat that the data quality is low and the nature of the HH is transient, factors which are beyond the scope of this assessment).

# Madagascar

The poverty incidence indicators presented above show Madagascar’s position as compared to the other project countries. In a recent report(1), the World Bank identified Madagascar as the poorest country in the sub-Saharan Africa (where internationally comparable data was available), with pre-crisis GDP per capita of US$300, and an average annual growth rate of 2% over the period 1985-2007. The country’s agricultural sector is characterized by low productivity – by 2012, Madagascar’s GDP per employed worker had fallen to the lowest in the world except for the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC)(2). The high level of multidimensional poverty in Madagascar is also reflected in the fact that 58% of the population lives below the poverty line(3), and 40% live on less than US$1.90 per day, a figure exceeded by the majority of the Malagasy population. Given that an additional “12% of the population is vulnerable to multidimensional poverty”, it can be posited that even minor external shocks can destabilize their livelihoods and push them into poverty(4). The World Bank(5) also notes that 30% are vulnerable to multidimensional poverty(6) and enter/exit poverty levels (where households are already facing multidimensional deprivation).

A common finding in the World Bank’s report is that about a fifth of all households nationally are headed by women – primarily because they are widowed, divorced, or separated. While on-primary inspection, FHHs do not appear, as a group, to be significantly worse off than their male counterparts(7). However, this is not always the case and varies according to location and age, as well as importantly, the marital status of the household head(8). Conditional on location only, FHHs in both rural and urban areas experience lower consumption than MHHs(9).

The poverty incidence indicators presented above show Mauritius’ position as compared to the other project countries. In contrast to the LDC status of Comoros and Madagascar, the World Bank classifies Mauritius as an upper-middle income country with low levels of poverty and inequality, having diversified its economy and accomplishing unprecedented structural transformation(10). However in 2015, the country was downgraded from a high-income to a lower-middle-income status from western markets(11), the economy has witnessed initial setbacks. Concurrently, inequality has risen, lowering the standards of the poorer sections of the populace – which has complex gendered aspects that inform this.

The main contributor to rising inequality is the variance among earnings (males) situated in different social classes – showing the imperatives of redistribution and pro-poor investment in the country. Mauritius’ female poverty rate is 20% compared with a male poverty rate of 14%, as shown in the table below, and women continue to be disadvantaged in access to the labour market, which can

# Comoros

Indicators compiled for the labour and education sectors show the interrelated nature of two factors that help these determine both individual and national well-being. Comoros, among the four countries targeted by this project, displays the lowest labour force participation (both male and female) – testifying to its status as an underdeveloped economy, dependence on remittances and high unemployment rates. The lack of a diversified economy and the low level of education can be seen in the literacy rate for adults aged 15+ (40%), which is below the UNICEF (2013) threshold of 50%. This, coupled with the lack of diversification in the economy and natural-resource-based livelihoods for a majority of the population can create impediments to adaptation opportunities, access to climate services and export-oriented industries.

An ADB Country Gender Assessment[7] finds that the labour market in Comoros is characterised by the feminisation of precarious employment, informal activities, and unemployment – the agricultural sector employs 50% of women but only 21% of men. In the fisheries sector, women employ only 30% of women, particularly in low-level jobs. There are more self-employed women (56.1%) than men (47.9%), in tandem with 47.6% of the unemployed in Comoros being women.[8] To cite a few examples: in the agricultural sector, women cultivate rice and other crops; in the fishing communities, pêcheur à pieds (fishing by foot) is a common practice. Men undertake boat-capture fishing, while women extract small fish, oysters and crabs by walking along the sea coast. Based on these activities, women earn less than men. In Comoros, both self-employment and self-employment in women occupy a further 19.5% of jobs.[8] Female employment in Comoros remains

[8] Ibid.

low with only 13.7% of women in the wage-earning group, 69.2% of which are in 'unsheltered employment'.**


### Madagascar

The International Labour Organization – ILO in its flagship Key Indicators of the Labour Market – KILM study[1] finds a positive correlation between education and productive employment in different countries. In Madagascar, ILO's findings are conclusively replicated: although labour force participation rates for women are low (25%), this is because more than 70% of the population are dependent on agriculture. In fact, literacy rates collated for the country are misleading: over half of the population[2] and consequently, the workforce lack qualified training and knowledge to access productive employment opportunities in the formal sector. Women who depend on agricultural livelihoods-dependent economy and households are vulnerable to external shocks and climate risks, retarding the importance of robust CS, IWS and hydromet products and services to strengthen their resilience.

Further, the World Bank identifies a three-tier gender gap in Madagascar.[3] For example, median earnings for women in non-agricultural employment reach only two-thirds of earnings tabulated for men. Women are also less likely than men to be employed in formal sector jobs with similar characteristics as men are paid comparatively less.[4] Additionally, the gender wage gap is highest in the informal private non-agricultural sector, and this appears to be largely linked to firm size and ownership. The World Bank[5] highlights that women's access to productive employment in at least three areas: access to education, access to higher paying jobs and same pay in those jobs, and access to financial capital and other institutions that foster firm growth.


### Mauritius

Indicators compiled on labour and education disclose the linkages between the two sectors, and how these help determine both individual and national well-being. In Mauritius, the general dynamic of the labour market is evident: female labour force participation is quite low compared to peer economies in the region. This is due to women's greater involvement in unpaid work within domestic and household labour (care, reproductive and related work) in Mauritian society.

A study[6] conducted on gender, education and labour market however problematises the 'common sense' hypothesis suggesting better educational access implies higher labour market opportunities. In fact, while girls are more likely to be enrolled at all levels of education (primary, secondary and tertiary), their access to job opportunities is reduced.[7] The female unemployment rate is higher than that of males, while women who manage to enter the labour market are

MATERNAL MORTALITY RATIO & UNDER-FIVE MORTALITY RATE: The World Health Organization (WHO) identifies MMR as the ratio of maternal deaths and pregnancy-related causes to a hundred thousand births. The MMR represents the risk associated with each pregnancy, i.e., the obstetric risk. The under-five mortality rate is defined by WHO as the number of children dying before their fifth birthday per 1000 live births. In most countries, the majority of deaths below the age of 5 occur in infancy. In the mortality between high-income and low-income countries remain large, despite the fact that the gap has narrowed over time. The under-five mortality rate is around three times higher in low-income countries (32 deaths per 1000 live births) than in high-income countries (10 deaths per 1000 live births). ### Comoros

Selected health and social indicators presented above, particularly the staggering maternal mortality ratio (MMR) and under-five mortality rates, display how the health system in Comoros is plagued by limited access to medical centers, insufficient resources with frequent drug shortages, skilled staff shortages, inadequate staff training opportunities, and weaknesses in health data collection and reporting. The country has a high prevalence of malaria, which is endemic throughout the archipelago and has seen a downward trend marked at 353 deaths in the latest survey (2015). Although GBR rates are lower and atypical in the region, Comoros has a high rate of child marriage (32%, according to the last Demographic Health Survey).

The WHO suggests that climate change effects on health outcomes and indicators can be based on approximation; however even conservative predictions have tended towards highlighting the negative impacts. The country is particularly vulnerable due to its low elevation, increasing frequency (and intensity) of climate-related disasters. Limited healthcare system can only be expected to falter (if not fail entirely), underpinning the necessities of strengthened climate services in the country.

**Moderator:** The data gathered on Madagascar for health and social indicators reveal a similar predicament to that of Comoros. High MMR and under-five mortality rates, along with the country's struggles with pneumonic plague in 2017 and 2018, indicate a lack of investment in the public health system.

## Mauritius

In contrast to Comoros and Madagascar, Mauritius' performance on health and social indicators is better than expected given the importance of consistent and relevant government investment in the public health sector and social institutions. According to time-series data reported by the Government of Mauritius, however, there are recent spikes in MMVR and under-five mortality (especially in 2017). Gender-based violence (GBV) is a significant issue for women in Mauritius, with 35% reporting experience of GBV in their lifetime, including partner and non-partner violence, while a similar proportion of men admit to perpetuating violence against women in their lifetime. Violence occurs primarily in intimate relationships and is more prevalent among younger women. Mauritius is considered a safe country, in addition to minimal rates of reporting: only 0.3% of women in Mauritius have reported domestic violence.16 It can be speculated from the Gender Links baseline study that actual levels of GBV are likely higher than those reported.

Linkages between health indicators, climate change resilience and GBV are less tenous than previously assumed and have been emphasised more and more through targeted socio-antidiscrimination policies. For the overall success of climate change adaptation efforts, these considerations need to be taken into mainstreaming activities, to ensure better targeting of beneficiaries and delivery of project goods.

## Seychelles

In a similar vein to Mauritius, Seychelles has better indicators of health outcomes (such as life expectancy and child mortality rates) than many other small island states, primarily because of free access to healthcare provided by the state. Since the 2000s, the country has recorded zero maternal mortality; deaths among skilled birth staff have been below 100%, and the proportion of women receiving prenatal care has consistently been over 99%. The Island nation achieved


---
**Page 191**
---

most of the health-related MDGs, and is on track to operationalise policy and budgetary allocations towards SDG 3 (Good Health and Well-Being).

Yet, challenges crop up in the broader health landscape in the Seychelles. UNODC reports that Seychelles is among the countries and areas with the highest rates of injected drug use (more than 3 times the global average).16 Interviews held with the Gender Secretariat in Victoria confirmed these findings and provided the general nature of substance abuse in the country, demand for illegal heroin and other substances originate primarily from unemployed youth and men in the country.

LGBT people in Seychelles have uniquely nurtured both women and men in their lives. In partnership with Gender Links, a local NGO, UNODC has been working to address gender-based violence (41%), emotional violence (29%), followed by physical violence (12%), economic violence (9%), and sexual violence (6%). The study also found that LGBT people are more likely than the general population to experience different types and complex incidence of GBV in the islands. Although the linkage between GBV and environment is a nascent topic of research, the evidence of violence as a threat multiplier of risks to and from climate change is clear.

The WHO suggests17 that SDG 13 (Litter Action) and SDG 5 (Good Health and Well-Being) are closely related, with results gained in one sector positively impacting results in the other. Targeted health-related investment for continued results, thus, is important for the archipelago – with better SDG 5 outcomes leading to better SDG 13 outcomes in disaster-related health outcomes.

## NOTE:

Calculated by the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD)'s Development Centre, the SIGI is a composite index that measures social discrimination against women in five key areas: family law, inheritance laws, and practices across 83 countries. Discriminatory social institutions intersect across all aspects of "gender" and "women's rights," thus affecting women's access to education, empowerment opportunities and undermining their agency and decision-making authority over their lives.

As underlying drivers of gender inequalities, discriminatory social institutions perpetuate gender gaps in development areas such as education, health and wealth – and hinder progress on the right-based social transformation that benefits both women and men.

The SIGI's variables quantify discriminatory social institutions such as unequal inheritance rights, age-related labor laws, violence against women, and access to land. The index is based on a combination of country classifications, unique database and its innovative simulator. The SIGI provides a strong evidence base for effectively policy action in the area of gender equality and women's empowerment, allowing policy makers to scope out reform options and assess likely effects on gender equality in social institutions.

## COMMODITIES

### CEDEAO

Commited as the CEDEAO on 15 October 1994, but has not yet ratified. It "bound nations also ratified the Maputo Protocol (The Protocol to the African Charter on Human and People's Rights) on the rights of women in Africa" as one of its main objectives.

### Gender Policies

The National Commission for Women and Children Affairs was established by the 1992 Liberian Constitution to improve the legal participation of women. The Constitution was revised in 2018 to include provisions that promote gender equality, including a provision on the right of women to vote with provision for government intervention, where necessary, for the actualization of this right.

### Governmental Mechanisms

The National Commission on Solidarity, Civil Protection and Promotion of Gender is the primary governmental body responsible for promoting gender equality in Liberia. The 2018 report produced by the Commission shows that baseline understanding of gender inequalities

**Page 193**
---

# Madagascar

CEDAW:  
Madagascar ratified the CEDAW on 17 March 2008. Madagascar has also signed the Maputo Protocol.

### Gender Policies  

The following policy tools towards gender mainstreaming exist in the country: Gender and Elections Act, National Action Plan for Combating GBV, and a National Action Plan for Combating GBV.

Madagascar is identified as having "high discrimination" on the SIGI index, which takes stock of discriminatory laws and practices that affect women's access to political participation, education, financial resources, and control (over assets).

Women are protected under the law from discrimination based on gender. *Yet*, regarding basic citizenship rights, the rights of women and men are unequal. Women have the right to vote in elections but are not allowed to run for public office or be elected as a representative. Additionally, women cannot inherit property if they are married.

Regarding civil law, women and men have equal rights in 2007; however, the law was amended to raise the legal age of marriage for women from 18 years old to 21 years old. The law also provides both civil and criminal penalties if a woman is raped by an official or her husband.

### Gender Mainstreaming Mechanisms  

Madagascar has been proactive in incorporating gender mainstreaming through its state machinery, and it currently developing a draft National Gender Policy Framework with EU support.


# Mauritius

CEDAW:  
Mauritius acceded to the CEDAW on 09 July 1984, but is yet to ratify it. In June 2017, Mauritius signed the Maputo Protocol.

### Gender Policies  

Under the CEDAW, all citizens are equal before the law and are protected from discrimination based on gender. Women have the right to access justice, including provisions for legal aid and interpretation services; for example, in cases where a woman is unable to understand or speak English. The law also provides women with rights in matters relating to personal status (marriage, divorce, death and inheritance).

Mauritius has been proactive in incorporating gender mainstreaming through its state machinery.

### Gender Mainstreaming Mechanisms  

The Ministry of Social Protection and Promotion of Women is the national gender machinery for the country and has extensive experience working with different funds and UNICEF agencies.

# Composite Indices – HDI, GDI, GGII, CGGI, and MPI

Implementing gender responsive adaptation action requires situating the project’s results framework on a thorough and context-driven baseline. A collection exercise of scores and rankings from composite indices, especially due to the lack of decentralized, nationally available data, has been included in this assessment to reflect the overall gender performances of the four countries. These indices are designed to provide both quantitative (e.g., numerical) and qualitative (conclusive) measures of current levels of development, gender equality, poverty, and labour force participation.

As Boaventura’s research[17] shows, composite indices present both challenges and advantages. For example, numerous fallacies have been identified in the methodologies employed in composite indexing. These indices are mainly quantitative, and present empirical and aggregate measures of

complex development phenomena, making values apparently objective, at the cost of subjective nuances. Yet, these remain invaluable as useful complements to income-based development indicators, understanding relative degrees of development, simplifying complex development constructs as well as providing access to non-technical audiences.

This baseline, at the outset, uses scores of three different UNDP composite indices: Human Development Index (HDI), Gender Inequality Index (GII) and Gender Development Index (GDI) in points of departure. Secondly, the baseline collates scores from the World Economic Forum (WEF)'s Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI) and Oxford Poverty and Human Development Initiative (OPHI's) Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI), among others, to provide a comprehensive picture.

### HDI
**HUMAN DEVELOPMENT INDEX**
UNDP (2018) – out of 189 countries/areas

| Country | Comoros | Madagascar | Mauritius | Seychelles |
|---------|----------|------------|-----------|-------------|
| Score   | 0.63     | 0.74       | 0.85      | 0.82        |

**NOTE:** This index measures and combines three basic dimensions of human development (longevity and healthy life, knowledge and decent standard of living) and provides an overall socioeconomic landscape of a country.

In line with Boyce's argument, however, the HDI should be treated as indicative, not conclusive. It provides an overview of the relative level of development in a country, but it does not provide information on gender equality. The GII has shown the need to supplement the HDI with other indicators associated with economic and social cohesion, sound development strategies, and sustainability in growth models.

### GII
**GENDER INEQUALITY INDEX**
UNDP (2018) – out of 189 countries/areas

| Country | Comoros | Madagascar | Mauritius | Seychelles |
|---------|----------|------------|-----------|-------------|
| Score   | 163      | 157        | 141       | 120         |

**NOTE:** This index, showing inequalities in achievement between men and women in three aspects (productive health, reproductive health, and power), is a composite index. It combines the scores of four sub-indices: political empowerment, financial access, speaking to the gender opportunities of men and women in the countries. It provides a primary summary measure of gender inequality in a country.

The GII displays useful features towards the gender status quo hypotheses, which could then be derived in the context of this project. The GII is also used as an indicator for the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) under Goal 5: Gender equality. Under this goal, it includes the inclusion of relative performance indicators for women vis-à-vis men, along with absolute women's empowerment.

GII scores for Comoros, Mauritius and Seychelles are unavailable as of August 2019.

|---------|----------|------------|-----------|-------------|
| Score   | 0.56     | 0.73       | 0.82      | 0.84        |

| 1 | Iceland | 97.5 |
| 2 | Norway | 96.4 |

| 4 | Sweden | 95.8 |

**NOTE:** The GGI (lighter color shows the lowest of female to male ratios). GGI represents what is missing, hence, for a fuller understanding UNDP's GGI group-based categories have been used here grouped by UNDP to show the absolute deviation from the ideal situation. These are: 1) Gender parity; 2) Gender equality; and 3) Gender inequality. The GGI measures the Human Development Index (HDI) and GDP (or HDIIP), and shows the real gender gap in human development achievements, Gender Disparity and Inequality. To create more robust indicators of poverty and underdevelopment*, more robust data needs and indicators are required to create aggregate indices that can encompass all dimensions of poverty and inequality, particularly with the categorization of 'women'. **

GGI grouping for Beninches is unavailable as of August 2018.

| Rank | Country | Score |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Iceland | 97.5 |

| 3 | Finland | 95.9 |
| 4 | Sweden | 95.8 |
| 5 | Germany | 95.0 |

**NOTE:** The GGGI (2018) benchmarks 144 countries on their progress towards gender parity on four thematic dimensions: economic participation and opportunity, education attainment, health life expectancy, and political empowerment. The index takes stock of national gender gaps on economic, political, education- and health-based criteria, and provides country rankings based on these dimensions across regions and income groups.

GGGI rankings for Comoros and San Marino are unavailable as of August 2018.


### MPI
**MULTIDIMENSIONAL POVERTY INDEX (WORLD) - and 136 countries**

| Rank | Country | Score |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Iceland | 97.5 |
| 2 | Norway | 96.4 |
| 3 | Finland | 95.9 |
| 4 | Sweden | 95.8 |
| 5 | Germany | 95.0 |

**NOTE:** Calculated by the Oxford Poverty and Human Development Institute (OPHI) and UNDP, the Global Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) measures the proportion of people living in multidimensional poverty across all dimensions of human development. It complements traditional income-based poverty measures by capturing severe deprivation regarding different indicators such as health, education, and standard of living. The MPI is a composite index that reflects both the intensity and the extent (or intensity) of their poverty. The MPI can support the effective allocation of resources by making it possible to identify the most deprived clusters of people, addressing ODA strategically, and monitoring specific impacts of policy intervention.

# CONCLUSION: REGIONAL POTENTIAL & WAY FORWARD

This Gender Assessment has identified and expanded upon both explicit and implicit gender and socio-economic issues that could be addressed through project components. The findings from the Assessment also inform the results framework (Section 5), which we specify this proposal's desired results, corresponding actions, indicators, and expected contributions of partners and budget allocations, through the results framework (Section 4 of the funding proposal or FP).

Given mounting evidence that dynamic, long-term planning for the uncertainties posed by climate change is essential to achieving gender equality in climate services, gender mainstreaming in climate services. Gender equality has also gained priority in the GCF, AFD, and other agencies' portfolios as a part of implementing a holistic social development mandate with stakeholders at all levels. This project will contribute to this growing international conversation on gender mainstreaming in climate change adaptation efforts.

If implemented effectively, this project has the potential to become a good practice gender-mainstreaming guide for future interventions in the Comoros, Madagascar, Mauritius and Seychelles (regionally), the Indian Ocean region (regionally) as well as among other SIDS and LDCs, globally.

# GENDER ACTION PLAN

This section provides the Gender Action Plan. The first table shows the main outputs and responsibilities expected from the Gender Consultant to be hired during the project cycle. The Gender Consultant will also participate in activity 1.4.3, which will conduct a baseline study of access to climate services, institutional capacities of NRMIs, and needs of these different institutions. This will be conducted with the aim to build upon the formative research on gender-responsive climate change adaptation and improved community resilience in vulnerable communities.

The second table presents a summary of gender targets for each component of the Gender Action Plan. The first column lists the components, followed by the gender targets under each component:

- **Component 1: Capacity Building, Institutional Development, Regional Cooperation and Public Private Engagement**
    - Include gender-responsive policy impacts in regional and national frameworks developed for climate services
    - Ensure capacity building through the design of training modules to assist transition and development plans of NRMIs, DRR institutions and relevant sectoral ministries
    - Increase gender parity in new staff and trainees hired for EWS, CS, and hydromet services

- **Component 2: High Quality Climate-related Data, Improved Bioclimatic Indices, Seasonal Forecasts and EWS, and Early Warning Systems**
    - Ensure gender engagement training of observers and forecasters, particularly those involved in impact forecasting and early warning systems
    - Research and include community and social vulnerabilities, particularly gender-differentiated capacities of resilience, in the hazard maps and climate vulnerability maps developed for each country

- **Component 3: Enhanced Use of Climate Services for Climate Change Adaptation and Improved Capacities in Developing People-centered M&E EWS for DRR**
    - Conduct needs assessments to ensure gender responsiveness of daily weather bulletins, seasonal forecasts and agricultural advisories
    - Ensure gender responsiveness in EWS dissemination, particularly with regard to the limited access women have in public (e.g., radio stations), or via information channels (radars in Mediterranean)
    - Incorporate time use analysis to ensure effective and meaningful participation of women and other vulnerable groups in any trainings and/or information campaigns for EWS and CS

# COMPONENT 2: CAPACITY BUILDING, INSTITUTIONAL DEVELOPMENT, REGIONAL COOPERATION AND PUBLIC PRIVATE ENGAGEMENT

## GENDER IMPACT STATEMENT: Integrated institutions reinforced capacity and greater regional cooperation among the four IOC countries and their ability with gender mainstreaming will ensure that a climate change adaptation strategy is developed and implemented in a manner that is inclusive of women and girls.

### COMPONENT OUTCOME 1: A standardised monitoring framework for greater gender equality, particularly for climate change adaptation through CS, EWS, hapkorem, for the IOC countries.

1.3: A STANDARDISED MONITORING FRAMEWORK DEVELOPED AND IMPLEMENTED ACROSS THE IOC COUNTRIES

# COMPONENT 2: HIGH QUALITY CLIMATE-RELATED DATA, IMPROVED MULTI-HAZARD IMPACT-BASED FORECASTS AND FLOOD AND CLIMATE RISK ASSESSMENTS

## GENDER IMPACT STATEMENT:
Improving climate data and risk assessments, leading to more precise climate change projections, will provide a "gender lens" to risk exposure in the IOC countries. GENERATION OF HIGH QUALITY CLIMATE DATA: The generation of high quality climate data will be achieved through the use of innovative technologies such as satellite imagery, remote sensing, and other advanced techniques which will allow for better representation of gender-relevant variables within the climate data. Further, altering the skewed gender ratio not only improves representation rates, but also creates a broader field of engaging and empowering women through climate change adaptation practices.

### 2.1. ENHANCED HYDRO-METEOROLOGICAL OBSERVATION AND MONITORING BY MAKING USE OF INNOVATIVE TECHNOLOGIES FOR COST-EFFECTIVENESS AND GENDER-RELEVANCE

### 2.3. REGIONAL FACILITIES FOR MAINTENANCE AND TRAINING ESTABLISHED WITH FOCUS ON INCREASING CAPACITY IN TARGETED GROUPS

### 2.5. SCIENCE BASED MULTIMODAL WEATHER AND CLIMATE RISK INFORMATION GENERATED AND DELIVERED TO TARGET AUDIENCES IN THE SELECTED SECTORS

# Capacity Analysis (Exposure to Hazards)

## 2.3.4 Develop climate vulnerability maps

### Purpose:
Ensure that mainstream climate vulnerability maps are developed in a responsive and gender-sensitive manner through stakeholder consultations.

### Output:
- Climate vulnerability maps for each country, reflecting different areas within the country.
- The GC will assist with sub-national consultation towards the development of climate vulnerability maps.

### Consultation and Meeting Minutes
- Stakeholder and community engagement report by the GC.

## 2.3.5 Ensure climate risk analysis (exposure to hazard) and mainstream climate vulnerabilities are considered in existing datasets

### Purpose:
Ensure that climate risks and vulnerabilities are considered when developing risk maps, as per activity 2.3.3.

### Output:
- Climate risk analysis output will deliver risk maps that are able to quantify and map climate vulnerabilities and hazards on existing datasets.
- This will therefore be a key output point for activities 2.3.1 & 2.3.4.

## 2.3.6 Establish downscaling of global, regional, and seasonal climate products at the national level, with focus on agricultural production and agrometeorological products

### Purpose:
Ensure that agrometeorological products are developed in a responsive and gender-sensitive manner through stakeholder consultations.

### Output:
- Agrometeorological products for each country.
- The GC will assist using research undertaken under activity 2.4.1, and for the development of activities 2.3.2 to 2.3.5.

### Consultation and Meeting Minutes
- Stakeholder and community engagement report by the GC.

# Component 3: Enhanced Use of Climate Services for Climate Change Adaptation and Improved Capabilities in Implementing a People-Centered MH-IBF-EWS for DRR

## 2.3.7 Build the capacity to use climate models for specific purposes.

This activity will focus on increasing knowledge and skills in monitoring and forecasting food insecurity (food security model). Therefore, a capacity building approach has been identified for this specific activity.
Ensure gender-disaggregated participation of MH-IBF-EWS staff, as noted above. Rapid analysis (baseline) disaggregates the data by sex, age, and other relevant factors. Each MHMS will be supported to develop an equitable framework with analysis tools and training opportunities.

| min: 6 | Develop a gender-disaggregated framework |

**Outputs:**  
1. Production, Dissemination and Uptake of CP-CL including MH-IBF-EWS, Improved Gender Responsiveness

COMPONENT 3: ENHANCED USE OF CLIMATE SERVICES FOR CLIMATE CHANGE ADAPTATION AND IMPROVED CAPABILITIES IN IMPLEMENTING A PEOPLE-CENTERED MH-IBF-EWS FOR DRR

IMPACT STATEMENT: Strengthened capacity of national and regional stakeholders to use climate services for climate change adaptation, improved capabilities in implementing a people-centered MH-IBF-EWS for DRR, and reduced hazards in the region.

OUTCOME STATEMENT: Strengthened MHMS staff capacity, knowledge brokers, as well as regional platforms, to deliver gender responsive CP-CL EWS.

OUTPUTS:
1. Production, Dissemination and Uptake of CP-CL including MH-IBF-EWS, Improved Gender Responsiveness

## GENDER TRAINING MODULE

This deliverable will feed into the activities with training components. The GC will deliver a generic gender module for EWS and CS covering the importance of gender analysis for EWS, CS and hydromet. Particularly, information on user-based disaster risk management (DRM) participation in agriculture) and gender-disaster management (GDM) will be highlighted. The GC has to train this training module for each country.
